# BOOK ONE

->**The First Book**<-

thus also by "running," all things are brought into being through Him. *D.* Regarding the etymology of the name <note>original: "Etymologia nominis." Referring to the Greek *Theos* derived from *theein* (to run) or *theorein* (to see).</note>, I am sufficiently and plausibly persuaded; but I do not quite see how He moves Himself, who is everywhere, without whom nothing can exist, and outside of whom nothing extends; for He is the place and the boundary of all things. *M.* I did not say that God is moved outside of Himself, but by Himself, in Himself, and toward Himself. For no other motion ought to be believed in Him except the desire of His own will, by which He wills all things to be made; just as His "standing" is not as if He stands still after motion, but is understood as the unchangeable purpose of that same will of His; by which He ordains that all things remain in the unchangeable stability of His reasons. For in Him, "standing" or "motion" are not properly used, as these two appear to be opposites of one another. True reason forbids opposites to be thought or understood in Him, especially since "standing" is properly the end of motion. God does not begin to be moved in order to arrive at a certain state of standing; therefore these names, like many similar ones taken from the creature, are referred to the Creator through a certain divine metaphor, and not irrationally; since He is the cause of all things that are in state and motion. For from Him they begin to "run" so that they may exist, since He is the beginning of all, and through Him they are carried toward Him by a natural motion, so that in Him they may stand unchangeably and eternally, since He is the end and the rest of all; for beyond Him they desire nothing. In Him they find the beginning and the end of their motion. For God is said to be "running," not because He runs outside of Himself—He who always stands unchangeably in Himself because He fills all things—but because He makes all things run from non-existence into existence. *D.* Return to the proposition; for these things seem to have been said not irrationally. *M.* I ask you to remind me which proposition you seek. For when we attempt to speak on incidental questions, we very often forget the principal questions. *D.* Was it not this? We proposed to investigate, according to our strength, by what reasoning it is said by those who dispute concerning the divine nature, that the same nature "creates and is created." For that it creates all things, no one of sound mind doubts; but in what way it is said to be "created," it seemed to us, should not be passed over transitorily.

*M.* Quite right. But as I think, from those things which have been said before, no small entrance has been opened toward solving this question. For it has been agreed between us that by the motion of the divine nature nothing else is to be understood except the purpose of the divine will to establish those things which are to be made. Therefore, the divine nature is said to be "made" in all things, which is nothing else but the divine will; for in it, to be and to will are not different, but to will and to be are one and the same in the establishing of all things that have seemed fit to be made. For example, if someone should say: the motion of the divine will is directed to this, that those things which are may exist; He therefore creates all things which He brings forth from nothing, so that they may pass from non-being into being; but He is "created" because nothing exists essentially except itself; for it is the essence of all things. For just as there is no natural good except Him, but everything that is said to be good is good by participation in the one supreme Good: so everything that exists does not exist in itself, but exists by participation in the truly existing nature. Not only, therefore, as was considered in those things said before, is the divine nature said to be "made" while the Word of God is born in a wonderful and ineffable way in those who are reformed by faith, hope, charity, and the other virtues; as the Apostle says speaking of Christ, <note>1 Corinthians 1:30</note> *who was made for us wisdom from God, and justification, and redemption:* but also because it appears in all things that are—it which is invisible in itself—it is not incongruously said to be "made." For even our intellect, before it comes into thought and memory, is not irrationally said "not to be"; for it is invisible through itself, and known to no one except God and ourselves. But when it comes into thoughts and receives a form from certain phantasms <note>mental images or impressions</note>, it is not undeservedly said to be "made"; for it is made in the memory, receiving certain forms of things, or of voices, or of colors, and of other sensible things, it which was formless before it came into the memory. Then it receives as it were a second formation, when it is formed by certain signs of forms or voices—I mean letters, which are signs of voices, and figures which are signs of forms, <term>matheseos</term> <note>original: "matheseos" (Greek: learning/knowledge, often specifically mathematical or astrological diagrams)</note> or other sensible indications through which it can be suggested to the senses of those perceiving. By this likeness, although it be far removed from the divine nature, I think it can be persuaded how that nature, while it creates all things and is known to be created by none, is created in a wonderful way in all things which are from it; so that just as the intelligence of the mind, or its purpose or counsel, or in whatever way that most intimate and primary motion of ours can be called, when it has come into thought as we said, and has received certain forms of phantasms, and then has proceeded into signs of voices or indications of sensible motions, is not incongruously said to be "made" (for it is "made" when formed in phantasms, it which through itself lacks all sensible form): so the divine essence

<meta>catchword: which</meta>

<summary>This page explores the paradox of God as both the unmoving Creator and a "running" force that is "created" within all things. It uses the analogy of human thought becoming "made" when it takes the form of words or images to explain how the invisible divine essence becomes manifest in the created world.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between a Master and a Disciple regarding the first division of nature: that which creates and is not created. It follows the discussion of how the Divine Nature "creates itself" by manifesting in all things.</meta>

# ON THE DIVISION OF NATURE

<meta>catchword: tror</meta>

that which, subsisting through itself, surpasses all understanding, is rightly said to be created in those things which are made by it, through it, in it, and towards it. This is so that it may be known by those who seek it with upright zeal, whether in those things through the intellect (if they are only intelligible <note>original: "intelligibilia" — things perceived by the mind rather than the senses</note>) or through the sense (if they are sensible things). Concerning these matters, I believe enough has been said.

14. **Disciple.** Clearly enough, unless I am mistaken; but it is still necessary that you explain why the divine nature is said to be only creating and not created, when, as was persuaded by the previous arguments, it both creates and is created; for these things seem to contradict one another.
**Master.** You are keeping a careful watch, for I see this too is worthy of inquiry.
**Master.** Pay attention, therefore, to what follows, and apply your mind's insight to this brief response.
**Disciple.** Lead the way; I will follow intently.
**Master.** You do not doubt that the divine nature is the founder of the Universe.
**Disciple.** Proceed to the rest, for to hesitate on this would be a sin.
**Master.** Similarly, you perceive by faith and intellect that it is created by nothing.
**Disciple.** Nothing is more certain than that.
**Master.** Therefore, you do not waver when you hear that it is created—not by another, but by itself.
**Disciple.** I do not waver.
**Master.** What then? Is it not always creating, whether it creates itself or creates the essences created by it? For when it is said to "create itself," nothing else is rightly understood but the founding of the natures of things. For that very creation—that is, the manifestation in something—is truly the <term>substitution</term> <note>original: "substitutio" — here meaning the bringing into substantial existence or "standing under" of all things</note> of all existing things.
**Disciple.** Thus far, what has been said seems probable: but I would like to hear what Theology <note>The author uses "Theology" here to refer specifically to the Holy Scriptures or the teachings of the Church Fathers.</note> teaches about this ineffable and incomprehensible creator and causal nature of all; that is, whether it is, what it is, or of what kind, and how it is defined.
**Master.** Has not the truth been sufficiently and clearly persuaded by that very Theology you named—which is concerned either solely or mostly with the divine nature—that from those things which are created by it, we know only that it essentially exists, but not *what* that essence itself is? For as we have often said, it surpasses not only the efforts of human reason, but even the purest intellects of the celestial essences <note>i.e., the Angels</note>. Yet that it *is*, the Theologians have rightly scrutinized through the insight of the mind from those things that exist; and that it is *wise*, from the divisions of things into essences, Genera, Species, Differences, and Numbers; and that it *lives*, from the stable motion and mobile state of all things. By this reasoning, they most truly found the cause of all to be triply-subsisting. For as we said, from the essence of those things that are, it is understood to *be*; from the marvelous order of things, it is found to be *wise*; from motion, it is found to be *life*. Therefore, the cause of all and the creative nature both knows and lives. And through this, the seekers of truth have handed down that the Father is understood through essence, the Son through wisdom, and the Holy Spirit through life.
**Disciple.** These things are sufficiently and clearly persuaded to me, and I perceive them to be most true, since indeed *what* or *of what kind* it is cannot be defined at all. For that which is not permitted to be understood at all, cannot be defined at all. I would, however, like to hear by what reasoning the Theologians dared to predicate unity and trinity of the cause of all.
**Master.** In this last proposition of yours, we do not have to labor greatly; especially since St. Dionysius the Areopagite <note>A 5th-century mystical theologian whom Eriugena translated from Greek to Latin, believing him to be the convert of St. Paul.</note>, a most true and approved theologian, persuades us of the mysteries of the divine power and Trinity. For he says that by no words or names, or any articulated significance of the voice, can the supreme and causal essence of all be signified. For it is neither unity nor trinity such as can be thought of by a human intellect, however pure, or considered by an angelic one, however serene. But so that the religious movements of pious souls might think and proclaim something concerning an ineffable and incomprehensible matter—chiefly for the sake of those who demand a reason for the Christian Religion from Catholic men, whether for the sake of learning the truth (if they are good) or for the sake of testing and criticizing (if they are evil)—these symbolic words of religious faith were both discovered and handed down by the Holy Theologians. Thus we believe in our heart and confess with our mouth that the divine goodness of one essence is constituted in three persons in three substances; and this was not discovered without the insight of spiritual intelligence and rational investigation. For contemplating one ineffable cause of all, and one principle, simple and individual, and universal—insofar as they were illuminated by the divine spirit—they called it "Unity." Likewise, beholding that same unity not in a certain singularity and sterility, but in a marvelous and fertile multiplicity, they understood three substances of the unity: namely the unbegotten, the begotten, and the proceeding. They named the relation <note>original: "habitum"</note> of the unbegotten substance to the begotten substance, the Father; the relation of the begotten substance to the unbegotten substance, the Son; and the relation of the proceeding substance to the unbegotten and begotten substance, the Holy Spirit. But since almost the entire intention of the holy expositors of Holy Scripture is occupied with this matter, it is enough, as I judge...

<summary>The Master explains that God "creates Himself" by manifesting in created things and discusses why the Trinity is described as Essence, Wisdom, and Life. He cites Dionysius the Areopagite to explain that terms like "Unity" and "Trinity" are symbolic concessions to human understanding.</summary>

# BOOK ONE

<page-num>9</page-num>

previously discussed as being present. **D.** That is clear enough. However, I would like to hear more plainly about the relation <note>Latin: *habitu*; referring to the disposition or relationship between the persons</note> of the three divine substances. For one might take these mystical names of the Holy Trinity—namely, Father, Son, and Holy Spirit—not according to their relation, but according to their nature. For "Father" seems to be the name of the substance of the father. Similarly, "Son" is the name of the substance of the son, and the naming of the "Holy Spirit" signifies nothing other than his very substance. **M.** Perhaps we ourselves would not deny believing and confessing that very thing, if the supreme and venerable authority of Saint Gregory the Theologian <note>Gregory of Nazianzus (c. 329–390 AD), one of the Cappadocian Fathers</note> and the approval of true reason did not forbid us from believing such things. For when he was asked by the most poisonous Eunomians <note>A radical Arian sect that argued the Son was of a different substance than the Father</note>—who oppose the Catholic faith—concerning this name "Father," whether it signifies nature or operation, he, being wonderfully illuminated by divine grace, replied: *it belongs neither to Nature, nor to operation, but only to the relation to the son.* For if he had answered that "Father" was a name of nature, they would have immediately followed up by saying that "Son" was likewise a name of nature; and if this were granted, it would necessarily follow that "Father" is the name of one nature and "Son" the name of another, for in one and the same nature two names differing from each other cannot exist; and by this they would prove that the Father and the Son are <gloss>i.e., of different essence</gloss> <term>heteroousios <note>original: ἑτερουσίους; meaning "of a different substance"</note></term>, that is, of different essence or nature. A similar answer was given regarding "operation"; for if it were granted to them that "Father" was a name of operation, they would immediately conclude that the Son is a creature, since "Father" would be conceded as a name of his operation, that is, of his creation. **D.** It is most certain that this response is praiseworthy and inspired by truth, but we ought to consider it a little more fully; for it seems to me they could not immediately rebuke him even if he said "Father" was a name of nature. Why? Can two names, differing from each other in sound but not in sense, not be understood in one and the same nature? For we see that Abraham and Isaac—namely, a father and a son—signify one nature; for "Abraham" is not the name of one nature and "Isaac" of another, but of one and the same. **M.** You would be right if you could similarly affirm in your example of Abraham and Isaac that "Abraham" or "Isaac" signifies nothing else than what "Father" and "son" signify in them. For ABRAHAM is the name of Abraham himself, and "Father" is a name for the same Abraham. Similarly, ISAAC is the name of Isaac, and "son" is a name for the same Isaac; but "Abraham" and "father," or "Isaac" and "son," are not predicated of the same thing. For "Abraham" is said of the substance of Abraham, that is, of his specific person; but no one of sound mind will doubt that he is called "father" because of his relation to his son Isaac. The same must be understood of Isaac: for by this name ISAAC, his own individual substance is signified; but his relation <note>Latin: *habitus*</note> to his father is known through the term "son." For you cannot deny that such names as "father" and "son" are relative, not substantive. If, therefore, among us—that is, in our nature—such words are predicated not substantially but relatively, what are we to say of the supreme and holy Essence, in which Holy Scripture has established such names—namely, Father, Son, and Holy Spirit—as the relations or dispositions of the substances toward one another? **D.** I see now that the response of the aforementioned Holy Theologian is entirely supported by truth. For it is not possible, as has been argued, whether in divine or human nature, for a name of relation to be accepted as substance or essence. However, I would like to know clearly and briefly from you whether all the Categories, since they are ten in number, can truly and properly be predicated of the one essence of the supreme divine goodness in three substances, and of the three substances in the same one essence. **M.** I do not know who can speak briefly and clearly about this business; for one must either remain silent about such a cause in all respects and commit it to the simplicity of the orthodox faith—for it surpasses all understanding, as it is written: *who alone has immortality, and dwells in inaccessible light*—or, if one begins to dispute about it, he will necessarily and most truly persuade by many modes and many arguments that two principal parts of Theology must be used. One is the Affirmative, which by the Greeks is called <gloss>affirmative</gloss> <term>kataphatikē <note>original: καταφατικὴ</note></term>; and the Abnegative, which is called <gloss>negative</gloss> <term>apophatikē <note>original: ἀποφατικὴ</note></term>. The one, <gloss>negative</gloss> <term>apophatikē</term>, denies that the divine essence or substance is any of those things which are—that is, which can be spoken of or understood; the other, <gloss>kataphatikē</gloss>, predicates of it all things that are, and is therefore called affirmative—not to confirm that it is any of the things that exist, but to persuade that all things which are *from* it can be predicated *of* it; for the Cause can reasonably be signified by the things it has caused. For it says that it is Truth, Goodness, Essence, Light, Justice, Sun, Star, Spirit, Water, Lion, Bear, Worm, and countless other things. And it teaches this not only from those things which are according to Nature, but even from those things which are against nature; as when it says it is "inebriated," or "foolish," or "insane"; but of these things now is not [the time...]

B

<meta>The previous page concluded a discussion on the symbolic names of God. This page continues the dialogue between a Master (M) and a Disciple (D) regarding whether categories like "Goodness" or "Essence" can be applied literally to the Divine.</meta>

# ON THE DIVISION OF NATURE

<header>ON THE DIVISION OF NATURE.</header>

...to deviate from our purpose; for enough has been said concerning such matters by Saint Dionysius the Areopagite <note>A 5th-century Christian theologian and philosopher, often cited as an authority on mystical theology</note> in his *Symbolic Theology*; and therefore we must return to what you asked. For you had asked whether all the Categories <note>The ten basic ways of describing things (like quality, quantity, relation) defined by Aristotle</note> are to be predicated of God properly, or only some of them. **D.** We must return indeed, but first I think we should consider why that most holy father and theologian pronounced that the aforementioned names—I mean Essence, Goodness, Truth, Justice, Wisdom, and others of that kind, which seem not only divine but most divine, and to signify nothing other than that very divine substance or essence—become metaphorical; that is, transferred from the creature to the Creator. For it must not be thought that he said such things without some mystical and secret reason. **M.** You are wide awake, for I see that this too must not be passed over without consideration; and so I would like you to answer whether you understand anything to be opposite to God, or understood alongside Him. I say "opposite" either by privation, by contrariety, by relation, or by absence. "Understood alongside" means understood eternally together with Him, yet not co-essential with Him. **D.** I see clearly what you mean, and therefore I would not dare to say that anything is either opposite to Him or understood alongside Him as <term>heterousion</term> <note>original: "ἑτερούσιον"; meaning "of a different essence"</note>, that is, something of a different essence than He is. For opposites by relation are always so opposed to each other that they begin to exist at the same time and cease to be at the same time: whether they are of the same nature, like the simple and the double, or ratios; or by negation, like "is" and "is not"; or by absence, like light and darkness; or according to privation, like death and life; or by contrary, like health and weakness, voice and silence. For these are rightly attributed to things that fall under the intellect and the senses, and for this reason they are not in God; for those things which differ from themselves cannot be eternal. For if they were eternal, they would not differ from one another. For eternity is like unto itself, and as a whole through the whole, it subsists in itself as one, simple, and indivisible. There is indeed one principle of all things and one end, differing from itself in nothing.

16. By the same reasoning, I do not know who would dare to affirm that anything is co-eternal with God which is not co-essential with Him; for if this can be thought or found, it necessarily follows that there is not one principle of all things, but two or more widely differing from each other, which true reason is accustomed to reject without any hesitation. Justly do all things begin from one, but from two or more, nothing begins. **M.** You argue correctly, I think. If, therefore, the aforementioned divine names look toward other names opposite to them from across the way, it is necessarily understood that the things properly signified by them also possess opposite contraries; and because of this, they cannot be properly predicated of God, to whom nothing is opposite, or with whom nothing differing in nature is seen co-eternally. For true reason can find none of the aforementioned names, or others similar to them, for which there is not found another name departing from it, either on the opposite side or differing with it in the same genus. And indeed, we know it is necessary in names that we understand those things which are signified by them; but since the divine significations which are predicated of God in <margin>+</margin> Holy Scripture are transferred from the creature to the Creator—if indeed it is correctly said that anything can be predicated of God, which must be considered in another place—they are innumerable, and by the smallness of our reasoning they can neither be found nor collected all at once; yet a few divine words should be set down for the sake of example. God is therefore called "Essence," but properly He is not essence, to which "nothingness" is opposed; He is therefore <term>hyperousios</term> <note>original: "ὑπερούσιος"</note>, that is, <term>super-essential</term>. Likewise He is called "Goodness," but properly He is not goodness; for malice is opposed to goodness; He is therefore <term>hyperagathos</term> <note>original: "ὑπεράγαθος"</note>, more than good, and <term>hyperagathotes</term> <note>original: "ὑπεραγαθότης"</note>, that is, more than goodness. He is called "God," but He is not properly God; for blindness is opposed to vision, and the non-seeing to the seeing; [He is] <term>hypertheos</term> <note>original: "ὑπερθέος"</note>, more than seeing, if <term>Theos</term> <note>original: "Θέος"</note> is interpreted as "seeing." But if you recur to another origin of this name, so that you understand "God" (<term>Theos</term>) to be derived not from the verb <term>theoro</term> <note>original: "θεωρῶ"; I see</note>, but from the verb <term>theo</term> <note>original: "θέω"</note>, that is, "I run," the same reasoning similarly applies; for the non-running is opposed to the running, just as slowness is to speed; He will therefore be <term>hypertheos</term>, that is, more than running; just as it is written, "His word runs swiftly." For we understand this of God the Word, who ineffably runs through all things that are, so that they may be. In the same way we ought to accept "Truth." For falsehood is opposed to truth, and because of this He is not properly truth; He is therefore <term>hyperalethes</term> <note>original: "ὑπεраληθὴς"</note> and <term>hyperaletheia</term> <note>original: "ὑπεраλήθεια"</note>, that is, more than true and more than truth. The same reasoning must be observed in all divine names. For He is not properly called "Eternity," since temporality is opposed to eternity; He is therefore <term>hyperaionios</term> <note>original: "ὑπεραἰώνιος"</note> and <term>hyperaionia</term> <note>original: "ὑπεραιωνία"</note>, that is, more than eternal and more than eternity. Concerning wisdom also, no...

<summary>This page discusses why human language and categories like "Goodness" or "Essence" are only metaphorical when applied to God, as God transcends all opposites. It introduces the "hyper-" (more than) prefix to describe the divine nature as being beyond human concepts like truth, eternity, and motion.</summary>

# BOOK ONE.

no other reason occurs, and therefore it should not be thought that these are predicated of God in a literal sense; since both "unwise" and "folly" are opposites to "wisdom" and "the wise," therefore <note>original: "$\hat{\upsilon}\pi\epsilon\rho\sigma o\phi o\varsigma$"</note> **more-than-wise**, that is, plus-than-wise, and <note>original: "$\hat{\upsilon}\pi\epsilon\rho\sigma o\phi\acute{\iota}\alpha$"</note> **more-than-wisdom**, is rightly and truly said of Him. Similarly, He is **more-than-life**, since death is the opposite of life. In the same way, it must be understood regarding light, for darkness stands against light: thus far, as I judge, enough has been said of these things. **D.** Indeed, it must be admitted that enough has been said. For whatever things are necessary concerning such matters have been brought forward; therefore, the things that must be disputed in the present business, the purpose of our discussion does not now admit: return therefore, if you please, to the consideration of the ten-fold quantity of the Categories. <note>The "Categories" refer to Aristotle's ten classifications of being (substance, quality, quantity, etc.).</note> **M.** I admire the sharpness of your mind, which until now seemed quite watchful. **D.** Why do you say that, I ask? **M.** Did we not say that the ineffable nature can be signified by no word, no name, nor any sensible sound, nor by any signified thing in a literal sense? And you granted this; for it is not literally but metaphorically called Essence, Truth, Wisdom, and other such things, but is called **super-essential**, **more-than-Truth**, **more-than-Wisdom**, and the like; but do these not also seem to be, as it were, certain proper names, if "essence" is not said literally, but "super-essential" is said literally? Similarly, if He is not literally called "truth" or "wisdom," is He literally called "more-than-truth" and "more-than-wisdom"? Therefore, He does not lack proper names; for although these names are not commonly uttered by the Latins under a single accent or a single harmony of composition—except for that name which is "essential"—they are nevertheless pronounced by the Greeks as compounds under a single tone: for you will never, or hardly ever, find "super-good" or "super-eternal" and other similar terms uttered as compounds in Latin. **D.** I myself greatly wonder where I was aiming when I had passed over this inquiry untouched; and therefore I earnestly ask for it to be opened up by you. For in whatever way the divine substance is uttered—whether in simple parts of speech, or compound, or loose, in Greek or in Latin—as long as it is uttered literally, it will not seem to be ineffable. For that which can be spoken in some way is not ineffable. **M.** Now I see you are awake. **D.** I am awake indeed, but concerning this interposed question, I see nothing yet. **M.** Return then to those things which were settled between us a little earlier; for we said, if I am not mistaken, that there are two most sublime parts of Theology; and this we take not from ourselves, but by the authority of St. Dionysius the Areopagite, <note>A 5th-6th century Christian philosopher who wrote on the "Divine Names."</note> who most clearly asserts, as has been said, that Theology is bipartite, that is, <note>original: "$\kappa\alpha\tau\alpha\phi\alpha\tau\iota\kappa\grave{\eta}\nu$"</note> **affirmative**, and <note>original: "$\alpha\pi o\phi\alpha\tau\iota\kappa\grave{\eta}\nu$"</note> **negative**, which Cicero translates as "intention" and "repulsion." We, however, so that the force of the names may be clearer, have preferred to translate them as **Affirmation** and **Negation**. **D.** I see that I remember such things; but how they may be of use to these things we now wish to consider, I do not yet know. **M.** Do you not see that these two, namely Affirmation and Negation, are mutually opposed to one another? **D.** I see it well; and I judge that nothing can be more contrary. **M.** Pay attention then more diligently; for when you have arrived at the insight of perfect reasoning, you will clearly enough consider that these two, which seem to be contrary to each other, are in no way opposed to themselves while they are concerned with the divine nature, but in all things and through all things they agree with each other. And so that this may become clearer, let us use a few examples; for instance, the **affirmative** says: "He is Truth"; the **negative** contradicts: "He is not Truth"; here there seems to be a certain form of contradiction, but when the intention is looked at, no controversy is found. For she who says "He is Truth" does not affirm that the divine substance is literally Truth, but that He can be called by such a name through a **Metaphor** from the creature to the Creator; for she clothes the divine essence—which is bare and stripped of all literal signification—with such vocabularies. But she who says "He is not Truth," rightly acknowledging the divine nature to be incomprehensible and ineffable, does not deny that He *is*, but that He is neither literally called "Truth" nor *is* "Truth" in that sense. For she knows how to strip the divinity of all the significations with which the affirmative clothes it. One says, "He is wisdom," for example, clothing Him; the other says, "He is not Wisdom," stripping Him. One therefore says, "He can be called this," but does not say, "He literally is this"; the Other says, "He is not this," although He can be named from this. **D.** These things, if I am not mistaken, I see most clearly; things which until now seemed to me to be discrepant from each other, now appear clearer than light to agree with each other, and in nothing to dissent from themselves when they are considered regarding God; but how they pertain to the solution of the present question, I confess I do not yet know.

**M.** Attend then more vigilantly, and those very significations which were added before—I mean **super-essential**, **more-than-Truth**, **more-than-wisdom**, and others like them—explain as much as is possible for you to which part of Theology they pertain, whether they are to be applied to the affirmative or the negative. **D.** I do not quite dare to discern this by myself.

<summary>The Teacher explains the distinction between affirmative (kataphatic) and negative (apophatic) theology, arguing that they do not contradict each other when applied to God. He introduces "super-essential" terms as a way to bridge the gap between naming God and acknowledging His ineffability.</summary>
<keywords>affirmative theology, negative theology, Dionysius the Areopagite, ineffability, super-essential, metaphor, divine nature, kataphatic, apophatic</keywords>

--- Page 32 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between a Master (M) and a Disciple (D) regarding the nature of God and the limits of language, specifically focusing on the transition from "Super-essential" naming to the Aristotelian Categories.</meta>

# ON THE DIVISION OF NATURE.

I dare to say it. For when I consider that the aforementioned terms lack the negative particle, which is *not*, I am afraid to join them to the negative part of Theology. If, however, I join them to the affirmative part, I find that my understanding of them does not agree. For he who says "He is super-essential," openly denies that He is "essential." <note>original: "superessentialis... essentialem". The prefix "super-" (above/beyond) implies that God is not "essence" in the way created things are.</note> And by this, although the negation does not appear in the pronunciation of the words, it is nevertheless not hidden from the understanding of one who considers it well. Therefore, as I think, I am forced to admit that these aforementioned terms, which seem to lack negation, belong more to the negative part of Theology than to the affirmative, as far as can be understood.

**M.** I see that you have answered most cautiously and vigilantly, and I greatly approve of how you have most subtly perceived the meaning of the negative within the pronunciation of the affirmative part. Therefore, if it pleases you, let the solution to this present question be as follows: that all these things which are predicated of God by the addition of the particles "super" <note>above</note> or "more than"—such as "super-essential," "more than Truth," "more than Wisdom," and the like—are most fully comprehensive of the two aforementioned parts of Theology. Thus, in pronunciation they maintain the form of the affirmative, but in understanding they hold the power of the negative. Let us conclude this with a brief example. "He is Essence" is an affirmation; "He is not Essence" is a negation; "He is super-essential" is Affirmation and negation at the same time. For on the surface it lacks negation, but in the understanding it is full of negation. For he who says "He is super-essential" does not say what He is, but what He is not; for he says He is not essence, but more than essence. But what that is, which is more than essence, he does not express—asserting that God is not any of those things that exist, but that He is more than those things that exist; yet what that "being" is, he in no way defines.

**D.** I think we should not dwell longer on this question; and now, if it seems right, the nature of the Categories should be considered.

**M.** Aristotle, the most acute among the Greeks—the discoverer, as they say, of the discernment of natural things—concluded the innumerable varieties of all things which are after God and created by Him into ten universal genera, which he called the ten Categories, that is, the Predicaments. For as it seemed to him, nothing can be found in the multitude of created things and the various movements of minds that cannot be included in one of the aforementioned. These are called by the Greeks:

-> *ousia, poiotes, posotes, pros ti, keisthai, hexis, topos, chronos, prattein, pathein.* <-

Which in Latin are called:

-> Essence, Quantity, Quality, Relation, Position, Habit <note>or Condition</note>, Place, Time, Action, and Passion <note>or Being Acted Upon</note>. <-

Of these ten genera there are innumerable subdivisions, about which the present business does not permit us to dispute, lest we withdraw too far from our purpose—especially since that part of Philosophy called Dialectic <note>Logic</note> is concerned with the divisions of these genera from the most general to the most specific, and again by collection from the most specific to the most general.

17. But as the Holy Father Augustine says in his books *On the Trinity*, when one arrives at Theology—that is, the investigation of the Divine Essence—the power of the Categories is entirely extinguished. For in the very natures created by God and their movements, the Categories prevail with whatever power they have. But in that Nature which can neither be spoken of nor understood, they fail in every way and in all things. Nevertheless, as we said before, just as almost all things which are properly predicated of the nature of created things are said of the Creator of things through Metaphor for the sake of significance, so also the meanings of the Categories, which are properly recognized in created things, can be uttered not absurdly regarding the Cause of all things—not that they properly signify what It is, but that they may suggest figuratively what we, in a certain way seeking It, ought probably to think about It.

**D.** I see clearly that by no reasoning can the Categories be properly predicated of the ineffable Nature.

**M.** You see rightly; and I think we did not wish to labor in vain in the aforementioned reasonings on the two parts of Theology. For we would not have been able to arrive so easily and almost without any effort at this dispute of the Categories—namely, that they cannot be properly predicated of God—unless we had first clearly established that the primordial causes pre-created by the one Cause of all (I mean Essence, Goodness, Virtue, Truth, Wisdom, and others of this kind) signify God in no other way than figuratively. For if the created causes of all natures ordered after Him—which are perceived only by the affections of a pure mind—differ from the one ineffable Cause by the excellence of essence so much that they cannot be properly signified by their own names, what must be said of these ten...

<summary>This page explores the "super-essential" nature of God, arguing that terms like "Super-Wisdom" are both affirmative and negative. It introduces Aristotle's ten categories, concluding that while they describe created reality, they can only be applied to God metaphorically.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between a Teacher and a Disciple regarding the application of Aristotle's ten categories to the Divine Nature. The previous discussion established that God transcends human language.</meta>

# BOOK ONE.

...from the aforementioned types <note>The ten categories of Aristotle: substance, quantity, quality, relation, place, time, position, state, action, and passion.</note>, which are discerned not only in things understood by the mind, but also in things perceived by the senses? Is it truly believable that they could be predicated truly and properly of the divine and ineffable nature?
**Disciple.** I judge it to be unbelievable.
**Teacher.** It is not, therefore, <term>Ousia</term> <note>original: οὐσία; Greek for "Being" or "Substance."</note>, because it is more than <term>Ousia</term>, and yet it is called <term>Ousia</term> because it is the creator of the essences of all <term>Ousias</term> <note>original: οὐσιῶν; plural of Ousia.</note>. It is not quantity because it is more than quantity. For all quantity is extended in three dimensions: namely Length, Breadth, and Height; which same three dimensions are stretched out by the number six; for Length goes up and down; Height goes forward and backward; Breadth is extended to the right and to the left. God, however, lacks all dimension, and therefore lacks quantity. Likewise, quantity exists in a number of parts, either naturally joined together, such as a line and time; or naturally disjoined, such as numbers, whether corporeal or intellectual. But the divine substance is neither composed of continuous parts, nor distinguished by divisible ones. It is not, therefore, quantity. Yet quantity is not incongruously attributed to Him in two ways: either because "quantity" is often used to mean the greatness of power, or because He is the principle and cause of all quantity. Regarding Quality, it is to be understood no differently: for God is no quality, no quality happens to Him, and He participates in none. But in truth, quality is very frequently predicated of Him, either because He is the founder of all quality, or because quality is most frequently used in the sense of virtues. For both Goodness and Justice, and the other virtues, are said to be qualities; but God is Virtue, and more than Virtue.

18. The reasoning regarding Relation, however, does not lie open as clearly as the definitions of the other Categories appear; for this Category alone seems as if it might be properly predicated of God. And therefore I see that it must be investigated with the greatest diligence: whether in the supreme and Holy Trinity of the three greatest substances <note>The three Persons of the Trinity.</note>, "Father" is predicated relatively to the "Son," and similarly "Son" to the "Father," and also the "Holy Spirit" to the Father and the Son, because He is the Spirit of both; for the holy father Gregory the Theologian <note>Gregory of Nazianzus (c. 329–390 AD), a key figure in defining Trinitarian theology.</note> asserts without doubt that these are names of relationships. Or whether, just like the other Categories, this one which is called Relation or relationship is predicated of God metaphorically rather than properly—but whether this be so or not must be believed and understood.
**Disciple.** You proceed reasonably, and indeed toward investigating the mystery of truth. For none of the Categories except this one alone seems to be properly predicated of God; but whether it is so or not must be inquired into most piously and cautiously: for if it is said properly of God, almost all our aforementioned reasoning will be emptied out. For we said universally that nothing can be either said or understood properly [of God]; especially since the Category of Relation will not be counted among the ten types of Categories if it is pronounced properly of God. If this were settled, the number of Categories would be concluded not by ten, but by nine. It remains, therefore, for us to understand that this Category also, just like the others, is predicated of God figuratively. Indeed, true reasoning invites and compels us to this, lest those things which were said before begin to waver. For what then? Does it stand against true reasoning if we say that "Father" and "Son" are names of that very relationship which is called "toward something" <note>The Aristotelian definition of Relation: *ad aliquid*.</note>, and yet are more than relationship? For it is not to be believed that the same relationship exists in the most excellent substances of the divine essence as exists in those things which were created by it after it. For, unless I am mistaken, just as He surpasses all essence, wisdom, and power; so also He ineffably transcends all relationship. For who would believe that such a relationship exists between the Father and the Word as one can think of between Abraham and Isaac? For here, this fleshly relationship is sought from the division of nature through the sin of the first man and multiplication through generation; there, an ineffable bond of the unbegotten and begotten substance is believed; and as it is granted, it is known by a ray of divine light. Here, what is sought did not proceed from nature, as we said, but from a fault; there, what is thought is known to proceed from the ineffable fertility of divine goodness. But let us pass to the other Categories.
**Disciple.** Six remain, if I am not mistaken, of which the first is <term>Keisthai</term> <note>original: κεῖσθαι; Greek for "to lie" or "position."</note>, that is, "to lie down," which others call Position. Now, Position is understood as the placement of some creature, whether visible or invisible: for example, it is said of some body, it either lies or stands; similarly it is said of the soul, if it is quiet, it "lies"; if vigilant, it "stands." For "State" is usually applied to this Category; for "Motion" is referred to Time. But because God neither stands nor lies, the aforementioned Category can by no means be properly predicated of Him. But truly, since He is the cause of standing and lying—for in Him all things both "stand," that is, they subsist immutably according to their own reasons, and "lie," that is, they rest, for He is the end of all things beyond which they desire nothing—"to lie" or "Position" can be said of Him figuratively...

<summary>This page explores how the Aristotelian categories of Quantity, Quality, Relation, and Position apply to God, concluding that they can only be used metaphorically or figuratively because God transcends all human definitions.</summary>

# ON THE DIVISION OF NATURE
<meta>catchword: with</meta>

be predicated. For if God truly and properly lies down, or sits, or stands, He is not without position: if He is not without position, He is in a place: but He is not in a place, therefore He is contained by no position. <note>The Master argues that since God is omnipresent and infinite, physical postures (categories of 'Position') can only be applied to Him metaphorically.</note>

19. **M.** I see clearly what you mean; and through this I see that we must pass to the Category of Habit <note>original: "Habitudo" - here meaning 'relation' or 'disposition'</note>; which, because of its excessive breadth, seems to be the most obscure of all Categories: for there is almost no Category in which a certain habit cannot be found; for even essences or substances look toward one another by a certain habit. For we say "rational essence" and "irrational": by what proportion, that is, by what habit, do they look toward each other? For it would not be called irrational except by the habit of the presence of reason: For every proportion is a habit; although not every habit is a proportion. For properly, a proportion can be found in no fewer than two things; but a Habit is perceived even in individual things: For example, the habit of a rational soul is Virtue; therefore proportion is a certain species of habit. If, however, you wish it to be declared by an example how a proportional habit is found in essence, choose an example from numbers. For numbers, as I judge, are understood essentially in all things; for in numbers the essence of all things subsists: Do you see, therefore, what kind of proportion is in two and three? **D.** I see clearly, and I judge it to be a sesquialter <note>A ratio of 3:2, or one and a half.</note>, and by this one example of all other substantial numbers placed among themselves, I can know the various species of proportion. **M.** Attend therefore to the rest: and know that there are no species of quantity, or of quality, or of that which is called "toward something" <note>The category of Relation.</note>, or of position or place, or of time, of acting or suffering, in which some species of habit is not found. **D.** I have often sought such things, and so I found them. For to use such examples, in quantities, the great and small and middle compared among themselves possess much habit. Likewise in the quantities of numbers, lines, times, and other similar things, you will clearly find habits of proportion.

20. Similarly in quality; for example, in Colors, white and black, and the middle, whatever color it may be, is joined to itself by habit. For white and black, which occupy the extreme places of colors, look toward each other by the habit of extremity. But color looks toward its extremes, I mean white and black, by the habit of being in the middle. In that Category also which is called <gloss>toward something</gloss> <term>pros ti</term> <note>original Greek: πρὸς τί</note>, that is, "to something" or "relation," it clearly appears what kind of habit exists of the Father to the Son, or of the Son to the Father; of a friend to a friend; of the double to the single, and others of this kind.

21. Concerning Position also it is easily evident, how standing and lying down possess a certain habit between themselves. For these respond to each other from the opposite diameter; and you will by no means think of the understanding of standing as absolute from the understanding of lying down; but they always occur together, although in other respects they do not appear together.

22. What is to be said of place? when higher, lower, and middle things are considered; are they lacking in habit? By no means; for these names do not come from the nature of things, but from a certain respect of the one looking at them by parts. For "up" and "down" do not exist in the universe, and therefore neither higher nor lower; nor are there middle things in the universe; for the consideration of the whole rejects these, but the intention of the parts introduces them. The same is true of greater and smaller. For nothing can be small or great in its own kind, yet from the thought of those comparing different qualities, such things are found. And therefore the contemplation of places or parts gives birth to habit in such things. For no nature becomes greater or smaller than another nature, just as it is neither higher nor lower, since one nature of all things subsists, created from the one God. What of Time? does not habit clearly smile upon us in those things while they are compared among themselves? For example, of the Day to the hours, of the hour to the points, of the point to the moment, of the moment to the atoms <note>In medieval timekeeping, an 'atom' was the smallest unit of time, roughly 1/15th of a second.</note>. Similarly in the higher measurements of times, if anyone should ascend, he will find it. For in all these the habit of the whole to the parts, and also of the parts to the whole, is perceived. **D.** Certainly not otherwise. **M.** What in the various motions of acting or suffering? does not habit shine back everywhere? for to love and to be loved are the habits of the lover and the beloved, since they look toward each other, whether they be in one person, which is called by the Greeks <term>autopatheia</term> <note>original Greek: αὐτοπάθεια</note>, that is, when action and passion are inspected in one and the same person; as "I love myself": or between two persons, which is called by the Greeks <term>heteropatheia</term> <note>original Greek: ἑτεροπάθεια</note>, that is, when there is one person of the lover, and another of the beloved, as "I love you." **D.** And I discern these things to be true. **M.** I ask you therefore, why this Category of habit, since it seems to be naturally present in the other Categories, and specifically by itself as if by its own proper reasons...

They conclude that spatial and temporal distinctions like 'up' and 'down' are human perceptions rather than inherent properties of the unified nature created by God.</summary>

# BOOK ONE.

...supported by all things, it should hold its own place within the decimal quantity of the Categories. Or is it perhaps because it is found in all things, and furthermore subsists in itself? For the same reasoning must be observed in the Category of essence. What then? Since there are ten Categories, is one of them called essence or substance, while nine are indeed accidents, and subsist within a subsistence? For they cannot subsist by themselves. Essence seems to be in all things, without which they cannot exist, and yet it holds its own place by itself. For that which belongs to all belongs properly to none, but is common to all; and while it subsists in all, it does not cease to be by its own proper reason in itself. The same must be said of quantity. For we say: how great an essence, how great a quality, how great a relation, how great a position, how great a condition <note>original: "habitus"</note>, how large a place, how small or spacious a time, how great an action, how great a passion? Do you see how widely quantity extends through the other Categories, and yet it does not abandon its own proper state? What of Quality? Is it not also frequently predicated of all Categories? For we ask: what kind of <term>essence</term> <note>original: "οὐσία" (ousia)</note>, what kind of magnitude, what kind of relation, Position, Condition, Place, Time, Acting, Suffering? For we ask what kind all these things are; yet does Quality not abandon the reason of its own proper genus? What wonder is it then if the Category of Condition <note>original: "Habitudinis"</note>, while it is observed in all things, is said to possess its own proper reason? It is in no way to be wondered at, for true reason persuades us it cannot be otherwise. **M.** Do you not see, therefore, that the divine essence is a participant in no condition, yet it can be predicated of it not incongruously, since He is the cause of it? For if Condition were properly predicated of Him, He would in no way belong to Himself but to another; every condition is properly understood to be in some subject, and is an accident of something, which is impious to believe of God, to whom nothing happens as an accident, and who happens as an accident to no one, and is understood in no one, and no one in Him. **D.** Enough has been disputed concerning this Category, I believe.

23. **M.** What then? Can we not briefly gather from these things which have been said before concerning the remaining Categories? For God is neither place nor time; yet He is called the place of all things metaphorically <note>original: "translativè"</note>, and time, since He is the cause of all places and times. Indeed, the definitions of all things, like certain places, subsist in Him; and from Him as if from a certain time, through Him as if through a certain time, and into Him as if into a certain end of times, the motion of all things both begins and moves, and finishes; while He Himself neither moves Himself through time, nor is moved by another. For what? If He were properly called place and time, He would seem not to be outside all things by the excellence of His essence, but to be included in the number of all things that are. For place and time are counted among all things that are created. For in these two the whole world which now exists consists, and without them it cannot be. Therefore they are called by the Greeks <term>the "without-which" of the whole</term> <note>original: "τὸ ἄνευ τοῦ πᾶν" (to aneu tou pan)</note>, that is, those things without which the Universe cannot exist: for everything that is in the world must be moved by time and defined by place; and place itself is defined, and time moves; but God is neither moved nor defined; for the place by which all places are defined is the place of places; and because He is placed by no one, but places all things within Himself, He is not place, but more than place. For He is defined by no one, but defines all things. He is therefore the cause of all. In the same way, the cause of times moves times; but it is moved by no one, in no time. For He is more than time, and more than motion. Therefore He is neither place nor Time. Thus indeed the things which have been said appear clearer than light, so much so that enough seems to have been said for now concerning the nature of the Categories, and concerning their metaphorical transfer into the signification of the divine essence, for the sake of the other matters which necessarily pertain to the present business.

24. Of these ten Genera, four are in a state of rest, that is, <term>essence</term> <note>original: "οὐσία"</note>, Quantity, Position, Place: but six are in motion: Quality, Relation, Condition, Time, Acting, Suffering. I do not think this is hidden from you. **D.** On the contrary, it is clearly evident to me, and I would ask nothing more of these; but to what purpose is this? **M.** So that you may clearly know that the ten aforesaid genera are encompassed by two other higher and more general ones, namely, motion and rest; which again are collected by a most general genus, which is accustomed to be called by the Greeks <term>the All</term> <note>original: "τὸ πᾶν" (to pan)</note>, but by our people the Universe. **D.** This is very pleasing, because of those who think that in the nature of things no genus more general can be found to precede the ten aforesaid genera, which were both discovered and named by Aristotle. **M.** Does this division of the Categories into motion and rest seem valid to you, that is, four in rest, six in motion?

25. **D.** Valid indeed, but as of yet it is not sufficiently clear to me concerning two of them, I mean condition and relation; for these two Categories seem to me to be more in rest than in motion.

<summary>The Master explains how the ten Aristotelian categories (like place, time, and quality) relate to God only as their cause, not as literal descriptions. He further organizes these categories into the broader concepts of Motion and Rest, which together constitute the Universe.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between the Master (M) and the Disciple (D) regarding the ten categories of being and whether they are characterized by motion or rest.</meta>

# ON THE DIVISION OF NATURE

->**Master:**<- For whatever has reached a perfected state remains unchangeable. If it is moved in any way, it is clear that it is no longer a "state" <note>original: *habitus*; refers to a stable disposition or acquired quality</note>. For **Virtue** in the soul is truly a state of the mind when it adheres to it unchangeably so that it cannot be separated from it; therefore, no true state is found in bodies: for a man who is armed or clothed can lack those arms or garments. Likewise, in **Relation**, stability is judged to prevail; for the relation of a father to a son, or of the double to the single, and vice versa, is immobile: for a father is always the father of the son; similarly, the son is always the son of the father, etc. **M.** Perhaps you would not have hesitated so much if you looked more closely, because everything that is not simultaneously and naturally perfect within a creature, but proceeds through certain increments toward an inseparable and unchangeable perfection, must necessarily be in motion. Now, every state ascends toward perfection by a certain motion in that which possesses the state. Regarding this perfection, let him who is firm in this life take heart. Therefore, a state is in motion. Likewise, I wonder why you doubt regarding Relation, when you see that it cannot exist in one and the same thing; for it is always seen in two. Now, who has doubted that the appetite of two things toward each other occurs by a certain motion? There is also another reason which most clearly distinguishes those things in motion from those at rest. For I will not say more now about that most general principle which manifests that all things created by God after God are in motion. For all things are moved from non-existence into existence through generation, the Divine Goodness calling all things from non-being into being so that they may exist from nothing; so that each of those things that exist is moved toward its own essence, its genus and species, and its number by a natural appetite. We properly say those things are at rest which subsist through themselves and need no subject in order to be. But those things which exist in something else, because they cannot exist through themselves, we judge (not incongruously) to be in motion. Thus, state and relation are in a certain subject, in which they always desire to be by a natural motion, because they cannot exist without it. Therefore, they are in motion.

<margin>26.</margin> **D.** What then shall we say of Place? Of Quantity? Of Position? Which you have placed in a state of rest. For regarding <term>ousia</term> <note>original: *οὐσία*; essence or substance</note>, which is the same as essence, no one doubts that it needs nothing else to subsist. For all other things are believed to be supported by it. But these—I mean place, quantity, and position—are numbered among the accidents of essence. And through this, by desiring that subject in which they are and without which they cannot exist, they are moved. And if this is so, all things are in motion except <term>ousia</term>, which alone lacks motion; with that one exception by which all things desire to be, since it alone subsists through itself. **M.** Your inquiry is not altogether absurd, because you follow common opinion; but if you look more closely, you will find that place is contained by nothing, but rather contains all things that are placed in it. For if place is nothing other than the boundary and definition of every finite nature, surely place does not desire to be in something; rather, all things that are in it rightly desire that very boundary and end of theirs, in which they are naturally contained, and without which they seem to flow into infinity. Therefore, place is not in motion, since all things in it move toward it, while it stands still. Reason teaches the same regarding quantity and position. For what of it? Everything that participates in sensible or intelligible quantity or position desires to rest in it only so that it may reach perfect quantity or position: therefore they do not desire, but are desired. Thus they are not in motion; they stand still. **D.** Should these three—Quantity, Position, and Place—then be called accidents of <term>ousia</term>, or are they substances in themselves?

<margin>27.</margin> **M.** I see this also is worthy of inquiry; for according to the opinion of the Dialecticians <note>The practitioners of logic and formal debate, specifically following Aristotelian categories</note>, everything that exists is either a subject, or "of a subject," or "in a subject." Yet, when consulted, true reason responds that "subject" and "of a subject" are one and the same, and differ in nothing. For if, as they say, Cicero is a subject and a primary substance, while "man" is "of a subject" and a secondary substance, what is the difference according to nature, except that one is one in number and the other in species? Since species is nothing other than the unity of numbers, and number is nothing other than the plurality of the species. If, therefore, the species is whole and one, and individuals are one in number, and the numbers are one individual in species, I do not see what the distance is between "subject" and "of a subject" regarding nature. The same must be understood regarding the accidents of primary substance. For that which is said to be "in a subject" is not one thing, and that which is "in a subject" and "of a subject" simultaneously another. For to use an example, a discipline <note>A branch of knowledge or skill</note> is one and the same in itself and in its species and numbers. Therefore, the specific discipline of an individual (which is called by Dialecticians "in a subject" only) is not one thing, and the general discipline (which is called by the same "in a subject" and "of a subject") another,

<summary>The Master and Disciple debate whether the Aristotelian categories like Place, Quantity, and Relation are characterized by motion or rest, concluding that Essence and Place are stable while others move toward perfection. They also begin to deconstruct the logical distinction between individual subjects and their species.</summary>

--- Page 37 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical dialogue regarding the nature of Being and the Aristotelian Categories, specifically focusing on how Essence relates to its surroundings.</meta>

# BOOK ONE.

It is called, as if in a subject—namely, the first subsisting substance—to be predicated of a subject, that is, as the proper characteristic of some discipline; but it is one and the same in the whole and in the parts, and through this it remains both the subject and within the subject. However, if you look more sharply, following the footsteps of Saint Gregory the Theologian <note>Gregory of Nazianzus (c. 329–390 AD), one of the Cappadocian Fathers.</note> and his most wise interpreter Maximus <note>Maximus the Confessor (c. 580–662 AD), a Christian monk and theologian.</note>, you will find that **essence** <note>original: οὐσίαν (ousian)</note> is entirely incomprehensible in all things that exist, not only to the senses but even to the intellect. And therefore, it is understood to exist from these, as it were, its "circumstances"—I mean place, quantity, and position; to these is also added time. For within these, as if within certain surrounding boundaries, essence is known to be enclosed, so that neither do the accidents <note>Properties that are not essential to a thing's nature, like color or size.</note> seem to be in it as if subsisting in it (because they are external), nor can they exist without it (because it is their center, around which times revolve, while places, quantities, and positions are situated on all sides). Therefore, certain of the **Categories** <note>The ten classifications of reality defined by Aristotle.</note> are predicated around **essence**, which are called **circumstances** <note>original: περιεστῶτα (periestōta), meaning "standing around."</note>, because they are seen to exist around it. Others, however, are in it, which are called by the Greeks **accidents** <note>original: συμβεβηκότα (symbebēkota)</note>: Quality, Relation, Condition, Action, and Passion <note>In this context, "Passion" means being acted upon or receiving an action.</note>. These are also understood outside of essence in other Categories; for example, Quality in quantity, such as color in a body. Likewise, Quality in **essence**, such as invisibility and incomprehensibility in genera. And likewise Relation outside of **essence**, such as Father to son, or Son to father. For these are not from nature, but from the accidental corruptible generation of bodies, since a father is not a father by the nature of the son, nor is a son a son by the nature of the father. For the father and son are of one and the same nature. Moreover, no Nature begets itself, or is begotten by itself. Therefore, relation is in **essence** itself when a genus is referred to a species, and a species to a Genus. For a Genus is the Genus of a Species, and a Species is the Species of a Genus. **Condition** <note>original: Habitus</note> is also found both outside and inside **essence**; we say "armed" or "clothed" according to the body. But the Condition of **essence**, of a genus or species, is that very immovable virtue through which a Genus, while divided through Species, always remains one and individual in itself, and is the whole in each individual species, and the individual species are one in it. The same virtue is also perceived in the species, which while divided through numbers <note>i.e., individual instances.</note>, guards the inexhaustible power of its individual unity. And all the numbers into which it seems to be divided unto infinity are finite in it, and are one individual thing. Concerning **acting** and **being acted upon**, no one doubts them, since we see that bodies, while they pertain to quantity, both act and are acted upon. The genera and species of **essence** itself also seem to act when they multiply themselves into diverse species and numbers. If anyone, by the power of true reason according to that discipline called **analytics** <note>original: ἀναλυτικὴ (analytikē)</note>, should unite numbers into species, and species into Genera, and genera into **essence** by collecting them, they are said to be "acted upon"; not because he himself collects them (for they are collected by nature, just as they are divided), but because he seems to collect them by an act of reason. For when he divides the same, he is similarly said to "act," and they to "be acted upon."

28. **D.** <note>The Disciple</note> Although these things seem to be obscure, they do not escape my mind so much that nothing pure and distinct shines back to it. And since I see almost all the Categories chained together among themselves, so that they can hardly be discerned from each other by certain reason—for all are inserted into all, as I see—I beg you to open up in what specific property each individual thing may be found. **M.** <note>The Master</note> How does it seem to you? Is **essence** contained universally and properly in the most general genera and in the more general genera, and in the genera themselves and their species, and again in the most specific species, which are called **atoms**, that is, individuals? **D.** I see nothing else in which **essence** can naturally be in being, except in genera and species, descending from the top to the bottom—that is, from the most general to the most specific, which are individuals; or reciprocally upwards from individuals to the most general. For in these, as if in natural parts, universal **essence** subsists; therefore in the genus toward the rest. **M.** Does the property of quantity seem to you to be anywhere else except in the number of parts, spaces, and measures, whether they be continuous (as are those of lines, times, and other things contained by continuous quantity) or whether they be separate, distinguished by certain and natural boundaries (as are numbers and every multitude, in which it is manifest that discrete quantity consists)? **D.** This also appears clearly. **M.** And does that which is called Quality hold its proper seat anywhere except in figures and surfaces, whether in natural bodies or in geometric ones, such as those that are flat, triangular, square, polygonal, or round? Also solids in surface, such as those that are cubic, conic, or spherical. For solid bodies, whether natural or Geometric, insofar as they are considered internally because of the three spaces in which they exist—I mean length, width, and height—are applied to quantity. But when their surface is perceived, they are joined to Quality. Likewise in incorporeal things, is it not—

<summary>This page explores the relationship between Essence (Ousia) and the Aristotelian Categories, arguing that while Essence is incomprehensible on its own, it is understood through its "circumstances" like quantity, quality, and place. The dialogue clarifies how genera and species contain essence and how different categories like quality and quantity overlap in physical and geometric forms.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between a Master (M) and a Disciple (D) regarding the nature of space, matter, and the Aristotelian categories. It follows the discussion of whether "place" is a physical body or an incorporeal definition.</meta>

# BOOK ONE.

-> C 3 <-

yield to corporeal things. For the **Idea** <gloss>original: *ιδεα*</gloss> <note>The Greek term *idea* here refers to the formal archetype or mental blueprint of a thing, which exists beyond the physical world.</note> is incorporeal, and is subject to no corporeal sense; around which, or within which, the other nine Categories revolve. But if that Idea is incorporeal, does it not seem to you that all things which either adhere to it, or subsist in it, and cannot exist without it, must also be incorporeal?

36. Therefore, all Categories are incorporeal when understood in themselves. Yet some of them, through a certain "wonderful union"—as Gregory says—compose visible matter. Others, however, appear in nothing and always remain incorporeal. For **Essence** <gloss>original: *ουσία* (ousia)</gloss>, relation, place, time, acting, and suffering are reached by no corporeal sense. But quantity, quality, position, and habit, while joining together to form matter (as we said before), are accustomed to be perceived by corporeal sense. If, therefore, "place" is numbered among those things which in no way yield to corporeal senses; and if bodies, should they not be perceived by the senses, are not bodies: is it not granted that place is not a body? For I speak of "bodies" as those things which are composed from the union of the four elements of the world. For the four elements of the world, while they are bodies distinct in themselves, surpass all mortal sense by their ineffable natural subtlety and purity. Therefore, place is one thing, and body is another. Or does it seem otherwise to you? 

**D.** In no way: and this final conclusion of our reasoning by no means allows us to concede that place and body are of the same kind. But I am not a little moved by what I see you have interposed: that visible matter joined to form (for whatever appears, appears through form) is nothing other than a "concourse of certain accidents." <note>In medieval philosophy, "accidents" are qualities like color or weight that can change without changing the essential nature of the thing.</note>

**M.** Let it not move you. For as I said, the great Gregory of Nyssa <note>A 4th-century Church Father whose work *On the Making of Man* (De hominis opificio) is cited here to argue that matter is actually a collection of non-material properties.</note> in his *Sermon on the Image* persuades us of this with certain reasons: saying that matter is nothing else but a certain composition of accidents, proceeding from invisible causes toward visible matter. And not without merit: for if there were some simple, immutable, and in no way dissolvable essence within this corporeal and dissolvable matter, it could by no means be entirely dissolved by any action. But now it is dissolved. Therefore, nothing indissolvable underlies it. For genera, species, and individuals <note>original: *atoma* (atoms/indivisibles)</note> always exist and remain for this reason: because there is in them something one and individual which cannot be dissolved or destroyed. The accidents themselves also remain immutably in their nature for this reason: because one individual thing underlies them all, in which all things naturally subsist as one.

37. **D.** Nothing is truer, as I judge; and I wait for you to conclude the present inquiry. 

**M.** What remains except for us to say, for example, that while we see our bodies situated on this earth, or surrounded by this air, it is nothing other than bodies being within bodies? By the same reasoning, fish in the waves, planets in the ether, stars in the firmament, are bodies within bodies: the smaller in the larger, the thicker in the thinner, the light in the lighter, the pure in the purer. For true reason teaches that all these sensible things, just like intelligible things, are contained in their own proper "places"—that is, in their natural definitions.

38. **D.** I do not resist this conclusion, as I see it is true; but I wonder greatly why it has come into the common use of human life to say that all these bodies—whether celestial, aerial, aquatic, or earthly—are the "places" of the smaller bodies within them: just as one estimates **Essence** <gloss>original: *ουσίαν*</gloss> to be nothing other than this visible and tangible body. Therefore, I earnestly beg that it not be tedious for you to dispute longer regarding this present difficulty. 

**M.** What then? Surely it was settled between us long ago that all things known by corporeal sense, reason, or intellect can rightly be predicated of God the Creator of all; yet the contemplation of pure truth approves that He is none of those things which are predicated of Him. 

**D.** Reason teaches that this is unshakable and clearer than light.

39. **M.** If, therefore, all things that exist are predicated of God—not indeed properly, but by a certain mode of "translation" <note>Metaphorical or figurative language (translatio).</note> since they are from Him—what wonder is it if all things that are "in a place," while they seem to be surrounded on all sides by things larger than themselves, can be named "place"? This is so even though none of them is properly a place, but they are contained by the "place" of their own proper nature. And since we see through **metonymy** <note>A figure of speech where a thing is called by the name of something associated with it.</note> (that is, "trans-naming") that things contained are named by the things that contain them; yet they are not so contained by them that they could not subsist within their natural boundaries without them. Indeed, the usage of mortals is to call a house a "wife" or a "family," though these are naturally distinct. For a house does not provide substantial being to a wife or family; rather, the "place" of their nature does. But when they are possessed within it, they are accustomed to be named by it. And similarly, the things that contain are named by the things contained; for example, Air contains light; therefore, illuminated air is called "light."

<summary>The Master explains that "place" is not a physical body but a conceptual definition, arguing that visible matter is merely a collection of incorporeal qualities (accidents) held together by a divine or formal order.</summary>
