<meta>This page continues the discussion of the liberal arts and begins a philosophical inquiry into the nature of "place" and the composition of the physical world, likely following an introduction to grammar.</meta>

# BOOK ONE

**GRAMMAR** <note>original: "Grammatica"</note> is the guardian of articulate speech and the directress of the sciences. **RHETORICA** is the science that discusses a limited case with eloquence and ornamentation, considering the person, the matter, the occasion, the quality, the place, the time, and the resources. This science can be briefly defined thus: **RHETORICA** is the wise and copious science of a limited case through seven circumstances. **DIALECTICA** is the diligent and investigative science of the rational conceptions of the common mind. **ARITHMETICA** is the fixed and uncorrupted science of the contemplations underlying numbers. **GEOMETRIA** is the science considering the spaces and surfaces of full and solid figures with a wise intuition of the mind. **MUSICA** is the science discerning by the light of reason the harmony of all things that exist, whether in motion or in a knowable state, and in natural proportions. **ASTROLOGIA** is the science investigating the spaces, motions, and returns of the celestial bodies at fixed times. These are the general "places" <note>or "topics"</note> of the liberal arts; they are contained within these boundaries, inside of which are innumerable others.

30. The seven *periochæ* <note>Greek: "circumstances"</note>, that is, the circumstances, are: who, what, why, how, when, where, and by what resources. *Student:* By these reasons I am forced to admit that "place" exists nowhere except in the mind. For if every definition is in a science, and every science is in the mind; then necessarily every place, because it is a definition, will be nowhere else but in the mind.

31. *Master:* You perceive correctly. *Student:* What then should be said of those who say that the dwellings of men and other animals are "places"? Similarly, they judge this common air and the earth to be the place of all who dwell in them? They say water is the place of fish, the ether the place of planets, and they think the celestial sphere is the place of the stars. *Master:* Nothing else, except that they should either be persuaded otherwise, if they are capable of learning and being taught; or they should be dismissed entirely, if they are argumentative. For true reason mocks those who say such things. For if place is one thing and a body is another, it follows that a place is not a body. Now, the Air is the fourth part of this corporeal and visible world; therefore, it is not a place. For it is well established that this visible world is composed of four elements, as if of four general parts. And it is like a certain body, joined together by its parts. From these universal parts, the specific and most particular bodies of all animals, trees, and herbs are composed—coming together in a wonderful and ineffable mixture—and into them they return again at the time of their dissolution.

32. Indeed, this whole world apparent to the senses revolves in constant motion around its axis; I mean around the Earth, around which, as if around a center, the other three elements—namely Water, Air, and Fire—revolve in unceasing rotation. And so, without any intermission at an invisible boundary, the universal bodies (I mean the four elements) coming together into one another, fashion the particular bodies of individual things. These, when dissolved, return again from their specific properties into the universalities; while the particular and natural essence of individual things remains always unchangeable like a center, which can neither be moved, nor increased, nor diminished. For accidents <note>non-essential qualities like color or size</note> are in motion, not essences. Nor are the accidents themselves in motion or subject to increase or decrease, but rather the participation in them by an essence suffers such changes. Otherwise, by true reason, they do not "exist"; for every nature, whether of essences or of their accidents, is immutable. But the participation of essences by accidents, or of accidents by essences, is always in motion, as participation can indeed be begun, increased, and diminished, until this world reaches the end of its stability in all things. After this, neither essence, nor accident, nor their mutual participation will suffer any motion; for all things will be one and the same immobile thing, when all things shall return into their immutable reasons. Concerning this return, we must discuss elsewhere.

33. By what reason only the center of the world, that is the Earth, always stands still, while the other elements revolve around it in eternal motion, requires no small consideration. For we have recognized the opinions of both secular philosophers and Catholic Fathers on this question. Indeed Plato, the greatest of those philosophizing about the world, asserts in his *Timaeus* by many reasons that this visible world, *as if a certain great animal, is composed of body and soul*; the body of this animal is *joined together* by the four best-known elements, and by the kinds and diverse bodies composed from them; but its Soul is the general life, because it quickens and moves all things that are in motion or at rest. Hence the Poet <note>Virgil, Aeneid VI</note> says:
> *In the beginning, the spirit within nourishes the heavens and the earth,*
> *the liquid plains, the shining globe of the Moon, and the Titan stars.*

<meta>This page continues the philosophical dialogue regarding the nature of "place" and "time" as conceptual boundaries rather than physical containers. The previous discussion likely established that all created things have a beginning and a limit.</meta>

# BOOK ONE.

That is to say, the measure of all things that have been created naturally precedes their condition in terms of reason. This mode and measure of each individual thing is called, and indeed is, its "place." Similarly, the principle of birth and the beginning is seen to precede, by reason, everything that is born and begins. Therefore, everything that was not and now is, received its being from the beginning of time. God alone, therefore, is infinite; all other things are bounded by "where" and "when"—that is, by place and time. This is not because place and time are not among the number of things created by God, but because all things in the universe preceded the spans of time by the sole reason of their creation <note>The author argues that the logical "definition" or "plan" of a thing exists before its physical manifestation in time.</note>. For of necessity, those things which contain are understood before those things which are contained: just as a cause precedes its effect, fire precedes a blaze, the voice precedes the word, and other similar examples. And through this, we do not estimate any other blessedness to be promised to those who are worthy, nor any other end for this world, than that all who are to receive the glory of <term>theoseos</term> <note>original: "theoseos" (Greek: θέωσις). This refers to the process of "deification" or "divinization," where the soul becomes united with God.</note>, that is, of deification, shall ascend beyond places and times. For those who are confined by place are finite. But eternal blessedness is infinite. Therefore, those who partake in eternal and infinite blessedness will be circumscribed neither by place nor by time.

<insert>
<image-desc>Round library stamp: NATIONAL LIBRARY OF ROME VITTORIO EMANUELE</image-desc>
</insert>

42. For what is written of Melchizedek alone—that he lacked father and mother, and had no beginning of days through birth into essence, nor an end of time—I judge should be understood universally of all who will be partakers of future blessedness. Indeed, all who are to return to the eternal reasons—which have neither a beginning of time through birth in a place or time, nor an end through dissolution, nor are circumscribed by any local position, so that these reasons alone are in them and nothing else—will certainly lack every local and temporal boundary. For since the Cause of all things lacks all circumscription and is infinite, those who are infinite will adhere to the Infinite forever. For God alone will appear in them when they transcend the boundaries of their own nature: not so that their nature perishes in them, but so that He who alone truly exists appears alone in them. And this is what it means to transcend nature: for the nature not to appear. Just as the air, as we have often said, when full of light does not appear, since the light alone reigns. Therefore, what is understood regarding the general place and general time of the universal creation must necessarily be understood regarding the specific places and times of its parts, from the highest to the lowest. Moreover, general place and general time precede, according to the intellect, all things that are within them. Therefore, the knowledge of specific and proper places and times precedes those things which are specifically and properly understood within them. And by this it is concluded that place is nothing other than the natural definition, mode, and position of each creature, whether general or specific: just as time is nothing other than the beginning of things through birth from non-being into being, and the certain dimensions of the motion of changeable things, until the stable end comes, in which all things shall stand immutably. **D.** <note>D. stands for "Discipulus" (The Student).</note> The intent of this reasoning is becoming a little clearer, I think. For it seeks nothing else, as far as I am given to understand, than to persuade us that place is nothing but the natural definition of each creature, within which the whole is contained, and beyond which it is in no way extended. And by this it is given to be understood that whether one says "place," or "end," or "boundary," or "definition," or "circumscription," they signify one and the same thing: namely, the circuit of a finite nature.

43. And although many kinds of definitions may seem to exist to some, that alone is truly to be called a definition which the Greeks call <term>horos</term> <note>original: "ὅρος". A boundary, limit, or defining term.</note>, and which our people are accustomed to call "essential." For others are either enumerations of its intelligible parts, or certain arguments from the outside through accidents, or various kinds of opinions. But the <term>horos</term> alone receives for defining only that which completes and perfects the perfection of the nature it defines. *For a definition, as Augustine says, holds nothing more and nothing less than that which has been taken up to be explained.* Otherwise, it is entirely faulty. **M.** <note>M. stands for "Magister" (The Master/Teacher).</note> You see it clearly: for the aforementioned reasoning seeks to persuade nothing other than what you have said. **D.** But I wonder, and do not clearly perceive, how the definition of any essence is said to be not within it, but outside it—that is, how it can be called neither its whole nor its part. **M.** Be more attentive, then, so that you may know this plainly as well. **D.** As much as the inner light allows. **M.** Tell me, please, since there are two genera of all things—for everything that is said to "be" is either visible and perceived by the bodily senses (or can be perceived), or it is invisible and considered by the gaze of the intelligence (or its consideration is possible either through itself or through something adhering to it)—in which of the aforementioned genera do you judge "definition" to be?

<meta>catchword: 44. D. Ri-</meta>

It concludes that in the state of deification (theosis), the soul transcends these limits to adhere to the infinite nature of God.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between a Teacher (Magister) and a Disciple (Discipulus) regarding the nature of "place" and "definition." It follows the argument that place is not a physical container but a mental boundary.</meta>

# ON THE DIVISION OF NATURE

44. **D.** That would be ridiculous. For who among the wise would rightly place a "location"—whether it be a boundary, a definition, or any kind of circumscription <note>original: "circumscriptionis," meaning the act of drawing a limit around something</note> by which the substance of each thing is encompassed—among those things that fall under the bodily senses? For one sees that the limits of a line, or of a triangle, or of any plane or solid figure, are incorporeal <note>non-physical or spiritual in nature</note>. Indeed, the point from which a line begins and in which it ends is neither the line itself nor a part of it, but the boundary of the line. And because of this, its "place" is not perceived by the sense, but is grasped by reason alone. For a "sensible point" <note>a physical dot</note> is a part of a line, but it is not the line's true beginning or end. Similarly, the line itself, when consulted by reason, is incorporeal, and is the beginning of a surface. Likewise, the surface is incorporeal, and is the end of a line, but the beginning of Solidity. Solidity, too, is incorporeal, and is the end of all perfection <note>in the sense of a completed geometric form</note>. For whatever the bodily sense touches in these things—whether it be a visible point, line, surface, or solidity—these are surely only the *figures* of incorporeal things, not their true substance, which is incorporeal. And in the same way, regarding natural bodies—whether they are sensible through the tempering of the elements by which they subsist, or whether they escape mortal sight by their own subtlety—the boundaries of nature are perceived by the intellect alone. For the Form which contains all bodily matter is incorporeal. Indeed, matter itself, if one looks closely, is joined together from incorporeal qualities. **M.** Therefore, do you judge that these things are concluded to be in the category of the invisible, which we have called the "places" of circumscribed things? **D.** I do indeed judge so, and I see nothing more certain.

45. **M.** You judge rightly. But because the category of the invisible allows itself to be divided again into many species—for some invisible things are those which understand and are understood, while others neither are understood nor understand—in which species of these do you think these "definitions" should be numbered? **D.** Surely in that which understands and is understood. For the act of defining is an action of a reasoning and understanding nature. **M.** It seems it could not be otherwise. For no nature that does not understand itself to exist can define either its equal or what is inferior to itself. For how can it know what is above itself, since it cannot grasp the knowledge of it? **D.** Therefore, the skill of definition belongs only to the intellectual nature which is established in humans and angels. But whether an angel or a human can define themselves, or a human an angel, or an angel a human, is no small question; I desire to know what you think of it. **M.** It seems to me that they can define neither themselves nor each other. For if a human defines himself or an angel, he is greater than himself and the angel. For that which defines is greater than that which is defined. The same reasoning applies to the angel. Therefore, I judge that these can be defined only by Him alone who created them in His own image. **D.** It seems to me to follow from this reasoning that no other natures are defined by the rational mind except those inferior to it, whether they be visible or invisible. **M.** Whoever says this does not wander from the truth. And therefore, where the definitions of those things which are defined are, there surely are also the "places" of those things which are circumscribed. For it has been established by the aforementioned reasons that "place" is definition, and definition is "place." **D.** It is established indeed. **M.** And yet the definitions of bodies, and of things lacking reason, are nowhere else but in the rational soul. In the soul, therefore, are also the "places" of all things that are locally comprehended. But if the rational soul is incorporeal—which no wise man doubts—it is necessarily manifest that whatever is understood within it is incorporeal. And "place" is understood in the mind, as was previously granted. It is therefore incorporeal. **D.** I see this is rightly concluded. For whether the angelic nature contains the definitions of things inferior to itself, as Augustine <note>St. Augustine of Hippo, a major influence on Eriugena</note> seems to prefer—for angels are believed to administer things inferior to them—or whether it always directs itself toward things superior to it, that is, toward the eternal causes of things: it does not contradict this reasoning. For it does not stray from the truth, as I see it, if one judges that the human mind, though still weighed down by earthly fantasies, can comprehend the created causes of natures inferior to itself, if it lives purely. But the angelic mind seeks the eternal reasons <note>the divine blueprints or "Logos"</note> of all things; and, always moved by love toward this, it draws human nature toward it. **M.** You see rightly. Do you see, then, that "place" is nothing other than the action of one who understands and comprehends, by the power of intelligence, those things which he can comprehend, whether they be sensible things or things grasped by the intellect? And if this is so, then that which is defined is one thing, and its definition is another. **D.** I see they are different. But the intellect that understands itself, when it defines itself, seems to be its own place. **M.** One might not say this absurdly—if any intellect could understand itself, except for God, who is called the Intellect of all things. If, however, every intellect besides God is circumscribed not by itself, but by what is superior to it, no intellect [can define] its own...

<summary>The Teacher and Disciple argue that "place" is not a physical container but a mental definition or boundary existing within a rational soul. They conclude that only God can truly define the highest intellectual beings (humans and angels), as the definer must be greater than the defined.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between a Master (M) and a Disciple (D) regarding the nature of place, essence, and the soul. It follows the previous discussion on whether place is a physical container or a mental definition.</meta>

# BOOK ONE.

D
<meta>catchword: therefore</meta>

there will be a place for itself, but it will be situated within that which is superior to it. And did we not agree among ourselves a little earlier that it could not be otherwise? **D.** I think this must be discussed more extensively elsewhere.

46. But now I would like to know whether the nature of the defining mind—that is, everything understood by it in the capacity of gathering knowledge—is one thing, and the place itself, or the definition of the thing placed or defined, is another. **M.** I see that this is not unworthy of inquiry, for many doubt this. But when we see that the liberal arts established in the soul are one thing, and the soul itself, which is a certain subject of the arts, is another; and yet the arts seem to be, as it were, inseparable and natural accidents of the soul: what prevents us from placing the discipline of defining among the arts, joining it to Dialectic <note>The art of logical discussion and investigation of truth</note>? It is the property of Dialectic to divide, join, and discern the natures of all things that can be understood, and to distribute proper places to each; and for this reason, it is accustomed to be called by the wise the "true contemplation of things." For since in every rational and intellectual nature three inseparable and always incorruptible things are considered—I mean <term>ousia</term> <note>original: "οὐσία" - Essence</note>, <term>dynamis</term> <note>original: "δύναμιν" - Power or Potential</note>, and <term>energeia</term> <note>original: "ἐνέργειάν" - Operation or Activity</note>, that is, essence, virtue, and operation (for these, by the testimony of Saint Dionysius <note>Dionysius the Areopagite, a highly influential mystical theologian</note>, adhere inseparably to one another, and are as they are; they can neither be increased nor diminished since they are immortal and immutable)—does it not seem likely to you, and consistent with certain reason, that all liberal disciplines are to be valued in that part which is called <term>energeia</term>, that is, the operation of the soul? Indeed, it has been truly sought and found by the Philosophers that the arts are eternal, and always adhere immutably to the soul, so that they do not seem to be like certain accidents of it, but natural virtues and actions in no way departing from it, nor able to depart, nor coming from elsewhere, but naturally implanted in it. Thus it is ambiguous whether they themselves provide eternity to the soul, since they are eternal and always adhere to it so that it may be eternal; or whether, by reason of the subject, which is the soul, eternity is administered to the arts—for the <term>ousia</term> of the soul and its virtue and action are eternal—or whether they so cohere to one another, while all are eternal, that they cannot be segregated from each other.

47. **D.** I do not know who would dare to resist this reasoning, since it is true. For whichever of these one affirms, it does not resist reason. Yet the last one posed by you shines out as more likely than the others. But to return to the same point: it is not entirely clear to me how <term>ousia</term> <note>Essence</note>, whether in genera, or in species, or in individuals <note>original: "atomis" - the indivisible units or individuals</note>, can be defined; since in the previously mentioned reasons of this little book it was concluded that it is comprehensible to no bodily sense and no intellect. **M.** No one can define <term>ousia</term> through itself and say *what* it is. However, it can only be defined from those things which adhere inseparably to it and without which it cannot exist; I mean from place and time (for every <term>ousia</term> created from nothing is local and temporal: local indeed, because it exists in some way, since it is not infinite; and temporal, since it begins to be what it was not). Therefore, <term>ousia</term> is in no way defined as to *what* it is, but it is defined *that* it is. For from place, as we have said, and time, and other accidents which are understood to be either within it or outside it, it is only given not what it is, but that it is. And one might not incongruously say this generally of every <term>ousia</term>, whether most general, or most specific, or intermediate. For even the Cause of all things, which is God, is known to exist only from those things which have been created by Him. But by no argument of creatures can we understand what He is; and therefore only this definition is predicated of God: *that* He is—He who is more than "being." **D.** To this reasoning also, no one of sound understanding, as I think, will stand in the way.

48. **M.** Now, therefore, I perceive more clearly than the sun that those people are to be laughed at—indeed, even pitied, and through this recalled to the true discernment of things, if they wish; or utterly abandoned, if they prefer to follow their custom which is most hostile to the truth—who think that the parts of this visible world are the natural places of the other bodies situated within them. For to speak, for example, of my own body (for it is most impudent to think the soul is contained by the bodily spaces of this world): if this air is its place, it follows that a fourth part of itself is its own place. For it is known to all that every visible body consists of four parts; namely, from fire, air, water, and earth. Nothing is closer to irrationality than to think a whole body is located by a part of itself. For the whole is rightly estimated to encompass all its parts, but a part does not encompass the whole. Likewise, if I say my body is in this air as if in its own place, it follows that it can have no certain place there. For this air is always revolving around the earth: and through this, it is necessary for a body situated in it to have innumerable places in one and the same space of time: which is conceded by no reason. Indeed, by the aforementioned reasons, it has been persuaded that place is in a state of rest, and is varied by no motion. Just as therefore

<summary>The Master argues that the liberal arts are eternal operations of the soul and that "essence" (ousia) cannot be defined by what it is, but only that it exists through the categories of place and time. He further refutes the idea that physical elements like air are the "place" of a body, as a part cannot contain the whole.</summary>

<keywords>essence, ousia, power, operation, soul, liberal arts, dialectic, Dionysius the Areopagite, definition of God, four elements, nature of place</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between the Master and the Disciple regarding the nature of space, time, and matter. The Master is currently arguing that physical elements like air or earth are not "place" itself, but rather the material components of bodies.</meta>

# ON THE DIVISION OF NATURE

Therefore, one who stands, sits, or swims in a river cannot hold onto that part of the river so as to say they possess a fixed "place" in the river; for it is certain that the water passes by without any pause. In the same way, no one should say that the place of their body is this air, which is mobile without any interruption and does not stand still for a single moment of time. 

If, however, someone objects to this reasoning by citing the earth—since it always stands still—claiming it is by right called the "place" of bodies, let them see that the earth is similarly the *matter* of bodies, not their *place*. Who, using reason, would dare to say that the matter of bodies is the place of those same bodies, especially since matter, when considered by reason in itself, is neither in motion nor at rest? It is not in motion because it has not yet begun to be contained by a certain form. For matter is moved through form; without form, it is immobile, as the Greeks maintain <note>original: "ut Graeci volunt." Eriugena frequently draws on Greek Patristic sources like Maximus the Confessor.</note>. For where shall that be moved which is not yet constrained by any certain place or certain time? Nor is it at rest, because it does not yet possess the end of its perfection. Rest, indeed, is the end of motion. How can that which has not yet begun to move become at rest? 

How then can the matter of a body be the place of the body made from it, when matter itself is not circumscribed in itself by any certain place, or mode, or form, and is defined by no certain reason except through negation? For it is denied to be any of the things that *are*, since all things that have been created are believed to be established out of it. 

Likewise, if the parts of this visible world are the places of our bodies or others, they cannot be our places forever. For when the body of a certain animal is dissolved, and its parts return separately to their natural seats from which they were taken, its place—for example, air, or water, or earth, or fire—will not exist; rather, the individual parts of the one body are so mingled with the individual elements natural to them that they are one *with* them, not *in* them as something inside something else. For what is returned to the air will *be* air, and will not be stationed as if in some "place" of the air. This is not so that a confusion of bodies occurs; but in a wonderful mode of nature, each will have its own proper part in the individual elements, the whole through the whole, not part in part: so that at the time of the resurrection, no one receives anything except what is their own, just as the light of many lamps is joined together so that there is no mixing in it, and no separation. For while it appears to be one and the same light, each lamp nevertheless possesses its own proper light, not mixed with the light of the other: but wonderfully they become wholes in wholes, and constitute one light. 

Therefore, air is one thing, and its place is another. I judge that the same must be understood concerning the other elements and the parts of dissolved bodies returned to them. And if this is so, it will necessarily be granted either that these general parts of the world are not the places of the bodies poured through them and composed of them; or that the bodies themselves have no certain place; or that they are entirely void of all place—which the nature of things and the true contemplation of it does not allow us to concede. For no creature (whether it be corporeal or incorporeal) can lack its own certain and immutable place and its certain spaces and boundaries of time. And therefore, as we have often said, these two—namely place and time—are called by philosophers <gloss>necessary things</gloss> <note>original: "ἀναγκαῖα" (anankaia). These are the "necessary" conditions for existence.</note>: that is, things without which [nothing can exist]. For without these, no creature beginning its generation and subsisting in any way can exist. 

And to use a very close argument: if the place of a body is everything that surrounds it, then color would be the place of a body. For there is no visible body that is not surrounded by the light of color. But if the color of a colored body is its place, it will necessarily be granted that a *quality* is the place of a body. But who is so overwhelmed by such dire folly as to admit that a quality is the place of a body? But if the color of a body is an incorporeal quality, and is surrounded on all sides by the body, yet it does not seem to any of the wise to be its place. By this same reason, although this air or any other element of the world surrounds the bodies placed within them, it is not conceded that they can in any way be their places.

49. **Disciple.** This has been discussed enough. But against those who do not think a body is one thing and the essence of the body another—being so seduced that they do not doubt that substance itself is corporeal, visible, and touchable—I think some things should be said briefly. For many, and almost all, are weary with such error, being ignorant of the natural differences of things.

**Master.** To fight against folly is nothing if not laborious. For it refuses to be conquered by any authority and is persuaded by no reason. But since the folly of men is not equal, nor are their minds obscured by the same darkness of error, I see that a few arguments should be used against them.

**Disciple.** They should certainly be used. For if it profits them, it will be a gain; if not, we shall make ourselves more certain of such distinctions of natures through the study of the discussion.

**Master.** Consider intently, then, these few dialectical collections out of many. Every body is composed of matter and form...

<summary>The Master argues that physical elements like air and earth are the material components of bodies rather than their "place," asserting that place and time are incorporeal necessities for all created existence. He begins a new refutation against the common error of confusing visible, tangible bodies with their underlying spiritual substance.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical dialogue between a Master (M) and a Disciple (D) regarding the nature of "Ousia" (Essence) and its distinction from the material body. The previous discussion likely established the definition of the soul or the spiritual nature of man.</meta>

<page-num>34</page-num>
->BOOK ONE<-

...is placed, when it can be dissolved, it is corruptible <note>In this context, "corruptible" means capable of decaying or being broken down into parts.</note>. However, the mortal body is composed of matter and form: therefore, it is corruptible. Likewise, every <term>ousia</term> <note>original: "ουσια"; the fundamental essence or "being" of a thing</note> is simple, and admits no composition from matter and form, since it is one and inseparable. Therefore, no <term>ousia</term> is rationally granted to be a mortal body. This was said because every <term>ousia</term>, although it is understood to be composed of essence and essential difference—for no incorporeal essence can lack this composition, since even the divine <term>ousia</term> itself, which is believed to be not only simple but more than simple, admits an essential difference. For in it, there is unbegotten and begotten substance: yet that composition itself, which is known by reason alone and is proven to be made by no act or operation, must rationally be judged as simplicity. But so that you may more firmly know that <term>ousia</term> is incorruptible, read the book of Saint Dionysius the Areopagite *On the Divine Names*; in that place where he disputes concerning the nature of demons and their malice, showing that no essence, neither theirs nor that of others, can be corrupted: and you will find him disputing most subtly that none of the things that exist, insofar as they are essence and nature, can be corrupted in any way.

50. For these three things in every creature, whether corporeal or incorporeal, as he himself teaches with the most certain arguments, are incorruptible and inseparable: <term>ousia</term>, <term>dynamis</term>, and <term>energeia</term> <note>original: "ουσια, δυναμις, ενεργεια"</note>, that is, essence, power, and natural operation. **D.** I ask for an example of these three. **M.** There is no nature, whether rational or intellectual, that is ignorant of its own existence, even though it may not know *what* it is. **D.** I do not doubt this. **M.** Therefore, when I say, "I understand that I am," do I not signify in this one word, which is "I understand," three things inseparable from one another? For I demonstrate that I am, that I am able to understand that I am, and that I do understand that I am. Do you not see that by one word my <term>ousia</term>, my power, and my action are signified? For I would not understand if I did not exist; nor would I understand if I lacked the power of intelligence; nor does any power lie silent in me, but it breaks forth into the operation of understanding. **D.** True and plausible. **M.** Is it not therefore necessary that those who say the material body is <term>ousia</term> must either confess that their body is not composed of form and matter but is an incorruptible <term>ousia</term>; or, compelled by truth, concede that their body is corruptible and material, and through this, is not <term>ousia</term>? **D.** It is certainly necessary. But you seem to me to be saying, not that every body is universally not <term>ousia</term>, but only denying it to every body compacted of matter and form. **M.** Look at these things intently: do not think I have spoken only of a certain species of bodies, but universally of every body, although I spoke specifically of the body composed of form and matter for the utility of the present question, against those who say their mortal and transitory bodies are nothing else but their <term>ousia</term>; and that their <term>ousia</term> is nothing else but their material body, composed of diverse species—namely form and matter—and various accidents.

51. But so that you may clearly know that universally no body is <term>ousia</term>, accept this type of argument. **D.** I will accept it, if first I see it necessary for a certain formula of the aforementioned argument to be made. For the previous reasoning seems more like an argument from the contrary than the image of a dialectical syllogism. **M.** Let the major premise be made thus: whether <term>ousia</term> is a corruptible body. Every <term>ousia</term> is incorruptible. Everything incorruptible is not a material body. Therefore, every <term>ousia</term> is not a material body. And conversely, therefore every material body is not <term>ousia</term>. Likewise: Every body that is composed of form and matter is not simple. But every <term>ousia</term> is simple. Therefore, no body composed of form and matter is <term>ousia</term>. Likewise, the <term>ousia</term> of all men is one and the same. For all participate in one essence, and because of this, since it is common to all, it belongs properly to no one. But a body is not common to all men. For each individual possesses his own proper body, but not a private <term>ousia</term>. Therefore, [essence] is common, and the body is not common. But <term>ousia</term> is common; therefore, it is not a body. The same reasoning shines clearly in other animals. **D.** This formula is suitable. Return, I pray, to the promised universal type of argument by which it is concluded that no body is <term>ousia</term>. **M.** Everything that is encompassed by length, breadth, and height, since it is enclosed by diverse spaces, is a body. But that which receives none of the aforementioned spaces, since it is one and simple, and can receive no movement of its nature through spaces, is necessarily incorporeal. But <term>ousia</term> is extended neither by length, nor breadth, nor height, and since it remains individual in the simplicity of its nature, it is incorporeal.

D 2

<summary>The Master uses dialectical logic to prove that "Ousia" (Essence) is an incorruptible, simple, and incorporeal reality, distinct from the material body which is composed of matter and form. He cites Dionysius the Areopagite and uses the triad of essence, power, and operation to illustrate the soul's nature.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between the Teacher and the Disciple regarding the fundamental divisions of nature, specifically focusing on the distinction between essence and body.</meta>

# ON THE DIVISION OF NATURE.

Therefore, since no **essence** <note>original: *οὐσία* (ousia); the fundamental being or substance of a thing</note> lacks space, it is not corporeal; just as no body, since it is extended by spaces, is an **essence**.

**D.** I wish to see this represented in a certain logical formula.

**M.** Let there be, then, a formula of a conditional syllogism: the major premise is whether **essence** is a body. If **essence** is a body, it receives the spaces of length, breadth, and height; but **essence** does not receive length, breadth, and height; therefore, it is not a body. But if you desire to hear an **enthymeme** <note>original: *ἐνθυμήματι*; a "thought-argument" or a syllogism based on common mental conceptions</note>—that is, a syllogism of common mental conception which holds primacy over all conclusions because it is drawn from things that cannot exist simultaneously—take a formula of this kind: Is it both **essence** and not incorporeal? It is **essence**. Therefore, it is incorporeal. For it cannot be at the same time both **essence** and not incorporeal. Likewise: Is it both **essence** and a body? It is **essence**. Therefore, it is not a body. Likewise: Is it both **essence** and not corporeal? It is **essence**. Therefore, it is incorporeal.

This is the greatest argument by which it is discerned that a body is one thing and **essence** is another. For **essence** is divided into genera and species; but a body is separated into parts like a certain whole. Furthermore, a body is not "the whole" in its parts. For in the head, or the hands, or the feet, the whole body is not contained. And it is greater in all its parts together, but smaller in individual parts when not together. On the contrary, however, the whole **essence** is in each of its individual forms (and species); it is neither greater in all of them collected together, nor smaller in individuals that differ from one another. For it is no larger in the most general genus than in the most specific species; nor smaller in the most specific species than in the most general genus.

To use examples: **essence** is not greater in all men than in one man, nor smaller in one man than in all men. It is not greater in the genus in which all species of animals are one, than in a man alone, or an ox, or a horse. Nor is it smaller in these individual species than in all of them together.

Likewise, a body can be cut into parts so that its "wholeness" perishes. For example, when it is dissolved into the very elements from whose coming together the added form is made, the whole perishes. For where the parts are not together, nor contained by their own form, the whole cannot exist together in any thing in act and deed; although they may seem to be together in the thought of one considering the natures of things. But it is one thing to be "together" in the rational consideration of nature—which always gathers all things together in the intellect and inseparably grasps them into a university <note>meaning the "totality" or "whole" of the universe</note>—and another thing which is performed by the operation of an agent or the suffering of a patient visibly in the separation or collection of sensible parts.

Indeed, the ratio of all numbers is unshaken in unity; it can neither be increased nor diminished. But corporeal or "imaginary" numbers can be increased to infinity and diminished until they cease to be. But **essence**, although it is divided by reason alone into its genera and numbers of species, nevertheless remains individual <note>undivided</note> by its own natural power, and is segregated by no visible act or operation. For the whole exists together and always in its subdivisions eternally and unchangeably, and all its subdivisions are together and always one inseparable thing within itself. And therefore, even if the body—which is nothing other than the quantity of **essence**, or to speak more truly, not quantity but a "quantified thing"—is segregated into diverse parts by act and operation or by the suffering of its own certain fragility; the thing itself, that is, the **essence** of which the body is the "quantified thing," endures in its own immortal and inseparable natural power.

**52.** I added this, however—that it is more correct to call the body a "quantified thing" <note>original: *quantum*</note> rather than "quantity"—because those accidents which are called "natural," when they are perceived naturally in themselves, are incorporeal and invisible. They are considered around the **essence** itself, or within it by the gaze of reason alone, and are like certain causes of their effects; just as quantity itself and quality are the causes of the "quantified" and the "qualified," and the other kinds of accidents about which I think we have said enough. Though they are invisible, they beget their visible effects. Therefore, the body is not the quantity of the **essence**, but the "quantified thing"; just as visible color which is sensed around a body is not the quality of the **essence**, but the "qualified thing" <note>original: *quale*</note> established in the "quantified." For it pleased me to insert into our little discussions the opinion of the Holy Father Augustine from the book he wrote on the *Categories* of Aristotle.

**53.** *Having described* **essence**, *he says, since it could not be defined for those reasons I mentioned above, the necessary order demanded a definition of the accidents: the first of which is quantity. And not without cause. For when we see something, it is necessary to estimate how much it is. But how much it is cannot be found unless it is collected by applying a measure. If, therefore, omitting the breadth, someone wishes to measure the length alone, the length without breadth subjected to measure is called a* **line** <note>original: *gramme* (Greek: γραμμή)</note>; *not because there is any length that lacks breadth, but because someone [measures] only...*

<column-break/>

<summary>This page argues that essence (ousia) is fundamentally different from body because essence is indivisible and present as a whole in all its parts, whereas a body is divisible and its parts do not contain the whole. It also distinguishes between abstract "quantity" (incorporeal) and the "quantified" body (corporeal).</summary>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical dialogue between a Master (M.) and a Disciple (D.) regarding the distinction between geometric and natural bodies, specifically focusing on the relationship between matter, form, and quantity.</meta>

# BOOK ONE

<note>original: "D 3" - This is a signature mark used by early printers to keep the pages in order.</note>

...measuring length is said to be measured by a <term>line</term> <note>original: "γραμμῇ" (grammē)</note>. However, when breadth is measured along with length, it is called a *Surface* <note>original: "Epiphania" - a Latinized Greek term for the outward appearance or surface of a thing</note>. But if height is also joined to the measurement, these things together complete a body. Yet we do not understand this in the same way we are accustomed to understand a natural body, lest we seem to return to <term>matter</term> <note>original: "ὕλην" (hylēn) - the raw material of the universe</note>. This is said so that we may know these individual rules are established in geometric bodies, in which these three dimensions can be separately distinguished. In natural bodies, however, they cohere inseparably; in these, quantity is separated from <term>matter</term> <note>original: "ὕλῃ" (hylē)</note> by the intellect alone. For when someone is seen to dispute concerning quantity, it is thought he is confusing it as if he were saying something about <term>matter</term> <note>original: "ὕλῃ"</note>. Do you see what the master of highest authority declares? He says that when someone is seen to dispute about quantity—that is, about the spaces by which a thing is made—he is thought by those who estimate <term>matter</term> <note>original: "ὕλην"</note> to be nothing other than the body they see, to be speaking of that very <term>matter</term>. Therefore, if geometric bodies, which we contemplate by the intuition of the mind alone and strive to fashion only by the imaginations of memory, subsist in some <term>matter</term> <note>original: "ὕλῃ"</note>, they are surely natural things, and there is no difference between geometric and natural bodies. But as it is, since we consider geometric bodies by the mind alone, and they subsist in no <term>matter</term> <note>original: "ὕλῃ"</note>, they are rightly called "imaginary" <note>original: "phantastica"</note>. Natural bodies, on the other hand, are natural because they subsist in their natural *beings* <note>original: "ousiis"</note>, that is, essences, without which they cannot exist; and therefore they are real. Otherwise, they would not be thought of in natural things, but by reason alone. Surely it is given to be understood that a body is one thing and <term>matter</term> <note>original: "ὕλην"</note> is another, since a body sometimes lacks <term>matter</term> <note>original: "ὕλῃ"</note>, and sometimes adheres to <term>essence</term> <note>original: "οὐσίᾳ" (ousia)</note> so that it may be real—without which it cannot become real, but is only figured by a certain imagination. But <term>matter</term> <note>original: "ὕλην"</note> in no way needs a body in order to be, since it subsists through itself. I believe enough has been said of these things. **M.** Plainly enough; but it remains, as I see, for you to discourse briefly on that matter and form from which you assert material things are composed. For I think it should not be passed over, since it is not clear enough to me whether the form that is subject to a genus is the same as the form that is joined to matter so that it may be a body.

53. Some forms are understood in <term>matter</term> <note>original: "ὕλῃ"</note>, others in quality; but those which are in <term>matter</term> <note>original: "ὕλῃ"</note> are the substantial species of a genus. For the genus is predicated of them because it subsists in them. For the genus, as we have often said, is in individual forms, just as individual forms are one in their genus. And all these—that is, genera and forms—flow from one source, <term>essence</term> <note>original: "οὐσίᾳ"</note>, and return into it by a natural circuit. But the forms which are attributed to quality are properly called "forms" in natural bodies, but in geometric bodies they are called "figures." For every geometric body is contained solely by spaces and figure, but by no substance. Indeed, every imaginary body is completed by three universal spaces: namely length, breadth, and height. However, not all geometric bodies are universally circumscribed by one figure. For some of them rise from a triangular figure, others from a square, others from a pentagon, and others from polygons into infinity; others grow from a circular surface. And therefore, just as the number of lines progresses starting from three, so the manifold order of figures and surfaces is varied. Thus, the number of spaces and lines in geometric bodies is assigned to quantity. But the order and position of sides and angles, and the relationships of surfaces, is proper to quality and is called a geometric form or, properly, a figure. While in natural bodies, whether they are segregated by the numbers and distinctions of members or natural divisions, or are naturally joined to one another, no one would deny that this is proper to quantity. But the order and position of natural parts or members is referred to quality, and is properly called "form." For we say the form of a man is erect, facing upward; but that of other animals is prone, facing downward. Hence those are called "deformed" who lack a fitting harmony of members, or are deprived of the beauty of color, which arises in bodies from the fiery quality that is color. For "color" <note>original: "color"</note> is so named as if it were "heat" <note>original: "calor"</note> by the change of one letter. And "form" <note>original: "forma"</note> is named from "warm" <note>original: "formo"</note>, that is, hot, by changing the syllable *mam* into *ma*. Indeed, the ancients used to say *formum* for hot. Whence also "tongs" <note>original: "forcipes"</note> are named from "taking what is hot" <note>original: "formum capientes"</note>. We are also accustomed to call those "enormous" <note>original: "innormes"</note> who exceed the natural rule of members, as if they were "without a norm" <note>original: "sine norma"</note>, that is, a rule. Do you not see that this consists not in the number or magnitude of members, but in the position of the parts of the body and the light of color? Or does it seem otherwise to you? **D.** Not as I think; but I look to know what such a difference achieves regarding the proposed question. **M.** Has it not been granted between us by the aforesaid reasons that a body is one thing and <term>matter</term> <note>original: "ὕλην"</note> is another? **D.** Sufficiently, and most greatly.

<meta>This page continues a dialogue between a Master (M) and a Disciple (D) regarding the metaphysical origins of the physical world, specifically how incorporeal categories like substance and quality combine to create visible bodies. This is a central theme in John Scottus Eriugena's "Periphyseon" (On the Division of Nature).</meta>

# ON THE DIVISION OF NATURE

<margin>O. 9</margin>
§ 4. **M.** Just as by natural distinction, quantity is separated from the intellect by <term>ousia</term> <note>original: "ousia"; the Greek term for "substance" or "essence," used here to denote the underlying reality of a thing.</note>, even though they cling to one another; so that substance is the subject of quantity and of the "how much," while quantity itself or the "how much" are accidents of the substance; is it not clearer than light that there is one form which is in the substance itself—not as an accident, but as it is perceived in relation to it—and another form which, joined to quantity from quality, creates a perfected body? **D.** I see now where you are heading. **M.** Do you think I mean that the essential form joined to matter creates a natural body? **D.** Not even that; indeed, I see that you do not. **M.** Tell me, I pray, how so. **D.** From the aforementioned difference of forms—namely, those of substance and those of quality—you seem to be persuading me of nothing else but that the form which is a species of quality, when added, perfects the body for which substance serves as the foundation. For these three are observed in all natural bodies: substance, quantity, and quality. But substance is always perceived by the intellect alone, for it appears visibly in nothing. Quantity and quality, however, are so invisibly within the substance that they break out visibly into the "how much" and the "of what kind" when, joined together, they compose a sensible body. For if a geometric body, beneath which no substance lies, is proven by reason to consist only of the quantity of spaces and lines and the form of quality which is called "figure"; what hindrance is there for us to say that a body of nature—under which the power of substance stands as a foundation for its permanence—is perfected by that form which is added from quality to the quantity assumed from matter, insofar as it is able to remain a "how much"? For I do not think you are suggesting anything else than that we should recognize that the matter of bodies is made from the meeting and tempering of the four elements of this world; and when any form from quality is added to this, a perfected body is made. For that which hinders many does not hinder me. For we seem to them to act against ourselves and to affirm things contrary and adverse to us, saying at one time that the union of the four elements begets matter, and at another time that the meeting of quantity and quality with substance is the cause of matter. And no wonder: since it is hidden from them that the elements of this world are composed from no other source than the meeting of the aforementioned accidents of substance. For heat coupled with dryness creates fire; heat with moisture creates air; moisture with cold creates water; and cold with dryness creates earth. And since the aforementioned qualities, coming together, cannot appear by themselves, quantity supplies them with a "how much" in which they may shine forth sensibly. Indeed, quantity is like a second subject after substance, and therefore it is placed first after it in the order of the <term>Categories</term> <note>A reference to Aristotle's Categories, a foundational text on the classification of reality.</note>; since without quantity, quality does not know how to become manifest. If, therefore, the elements are made of quantity and quality, and bodies are made from elements, then bodies are made from quantity and quality.

**M.** Since I see that you have acutely perceived the goal of our intention, tell me, I pray, whether this division of forms into two species of different kinds—namely, of substance and of quality—seems valid to you or not? **D.** Valid indeed, and like the truth, although I have conceded this to be reasonable not without a certain scruple in my mind. For you could more easily persuade me that a substantial form added to matter constitutes a natural body, rather than a qualitative one. For I would have believed it more probable that substance, not quality, is the cause of the constitution of a body. For by the aforementioned reasons, I am of the opinion that quality is the cause not only of matter, but also of form; except that it makes matter when mixed with quantity, but pours form over matter by itself alone. Not that I am unaware that one cause can produce many effects from itself; since I see both heat and light break out from one fire at the same time, and light itself is the cause of both brightness and shadows. What shall I say of how many diverse bodies are made from one and the same matter, or into how many numbers one form is multiplied? and other things of this sort. And therefore I would think that substance itself becomes the form of matter, and not its accidents.

**M.** I wonder greatly why those things which were clearly settled between us a moment ago slip so quickly from your memory. Consider more diligently, therefore, and let us briefly repeat the same things. **D.** I am ready: repeat them. **M.** Was it not unshakably agreed between us that substance is incorporeal? **D.** It was agreed. **M.** And yet I should not think you have already forgotten that quantities and qualities, insofar as they are thought of in themselves, are incorporeal, and do not subsist in any other subject except in the substance to which they happen to belong, and in which they remain inseparably. **D.** This also I firmly hold. **M.** Is it not therefore to be said with probability that everything which is composed of quantity and quality—that is, the "how much" and the "of what kind"—receives the cause of its constitution from nowhere else but from substance itself, to which quantity and quality themselves are proven to happen naturally as primary and greatest accidents, and without which they cannot exist? For whatever proceeds from those things which are in the source, I do not see why it should not be referred back to the source itself: especially since substance itself, insofar as it is substance, is in no way visible,

# BOOK ONE

<note>original: "LIBER PRIMUS"</note>

...visibly, tangibly, and with spatial extension, it is able to appear. Indeed, the gathering together of the **accidents** <note>In medieval philosophy, "accidents" are qualities like color or size that exist within a substance but are not the substance itself.</note> which are within it, or understood to be around it, can create a certain sensible and spatial thing through the process of generation. For **quantity** and **quality** join together the "how much" and the "of what kind." These two, joined to one another and receiving the spark of generation in a certain manner and time, reveal a complete body. All other accidents appear to be added onto these. Indeed, we primarily look for these four things in our own bodies, and in those of other animals and inanimate objects: **how much** it is (its size), what parts it consists of, whether it extends through spaces of length and breadth; **of what kind** it is, whether it has an upright and human form, or a prone and beastly one; at what **time** it was created; and in what **manner** it is defined. Thus, it is situated within itself so that it is not infinite, but rather a single thing limited within its own kind. These things, as we have said, are observed primarily in our bodies.

Beyond these, however, by a higher consideration, we contemplate ***ousia*** <note>original: "ouſian" (Greek: οὐσία). The fundamental essence or "being" of a thing.</note> which is the origin of substantial forms. For we say this body belongs to this ***Ousiades*** <note>original: "Ouſiadis". The essential or substantial form.</note>—that is, its substantial form: whether it be human, or equine, or of some irrational animal encompassed within ***ousia***. For by these names, we do not refer to the bodies of animals, but rather to their substantial forms.

55. Indeed, we ought to distinguish three things clearly, whether thinking about ourselves or about other animals: **what we are**, **what is ours**, and **what is around us**. 
*   **What we are** is our substance, which is vital and intellectual, existing above the body and all its senses and visible form. 
*   **What is ours**, but is not "us," is the body that clings to us, composed of quantity and quality and other accidents; it is sensible, changeable, dissolvable, and corruptible. It is most true to say it is nothing other than the instrument or seat of the senses, which is called by the Greeks ***aistheteron*** or ***aistheterion mela*** <note>original: "aiſtheron, vel aiſtherion mela" (Greek: αἰσθητήριον). A place or organ of sensation.</note>, that is, the "guardhouse of the senses." 
For while the soul is incorporeal and cannot manifest its operations through itself without the senses, and the senses themselves can only be kept within certain seats, the Creator of nature established the body for the use of the soul, in which He might guard its "vehicles," so to speak—that is, the senses. 
*   **Around us** are all the sensible things which we use, such as the four elements of this world and the bodies composed from them. For our mortal bodies cannot remain without these. Indeed, they are fed by **earth**, they drink of **moisture** (water), they breathe in **air**, and they are warmed by **fire**. They are nourished by two: earth and water; they are governed so that they may live by two: air and fire. Two are passive, passing into the body: earth and water; two are active, constituting the "workshop" of the body: air and fire. Indeed, the fiery power, whose seat is in the heart, distributes the subtle vapor of food and drink through hidden pores into various parts of the body, and separates the dregs for excretion. But fire itself, unless it is blown upon by the airy spirit and nourished by food and drink as if by kindling, is quickly extinguished; and without delay, the entire building of the body, dissolved and collapsed, grows stiff as the cold overcomes the power of heat. But there is another place for discussing these things.

**D.** 56. I accept these points as probable; yet I do not cease to turn over in my mind how these things can agree with one another—how incorporeal and invisible things, by their meeting, can create visible bodies among themselves. It would seem that matter is nothing else, and has no other cause for its constitution, except for those things which are considered by the gaze of wisdom alone—among themselves, in themselves, and not in some blended perception. This is especially so since the magnificent **Boethius** <note>An influential 6th-century philosopher and statesman.</note>, the greatest philosopher in both languages <note>Latin and Greek.</note>, asserts such things in his books *On Arithmetic*: 
> *Wisdom is the comprehension of the truth of things which exist and which possess an immutable substance. We say those things "exist" which neither increase by expansion nor decrease by contraction, nor are changed by variations; but they always preserve themselves by their own power, leaning upon the nature of their own support. These are qualities, quantities, forms, magnitudes, smallness, equalities, habits, acts, dispositions, places, bodies, and whatever is found united in some way to bodies. These things are indeed incorporeal in their own nature and thrive by the reason of an immutable substance; but by participation in a body they are changed, and by the touch of a variable thing they pass into a changeable inconstancy. Therefore, since these things have—as has been said—obtained an immutable substance and power by nature, they are truly and properly said to "exist."*

58. Does it not follow from this opinion that we are given to understand, in part, that **matter** (and the body made from it) is one thing, while **quantity**, **quality**, and the other things contemplated by wisdom alone (which always preserve the immutable power of their nature) are another? For matter and the body, to which these things happen, are diverse and inconstant through variable change. For what then? Does it seem likely that if from quantity and quality and the others...

# ON THE DIVISION OF NATURE.

...if matter subsisted through natural accidents <note>In philosophy, "accidents" are non-essential qualities like color or size that can change without destroying the thing itself.</note> joined to one another, would it not necessarily be immutable itself? For if this is understood regarding causes, why should it not also be understood regarding their effect? Just as quantities and qualities and other similar things are perceived by the gaze of the mind alone, so too might matter and body yield not to the bodily senses, but to the intellect. But as it stands, we perceive formed matter which becomes a body (for unformed matter is entirely intelligible <note>i.e., can only be grasped by the mind, not the eyes</note>) through bodily sense; yet we see quantity and quality by the intellect alone. How then can quantity and quality produce matter which is so far distant from them? **M.** You are deceived by a very sharp but false argument, or perhaps you wish to deceive others: but whether you yourself doubt these things, or are merely taking on the persona of others who doubt, I do not yet know. **D.** I perceive both in myself. For I have taken care to propose these things from the persona of others who, not without reason, either hesitate or are utterly ignorant concerning such matters; and yet I see that I myself am not yet of such purity in these things that I have nothing further to inquire about them. **M.** We must therefore use, I believe, reason and authority, so that you may be able to discern these things clearly. For in these two consists the whole power of finding the truth of things. **D.** We must indeed. For these things have been sought by many, but found by few. **M.** Tell me then what you think about matter itself, from which formed bodies are made: while it is unformed in itself, is it considered by sense or by reason? **D.** By reason, certainly; for I do not dare to say by sense. For matter lacking form can be reached by no bodily sense. **M.** You have answered correctly: but see to it that you do not question us again later about these things which you have now accepted, lest we linger too long on such points when higher things invite us to consider them. **D.** Concerning those things which have now been defined between us by the pure intuition of the mind, I do not think I shall trouble you further. However, regarding what you said—that higher things invite us to consider them—I do not cease to wonder. For I do not see what higher thing after God ought to be considered by reason than unformed matter; when one asks what matter is, what form is, and what is produced from matter and form: whence comes matter, and whether it should be numbered among the primordial causes <note>The "primordial causes" are the first patterns of things created by God, similar to Plato's Ideas.</note> which were created so primarily, or among the following causes which are born from the primordial ones? Whether it is to be accounted among those things which press upon the senses, or among those which belong to the intellects: and whether it can be defined while it is still infinite: or even if the infinite can be defined, which seems to resist reason, since it has been clearly established by the Holy Fathers that there are only two things which can in no way be defined, namely God and matter. For God is infinite and unformed, since He is formed by none, while He is the form of all things. Matter is similarly unformed and infinite, needing to be formed and limited from elsewhere, while in itself it is not formed, but "formable." And this likeness of the Cause of all things—from which, and in which, and through which, and toward which all things are—and of the unformed cause itself (I mean matter, which was created for this purpose, so that those things which cannot be reached by the senses in themselves might in some way appear sensibly within it) is understood by contrast. For the highest Cause of all is unformed and infinite through the excellence of all forms and limits. For He is not only the principal form of all, but more than form, surpassing every form, and forming everything formable and unformable. For He is the form of those things which can be formed and can form, since they either desire Him or are turned toward Him. And He is the "unformedness" of those things which cannot be formed, because of the excellence of His nature and its close likeness to that Cause. Indeed, that unformedness of things which are unformable is not called unformedness because it lacks all form, but because it transcends every sensible and intelligible form. And therefore the Cause of all is accustomed to be both affirmed and denied, so that it is said of Him: He is Form, He is not Form; He is Unformedness, He is not Unformedness. For whatever is predicated of Him can be both affirmed and denied, because He is above everything that is said and understood, and not understood. But matter is called unformed through the privation <note>The absence or lack of something.</note> of all forms. For nothing is formed by it, but it receives diverse and innumerable forms. **M.** You are not far from the truth. Therefore, since unformed matter is perceived only by the eye of the mind (I mean by reason), does it not necessarily follow that it is incorporeal? **D.** I would not dare to deny even this. **M.** Therefore it is incorporeal. **D.** It is indeed: and by my own judgment I see that I am convinced. **M.** Do you wish this to be strengthened by authority? **D.** Greatly, and I ask that it be done.

59. **M.** We find that many have disputed concerning matter, both those skilled in worldly wisdom and those in divine wisdom: but it is right to use the testimony of a few. Saint Augustine, in the books of his *Confessions*, asserts that unformed matter is the mutability of mutable things, capable of all forms. Plato agrees with this in the *Timaeus*, likewise saying that unformed matter is...

# BOOK ONE.

<page-num>33</page-num>

...the capacity for forms. With both of these in agreement with one another, it can be stated and defined thus: *The mutability of changeable things, capable of all forms, is formless matter.* <note>The author is synthesizing Platonic and Augustinian thought here.</note> Saint Dionysius the Areopagite, in his books *On the Divine Names*, says that matter is *a participation in ornament, form, and appearance, without which matter is formless in itself and can be understood in no way.* And so, according to Dionysius, it can be gathered: If matter is a participation in ornament, form, and appearance, then that which lacks participation in ornament, form, and appearance is not matter, but a certain formlessness. Therefore, whether formless matter is a mutability capable of forms, according to Augustine and Plato; or a certain formlessness lacking participation in appearance, form, and ornament, according to Dionysius; you will not deny, I think, that if it can be understood at all, it is perceived by the intellect alone. 

**D.** I granted this clearly some time ago. 

**M.** What then? Do you think that appearance itself, and form, and ornament—by whose participation that aforementioned formlessness or mutability is turned into matter—can be considered in any way other than by the mind’s gaze? 

**D.** In no way; for it has been sufficiently established by the previous arguments that form and appearance, without which no ornament can be made, are entirely incorporeal <term>incorporeal: existing without a physical body or material substance</term>. 

**M.** Now, therefore, you see that from incorporeal things—namely, from a changeable formlessness capable of forms, and from form itself—a certain corporeal thing (I mean matter) and a body are created. 

**D.** I see it clearly. 

**M.** You concede, then, that from the coming together of incorporeal things, bodies can be made. 

**D.** I concede it, compelled by reason.

60. **M.** And yet, while these things are so, you will necessarily admit that bodies can be resolved back into incorporeal things, so that they are no longer corporeal, but utterly dissolved. Indeed, incorporeal things, by their natural concurrence and wonderful harmony, compose bodies in such a way that they in no way abandon their natural state and immovable vigor. To use a certain likeness: shadow is born from light and a body; yet neither the light nor the body is moved into the shadow. But when the shadow is dissolved, it is understood to return to its causes, namely the body and the light. For true reason teaches that the body and light are the cause of shadows, in which they [the shadows] naturally rest silent, since they can appear in no place because of the brightness of light poured everywhere around the bodies. For those who think a shadow perishes when it does not appear to the senses are in error. For a shadow is not nothing, but something. Otherwise, Scripture would not say, *And God called the light Day, and the darkness He called Night.* <note>Genesis 1:5</note> For God does not give a name except to that which is from Himself; nor in that place does the depth of the spiritual contemplation <term>theoria: a Greek term for deep contemplation or mystical insight</term> take away the truth of the history. For there, according to the consideration of things made, we accept darkness and night as nothing other than the shadow of the earth, made in the likeness of a cone by the surrounding solar rays, and always opposite the globe of light. And the same reasoning applies to smaller shadows cast by any light and bodies, whether they are finite or infinite, and of whatever shape they may be. Do not wonder, therefore, that bodies are created from incorporeal causes and are resolved back into them again; and that these causes themselves proceed, having been created by the one and the same Creator of all things.

61. For from the Form of all things—namely, the Only-Begotten Word of the Father—every form is created, whether it be substantial or that which is assumed from quality and, joined to matter, generates a body. From Him also comes all formlessness. Nor is it a wonder that from the Form that is formless by excellence <note>original: "ex informi forma per excellentiam" - referring to God as a form so pure He transcends the category of form</note>, formlessness (which is the privation of all forms) should be established: since not only things of the same kind, but things of different kinds—that is, not only those that are of one genus, but also those of a different genus, and not only those said to be or not to be by excellence, but also by privation—flow from the same fountain of all. What then? Is it not now clearly shining for you that it was not without reason we said that bodies are made from the concurrence of accidents, following the authority of Saint Gregory of Nyssa; since you see other authors, whether Greek or Latin, asserting that bodies are made from incorporeal things? Therefore, it pleased me to bring the opinion of the aforementioned Father Gregory into our discussion. For in his book *On the Image* <note>Gregory of Nyssa's "De hominis opificio"</note>, acting against those who say matter is co-eternal with God, he says: *Nor is that conception of matter brought forth outside of those things found consequently, which declares that it subsists from the intellectual and the immaterial.* For we find that all matter consists of certain qualities; if it were stripped of these, it would be comprehended by no reason in itself. And yet every species of quality is separated from the subject by reason. Now, reason is a certain intellectual and incorporeal contemplation; as for example, when an animal or a piece of wood is proposed in contemplation, or any other thing having a material constitution, we understand many things about the subject by an intellectual division, the reason for each of which is held without confusion in relation to what is considered. For the reason of color is one thing, and that of weight another; again, the reason of quantity is one thing, and that of the intelligence—which is the property according to touch—is another. Softness is not a measurement of length. And the rest of them...
