# ON THE DIVISION OF NATURE
### I
<meta>catchword: all things</meta>

the things which have been said are not mixed with one another, nor with the body, according to reason. For in each of these, a specific cause is understood according to how it is interpreted; and none of the things that are <term>theorized</term> <note>original: theorizantur; to contemplate or examine intellectually</note> around a subject are mixed with another quality. If, therefore, color is intelligible, and solidity and quantity are also intelligible, along with the rest of such specificities; and if each one of these were taken away from the subject, the entire rationale of the body would be dissolved at once. It follows, then, that those things whose absence we find to be the cause of the body’s dissolution, their meeting together creates or takes on a material nature. For just as there is no body in which the thing—that is, <term>essence</term> <note>original: οὐσία (ousia); the fundamental being or substance of a thing</note>—and figure, and solidity, and distance, and weight, and the rest of the specificities are not present (yet each of these is not the body itself, but is found separately as something other than the body), so conversely, wherever the aforementioned things meet, they complete a bodily substance. But if the understanding of these specificities is intelligible, and God is also called an "intelligible nature," it is not inconsistent that these intellectual occasions for the generation of bodies were substituted from an incorporeal nature—by an intellectual nature, indeed, substituting intelligible powers, while the nature of these powers brings about a material generation through their meeting with one another.

62. Do you not see, then, that the greatest and strongest argument of the aforementioned teacher suffices? For if the body were something other than the meeting of the <term>essences</term> <note>original: οὐσίας (ousias)</note> of accidents, then if those accidents were removed, the body would subsist in itself by itself. Indeed, every subject that subsists by itself does not need accidents in order to be, just like <term>essence</term> itself: for whether they happen to it or not; whether those things are in it which cannot exist without it, or those things depart from it which can be separated from it (whether by thought alone or by act and deed); it always and unchangeably subsists by its own natural supports. But a body, once the accidents are removed, can in no way subsist, since it is supported by no substance. For if you take quantity away from a body, it will not be a body; for it is contained by the spaces and number of its members. Similarly, if you take quality away from it, it will remain formless, and nothing. The same reasoning must be observed in the other accidents by which a body seems to be contained. Therefore, that which cannot subsist by itself without accidents must be understood to be nothing other than the meeting of those same accidents.

63. What wonder, then, or what is contrary to reason, if we similarly understand the magnificent Boethius <note>An influential 6th-century philosopher and statesman</note> to have meant nothing else by a "variable thing" but the material body, which is constituted from the meeting of those things which truly exist (as he himself says), and while they are considered within it, it must necessarily undergo a certain mutability. Nor is this a wonder, for things that are unchangeable in themselves are perceived in one way in their simplicity by the pure gaze of the mind, and in another way by the bodily sense when they are composed in some matter made from their meeting; since we see that those things which are simple and incorruptible in themselves produce something composite and corruptible when they come together. To whom is it unknown that this mass of the earthly globe is compacted from four simple elements, which, while it is corruptible and dissolvable, yet those elements from which it is made remain in their indissoluble simplicity? And reason observes this commonly in almost all bodies. And of these things, I estimate enough has been said. D. Enough indeed; and I see that we must now return to the <term>theory</term> of the remaining categories. For there is no doubt that to hesitate longer over these is the mark of those who consider the natures of things too little. And because of this, I am ashamed and repentant of my slowness in many matters. M. Do not be ashamed or repentant; for although the things we are disputing are so clear to the wise that none of them hesitate over them, I do not doubt they are useful to the unlearned and those lower down who are picking their way on the journey of reason toward higher things. D. There is no way to doubt it; and I perceive this in myself; but proceed to the rest.

64. M. Two <term>Categories</term> <note>Aristotle's classifications of all possible predicates of a subject</note> remain for contemplation, if I am not mistaken: namely, "acting" and "being acted upon." For while disputing about Place, we discussed certain things about Time, as much as the necessity of the present dispute required. D. I ask nothing more of you now concerning Time and Place. For what has been said of these suffices; for if anyone were to say everything that reason desires to be considered about each one, the dispute would have little or no end. M. Consider, then, whether "action" and "passion" <note>In this context, "passion" means being the recipient of an action</note> are properly predicated of God; or whether, like the other categories, they should be thought to be said through metaphor. D. Through metaphor, surely. For why? Should it be thought that these two exceed the rules of the others, when they seem to be of lesser power? M. Tell me, I pray, what does it seem to you: to move and to be moved, is it not to act and to be acted upon? D. I do not see how it could be otherwise. M. Similarly, I think, to love and to be loved. D. They are gathered under the same rule. For no one skilled in the liberal arts is ignorant that these words, and those similar to them, are active and passive. M. If, therefore, words are of either active or passive significance, they are no longer predicated of God properly, but figuratively, and

<summary>The Master explains that material bodies are not independent substances but are formed by the "concurrence" of non-material, intelligible qualities. The dialogue then shifts to the categories of Action and Passion, concluding they can only be applied to God metaphorically.</summary>

# BOOK ONE.
E 2
<meta>catchword: natu-</meta>

...everything that is predicated metaphorically is said of Him not in reality, but in a certain manner. In reality, God neither acts nor suffers <note>original: "pati" - in a philosophical sense, to be acted upon or to undergo change</note>; He neither moves nor is moved; He neither loves nor is loved. **D.** This last conclusion requires no small amount of consideration. For against this, as I think, the authority of the whole of Holy Scripture and of the holy fathers seems to struggle. For how often, as you know, does Holy Scripture clearly pronounce that God acts and suffers, loves and is loved, esteems and is esteemed, sees and is seen, moves and is moved, and other things of that kind? Since the examples of these are innumerable and meet the seeker everywhere, I have decided to pass over them now lest they produce prolixity. It is enough to use this one Gospel example: *He who loves me will be loved by my Father, and I will love him, and manifest myself to him.* <note>John 14:21</note> Again, Saint Augustine, in his *Hexameron* <note>Augustine's commentary on the six days of creation</note> discussing divine motion, brought forth these words:

> The Creator Spirit indeed moves himself without time and place; he moves the created spirit through time without place; he moves the body through time and place.

If, therefore, "acting" and "suffering" are predicated of God not in reality—that is, not properly—as we said before, it follows that neither "moving" nor "being moved" apply. For to move is to act; to be moved is to suffer. Likewise, if He neither acts nor suffers, how is He said to love all things, and to be loved by all things which have been made by Him? For to love is the motion of the agent, but to be loved is the motion of the patient <note>the one being acted upon</note> and is both the cause and the end of motion. But I say this following common usage. For if anyone inspects the nature of things more diligently, he will find that many words which seem to be active on the mere surface of the voice nevertheless have a passive meaning in the intellect; and on the contrary, a passive surface signifies an act. For he who loves or esteems suffers <note>is affected by the object of love</note>; but he who is loved or esteemed acts <note>is the cause of the love</note>. But if God loves what He has made, He certainly seems to be moved: for He is moved by His own love. And if He is loved by those things which are able to love, whether they know what they love or do not know, is it not clear that He moves them? For the love of His beauty moves them. Therefore, how it can be said that He neither moves nor is moved, lest He seem to act and suffer, I am unable to consider by myself. And because of this, I most earnestly request that you untie the knot of this question. **M.** Do you think that in those who act, the agent is one thing, the power to act another, and the acting itself another, or are they one and the same? **D.** I do not think they are one, but three things differing from one another. For the lover—that is, the one who loves—is a certain substance of a certain and defined person, to whom happens a certain power through which he can act, whether he acts or not. But if the substance itself moves itself through that power, or does something, it is said to "act." And because of this, there seem to be three things: namely, the substance in which the "ability to act" exists. The effect of this possibility, as if of a certain cause, is "to act" in some thing, whether the action itself is reciprocal—that is, it returns to the same person—or passes into another.

**M.** You distinguish correctly. What does it seem to you? Must not the same distinction be observed in him who suffers, so that the patient is one thing, the power to suffer another, and the suffering itself another, whether one suffers from oneself or from another? **D.** The same indeed. **M.** Therefore, are these three things not of the same nature in those who love and in those who are loved? **D.** They are not, as I think. For the nature of substances is one thing, and that of accidents <note>non-essential qualities that can change</note> is another. For he who acts or suffers is a substance. But to be able to act or suffer, and the acting and suffering itself, are accidents. **M.** I wonder how you have forgotten those things which were sought and found, as I judge, and defined in the previous reasonings. **D.** I pray you, remind me, and recall those things to memory. For I do not deny that I am negligent and forgetful due to the vice of memory, which is oblivion. **M.** Do you remember that it was collected and concluded by us that <gloss>ousia, dynamis, energeia</gloss> <note>original Greek: οὐσίαν, δύναμιν, ἐνέργειαν</note>—that is, essence, as we have often said, power, and operation—are a certain inseparable and incorruptible trinity of our nature, which are joined to one another by a wonderful harmony of nature, so that the three are one, and the one is three? And they are not as if of different natures, but of one and the same; not as substance and its accidents, but a certain essential unity and substantial difference of three in one?

**D.** I remember, and henceforth I shall never give it over to oblivion. For it is most foolish and wretched to forget the clearest image of the Creator. But I do not yet see where this is going, unless perhaps because I responded, when questioned by you, that there were three things differing from one another—one indeed according to the subject, but two according to accident—which three seem to be far removed from the former three. And because of this, those three which we said were of one and the same substance are the only things that truly are: i.e., essence, power, and operation. But those which have now been added by me—that is, substance with accidents, namely the possibility of acting and the effect of that possibility, which is acting—must be considered not to be superfluous, and found by no [false] reason: or on the contrary: or even, what seems more correct for me to say, that both these and those which are in the nature of things, in their own...

<summary>The Teacher and Disciple debate whether God can be said to "act" or "love" in a literal sense, contrasting human accidental actions with the essential Trinity of essence, power, and operation.</summary>

# ON THE DIVISION OF NATURE

...are distinguished by natural differences. But whether this must be granted or not, I leave to be decided by your judgment. **M.** What you have most recently proposed seems to agree rightly with reason. For whoever says that an essential trinity—namely, essence, power, and operation <note>original: "essentiam, virtutem, operationem"</note>—is present, unshaken and incorruptible, in all things, and especially in rational and intellectual natures, does not, in my opinion, depart from the truth. This trinity, in all things in which it exists, can neither be increased nor diminished. But the following trinity is understood to be, as it were, a certain effect of the preceding trinity. For it does not conflict with the truth, I think, if we say that from that very essence—which is created as one and universal in all things, and is common to all, and is therefore said to belong to no one specifically because it belongs to all who participate in it—there emanates by a natural progression a certain proper substance of each individual participant, which belongs to no other except to that one alone to which it belongs. In this substance there is also a proper possibility <note>or potentiality</note>, which is assumed from no other source than from that universal power of the aforementioned universal essence and power. Similarly, it must be said of the proper operation of the most specific substance and power, that it descends from no other source than from that universal operation of the same universal essence and power. Nor is it surprising if these three things which are considered in individuals are called certain accidents of the aforementioned universal trinity, and its first appearances; since the universal trinity is in itself one, and remains unchangeably in all things which consist of it and in it, and can neither be increased nor diminished, nor corrupted, nor perish. But these things which are considered most specifically in individuals can both be diminished and varied in many ways. For not all are participants in the universal essence, power, and operation in the same way. Some have more, others less. Yet no one is entirely deprived of participation in it. The essence itself also remains one and the same in all its participants; it is present to no one more or less for the sake of participation. Just so is light to the eyes. For it is whole in each individual, and whole in itself. To be increased or diminished, however, is a certain defect or progress of participation; and therefore it is not irrationally judged to be an accident.

65. For that which is always the same is rightly called true substance; but that which varies proceeds either from the mutability of an unstable substance or from the participation of accidents, whether natural or non-natural. And do not wonder that certain accidents are said to belong to substances when they themselves subsist in other accidents; for you see that other accidents happen to quantity—which is without doubt an accident of substance—such as color, which appears around quantity; and times, which are understood in the lingering motions of things. For time is the certain and rational dimension of the delay and motion of changeable things.
**D.** In this, the goal of our intention does not differ, I think; but I would wish you to define briefly and clearly this final consideration of things. **M.** Let us posit, if it pleases you, a threefold understanding of things—that is, of essence, power, and operation—as the immutable substance and firm foundation of things established by the Creator of all. **D.** I think it should be so posited. **M.** Then that trinity which can be considered in individuals, proceeding from the first essential trinity like the effect of a preceding cause, and its primordial motions and certain primordial accidents, must be weighed as I see it. **D.** This too must be admitted. **M.** Whatever happens to those three subsequent things, whether internally or externally, whether by nature or by certain events, seems to become, as it were, an accident of accidents. **D.** I do not resist this conclusion either. For since there are, according to Aristotle, ten genera of things which are called categories <note>or predicaments</note>, and we find none of the Greeks or Latins opposing this division of things into genera, they place under one genus all the primary essences which the Greeks call *ousiai* <note>original: ουσίας - "beings" or "essences"</note>; (rightly so, because they exist through themselves and need nothing else to exist; for thus we see them firmly enclosed by the Creator of all as certain immutable foundations; and they subsist in the likeness of the principal cause of all things by their wonderful and unchangeable trinity, that is, as has often been said: essence, power, and operation) but the other nine genera are said to be accidents, and not without reason; for they do not subsist through themselves but in the aforementioned essential trinity. For as to what are called "place" and "time" by the Greeks—*topos* and *chronos* <note>original: τόπος & χρόνος</note>—that is, those things without which others cannot exist, it is not to be understood that the aforementioned substantial trinity of things is counted among those which cannot subsist without place and time. For that trinity does not need the help of place and time to exist, since it exists through itself before and above time and place by the dignity of its creation. But the nine genera which are attributed to accidents alone have been so divided by the authors that those very accidents which are primordially seen in essences are soon turned into substances, when to other acci-

<summary>The Master explains the relationship between the universal "essential trinity" (essence, power, operation) and the individual substances that participate in it. He integrates Aristotelian categories, arguing that while essence is independent of time and place, other categories function as accidents of this primary substance.</summary>

...subsist within accidents. For the first division of all things is into essences and accidents. The second is the division of accidents into substances <note>The author uses "substance" here in a specific logical sense where one attribute can become the foundation for another</note>, which extends almost to infinity; for what is an accident relative to what precedes it, soon becomes the substance for what follows it. But these matters must be debated elsewhere; for now, if it pleases you, let us follow what we have proposed.

**Master:** What then? Do you think that accidents belong to anything other than some essence or accident?

**Disciple:** No one skilled in the arts would say otherwise. For an accident deserves to be called such for no other reason than because it happens <note>original: "contingit," playing on the etymology of "accident" as something that "falls upon" or happens to a thing</note> to an essence, a substance, or another accident.

**Master:** Are "acting" and "being acted upon" <note>The categories of Action and Passion</note> contained within the number of accidents?

**Disciple:** Yes.

**Master:** Therefore, they belong to some substance. For these happen to specific substances; nothing "happens" to general essences.

**Disciple:** I would not deny this.

**Master:** Tell me, I pray, does anything "happen" to the supreme, simple, and divine nature?

**Disciple:** Far be it!

**Master:** Does that nature itself "happen" to anything else?

**Disciple:** I would not say that either; otherwise, it would seem to be passive, changeable, and capable of being part of another nature.

**Master:** It receives no accident, then, and is an accident to nothing?

**Disciple:** Truly none, and to nothing.

**Master:** Are "acting" and "being acted upon" accidents?

**Disciple:** This has been granted.

**Master:** Therefore, the supreme cause of all, the supreme first, which is God—does He not receive "acting" and "being acted upon"?

**Disciple:** I am greatly hemmed in by the force of this reasoning. For if I say this is false, perhaps reason itself will mock me and allow everything I have granted so far to waver. If I say it is true, it necessarily follows that what I granted regarding "acting" and "being acted upon" must also be granted regarding all other active and passive verbs, of whatever kind they may be. That is to say: God neither loves, nor is loved; He neither moves, nor is moved; and a thousand similar things. Even more: He neither "is" nor "subsists." But if this is so, do you see with how many and how great weapons of Holy Scripture I am overwhelmed? For from every side they seem to cry out against me and proclaim that this is false. Nor is it hidden from you, I think, how arduous and difficult it is to persuade simple minds of such things; indeed, even the ears of those who seem to be wise shudder when they hear these things.

**Master:** Do not be terrified now. We must follow reason now; for although reason investigates the truth of things and is not weighed down by any authority, it is in no way hindered from publicly opening and pronouncing those things which the scope of studious reasoning inquires into and laboriously finds.

66. Indeed, the authority of Holy Scripture is to be followed in all things, since the truth is held within it as if in its own secret dwellings; yet we must not believe that Scripture always uses the literal meanings of words or names when suggesting the divine nature to us. Rather, it uses certain likenesses and various modes of translated <note>metaphorical or figurative</note> words or names, condescending to our weakness and raising our still unrefined and childlike senses by simple teaching. Hear the Apostle saying: *I gave you milk to drink, not solid food.* For the divine oracles strive for this: that they might hand down and persuade us of something regarding the ineffable, incomprehensible, and invisible, in order to nourish our faith. Indeed, nothing else ought to be said or thought about God by those living purely and piously and studiously seeking the truth, except what is found in Holy Scripture; nor should any other significations or translations be used by those who either believe or dispute concerning God. For who would presume to say anything discovered by himself concerning the ineffable nature, beyond what that nature has modulated concerning itself in its own Holy Instruments—I mean the theological writers? But so that you may believe and hold this more firmly, I think the testimony of Saint Dionysius the Theologian <note>Pseudo-Dionysius the Areopagite, a highly influential 5th-century mystical philosopher</note> should be introduced here, if it seems right to you.

**Disciple:** It certainly does; I receive nothing more gladly than reason strengthened by the firmest authority.

**Master:** In the first chapter of *On the Divine Names*, the aforementioned Theologian commends the authority of Holy Scripture with great praise. And because he disputes in his usual complex and convoluted <note>original: "hyperbatice," referring to the use of transposed word order</note> manner, he seems very abstruse and difficult to understand for many. It has pleased me, therefore, to bring forth his opinion on this matter in a word order easier to understand than how it was written in its own place. *Universally,* he says, *one must not dare to speak, nor even to understand anything concerning the super-essential divinity, except those things expressed to us divinely from the sacred oracles. For the knowledge of that super-essentiality, which is above reason, intellect, and essence, must be referred to the higher splendors, which are constrained by temperance and simplicity regarding divine things, looking upward only as far as the ray of the divine oracles imparts itself.*

67. Do you see how he universally forbids anyone to dare to speak of the hidden divinity, except for those things said in the Holy oracles? These oracles he calls by the most glorious and true name of "higher splendors," constrained in simplicity and temperance regarding divine things. The same author says a little later in the same work: *Just as the incom-* <note>The text breaks off mid-word; "incomprehensible" is likely intended</note>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of God, specifically focusing on the "apophatic" or negative way of knowing the Divine, following the previous arguments regarding the limitations of human language.</meta>

# ON THE DIVISION OF NATURE

Those things which are incomprehensible and beyond contemplation are invisible to the senses; and in those things which exist in form and likeness, they are simple and lack likeness. In those things formed according to the shapes of bodies, they are the untouched and unfigured formlessness of the incorporeal. According to the same principle of truth, the super-essential <note>Latin: *superessentialis*; meaning "beyond being" or "above essence"</note> magnitude surpasses all essences, and the Unity beyond mind surpasses all minds. It is impossible for any power to grasp that which is above sense, and that Good which is beyond all reason is a secret to every reason—the unifying Unity of every unity, the super-essential Essence, the invisible Intellect, and the secret Word. It is irrationality, invisibility, and unnamability, existing according to none of the things that are. While it is the cause of being for all things, it is itself "Non-Being" <note>original: *non Ὄν*; Eriugena uses the Greek word for "Being" to explain that God is so far above existence that He can be called "Not-Being" in a superior sense</note>, for it is the summit of every essence, however it manifests itself properly and knowingly concerning itself. Regarding this super-essential and hidden Divinity, as has been said, one must not dare to speak or understand anything beyond what has been divinely expressed to us. For so the Divinity itself has best handed down concerning itself in the holy scriptures. Indeed, the knowledge and contemplation of what it is like is closed to all things that exist, being essentially separated from all. These points suffice concerning the need to follow the authority of Holy Scripture, especially in divine disputations.

68. But Reason strives universally in this: to persuade and to prove through certain investigations of truth that nothing can be said *properly* of God, since He surpasses every intellect and all sensible and intelligible significations. He is better known by not knowing; the ignorance of Him is true wisdom. He is more truly and faithfully denied in all things than He is affirmed. For whatever you deny of Him, you will deny truly; but not everything you affirm will you affirm truly. Indeed, if you should assert that He is this or that, you will be convicted of falsehood, because He is none of those things which exist, which can be spoken of, or understood. But if you pronounce that He is neither this, nor that, nor any other thing, you will be seen to be truthful, because He is none of the things that are, nor the things that are not. No one can approach Him unless, having first strengthened the journey of the mind, they leave behind all senses, intellectual operations, sensible things, and everything that is and is not; and so, as far as is possible, be restored in a state of unknowing to the unity of Him who is above all essence and intelligence. Of Him there is neither reason nor intelligence; He is neither spoken of nor understood; He has neither name nor word. 

Yet, it is not without reason, as we have often said, that all things from the highest to the lowest can be said of Him by a certain likeness, or unlikeness, or contrariety, or opposition; since from Him all things are made which can be predicated of Him. He did not only create things similar to Himself, but also things dissimilar; since He Himself is both like and unlike. He is also the cause of contraries; for by the power of those things which are truly created by Him, true reason proves that even those things which seem to be contraries—and are not privations of essence—are contained within Him. For no vice is found which is not the shadow of some virtue, either by a certain deceptive likeness or by open contrariety. By likeness, for example, pride bears the shadow of true power; luxury, of rest; fury, of fortitude; anger, of correction, and such like. By contrariety, however, as malice is the contrary of goodness. For just as goodness leads things from non-existence into existence so that they may be, so malice desires to corrupt all things that are and utterly dissolve them so that they may not be. And if this were to happen—that is, if all things perished—malice itself would perish at the same time. For if nature perished, vice would perish with it. But by the power of goodness, every nature is preserved from perishing. Yet malice is permitted to exist in that nature for the praise of goodness, by way of comparison with its contrary, and for the exercise of virtues through rational operation and the purging of nature itself: when "death shall be swallowed up in victory," and goodness alone shall appear and reign in all things, and malice shall universally perish. But these matters will be discussed more extensively in the fifth book. 

Therefore, let no authority terrify you away from those things which the rational persuasion of right contemplation teaches. For true authority does not oppose right reason, nor does right reason oppose true authority. Indeed, there is no doubt that both flow from one source, namely, Divine Wisdom.

69. One of these <note>referring to Authority</note> has granted and handed down many things to be thought and said concerning the incomprehensible and ineffable nature to those who seek piously, lest the study of true religion be entirely silent. This is done to nourish those still unrefined in the simplicity of the faith and to respond to the rivals of the Catholic faith, armed and fortified by divine bulwarks. The other <note>referring to Reason</note> corrects those simple ones still nourished in the cradles of the Church, piously and chastely, lest they believe or estimate anything unworthy of God; and lest they think that all things which the authority of Holy Scripture predicates concerning the Cause of all things are predicated *properly*. This applies whether they be the most glorious and highest things, such as life, power, and the names of other virtues; or middle things, such as the sun, light, a star, and all things from the higher parts of this world which are predicated of God; or...

<summary>Eriugena explores the "Apophatic" path, arguing that God is best understood through negation (what He is not) rather than affirmation. He also reconciles the relationship between Reason and Authority, stating they both derive from the same Divine Wisdom.</summary>

from the lower movements of visible creation; such as, in appearance, the brightness of a cloud, the sunrise, thunder, dew, shower, rain; likewise, water, river, earth, rock, wood, vine, olive, cedar, hyssop, lily, man, lion, ox, horse, bear, leopard, worm. Likewise, the eagle, dove, fish, whale, and countless other things; which, being established by nature, are transferred to the <term>Creative Nature</term><note>Latin: *natura conditrix*, referring to God as the creator who establishes all things.</note> through a certain transformation and figurative significance. And even more wonderfully, skillful Scripture has made translations not only from the creature to the Creator, but even from things contrary to nature; namely, from insanity, drunkenness, gluttony, forgetfulness, anger, fury, hatred, lust, and other similar things. By these, the minds of the less simple are less deceived than by the higher transfigurations which occur from nature. For the rational soul, although quite simple, might be deceived if it hears the names of natural things predicated of God and thinks they are said of Him properly <note>i.e., literally</note>; yet it is not entirely misled, as it would be if it heard names of things *against* nature predicated of the Founder of things—it would either judge them to be entirely false and reject them, or concede and believe them to be said figuratively.

**D.** I am not so terrified by authority, nor do I fear the onset of less capable minds, that I should be ashamed to pronounce with an open brow those things which true reason clearly gathers and defines without doubt; especially since such matters should only be treated among the wise, for whom nothing is sweeter to hear than true reason, nothing more delightful to investigate when sought, and nothing more beautiful to contemplate when found. But I desire to know what you are striving for with the aforementioned reasoning.

**70. M.** What would I seem to be engineering with these arguments, if not for you to understand that just as significant names for things—whether they be of substances, accidents, or essences—are used metaphorically <note>Latin: *translativè*</note> and not properly; so also the verbs signifying the natural or non-natural movements of <term>created nature</term><note>Latin: *natura condita*</note> can be predicated of the Creative Nature metaphorically, but not properly? For if the names of essences, substances, or accidents are placed upon God not in truth, but by a certain necessity of signifying the ineffable nature, does it not necessarily follow that the verbs which signify the movements of essences, substances, and accidents also cannot be said properly of God? For He transcends every essence, substance, every accident, every movement, action, and passion—and whatever is said or understood of such things, and whatever is neither said nor understood and yet is in them—by the incomprehensible and ineffable excellence of His nature. For what? If God is called "Love" through a metaphor, while He is more than love and surpasses all love; why should He not be said "to love" in the same way, while He exceeds the movement of love, because He desires nothing besides Himself, while He alone becomes all things in all? Similarly, if "Agent" and "Actor," "Maker" and "Creator," are named not properly now, but by a certain mode of translation; why should "to act" and "to make," or "to be acted upon" or "to suffer," not be predicated by the same kind of speech? Regarding other verbs which signify all movements of the entire changeable creation—whether natural or non-natural, intellectual or rational, irrational or corporeal, incorporeal, local or temporal, straight, oblique, angular, circular, or spherical—I think they must be understood in a similar way.

**D.** You urge me strongly to admit such things reasonably, but I would like you to bring in some protection from the authority of the Holy Fathers to strengthen these points.

**71. M.** You are not ignorant, I think, that what is prior by nature is of greater dignity than what is prior in time.

**D.** This is known to almost everyone.

**M.** We have learned that reason is prior by nature, but authority is prior in time. For although nature was created at the same time as time, authority did not begin to exist from the beginning of time and nature. Reason, however, arose with nature and time from the principle of things.

**D.** And reason itself teaches this. For authority proceeded from true reason, but reason certainly not from authority. Every authority which is not approved by true reason seems to be weak. But true reason, since it is fortified by its own powers, firm and immutable, needs to be strengthened by the support of no authority. For true authority seems to me to be nothing else than truth uncovered by the power of reason, and committed to writing by the Holy Fathers for the benefit of posterity. But perhaps it seems otherwise to you.

**M.** Not at all. And therefore, reason must be used first in these matters now at hand, and then authority.

**72. D.** Proceed in whatever order you wish, for I am your follower.

**M.** Does it seem to you that "to make" and "to suffer" <note>In the sense of "to be acted upon" or "to undergo change."</note> can exist without some movement of the one making or the one suffering?

**D.** Regarding the one making, I have no doubt. For I do not see how a maker can make without some movement of his own. But as for that which suffers, how it is moved in itself, I do not know so clearly.

**M.** Do you not see

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between the Master and the Disciple regarding the nature of motion and the relationship between the Creator and the created. It transitions from general definitions of action to the specific theological framework of Maximus the Confessor.</meta>

# ON THE DIVISION OF NATURE

Do you see that everything which acts, moves itself or is moved toward this: that what it desires to do may be moved from that which was not into that which is? For nothing can pass from that which was not into that which is without its own motion and the motion of another, whether it knows those motions or does not know them. I am not speaking now of that general and common motion, natural to all creatures, by which all things are moved from nothing into being; but of the motion used in time, by which changeable matter daily receives certain qualitative forms, whether by the movement of nature or by art. **D.** I perceive it now; and I accuse myself of excessive slowness, for I did not see that everything which is acted upon <note>original: "patitur" - to suffer, undergo, or be the object of an action</note> undergoes its own motions, or those of another, or both. **M.** Therefore, the doer and the deed both undergo motion. For that which acts undergoes its own motion in order to act. But that which is made sustains both its own and another's. Its own, indeed, by passing from that which was not into that which is; but another's, because it is not the cause of its own motion through itself, but rather the cause is the natural motion, or the free will, or a certain necessity of the one making it. That which is made, as we said, undergoes both its own and another's motion; but that which acts undergoes its own alone; although it often happens that the one who acts is moved to act by another cause, so that the doer and the sufferer appear to be one and the same. But the motion of the doer itself, although it may be born from various causes—whether natural, or willing, or unwilling—is called "its own" because it is understood to be not outside itself, but within itself. **D.** I do not deny that I am persuaded by you on that point. Therefore, I await what remains. **M.** I judge that no motion can lack a beginning and an end. For reason defines it no other way than that every motion begins from a certain principle and tends toward a certain end, in which it rests when it has arrived. This the venerable Maximus <note>Maximus the Confessor (c. 580–662), a Greek theologian whose work "Ambigua" Eriugena translated into Latin.</note> asserts most clearly in the third chapter of the *Ambigua*, saying:

> *If God is immutable, as being the fullness of all things, then everything that receives being from non-existing things is moved; it is rightly carried toward a cause that is altogether certain.*

For as the same Maximus teaches elsewhere, the cause of all things is the same as the end of all things. For God is the principle and cause of all creatures, and the end; since from Him they both receive and begin their being, and toward Him they are moved, so that in Him they may rest. The same author in the same chapter a little later:

> *Of those things which have been made, there is motion, whether the intelligible motion of intelligible things, or the sensible motion of sensible things. For none of those things which have been made is entirely immobile. Moreover, those who have sacredly introduced the holy knowledge of divine mysteries call such motion a "natural power" hastening toward its own end; or a "passion" <note>In the philosophical sense: an undergoing or being acted upon.</note>, that is, a motion coming from one thing toward another, the end of which is impassibility; or an "active operation," the end of which is perfection through itself.*

> *Truly, none of those things which have been made is its own end, since it is not its own cause; otherwise it would be both unbegotten, and lacking a beginning, and unchangeable, rightly having to be moved toward nothing in any way. For it would transcend the nature of those things which are; as if it existed for the sake of nothing. If indeed the true definition concerning Him is—though it belongs to another—which says: He is the end for the sake of whom all things are; but He Himself is for the sake of nothing, nor is He perfection through Himself <note>Meaning He is not a "process" of perfection but Perfection itself.</note>. Otherwise He would not be rightly full, and likewise; and He would have being from no one, for He is perfect through Himself, just as He is also without cause, nor is He "impassibility"; otherwise He would be both remaining, and infinite, and uncircumscribed. For to undergo <note>original: "pati"</note> is not naturally present in that which is universally impassible, which is neither loved by another, nor moved toward anything else through love.*

> *For it belongs to God alone to be the end, and perfection, and impassibility, rightly of the unchangeable, and the full, and the impassible.*

> *But it belongs to those things which have been made to be moved toward an end lacking a beginning; for all things whatsoever that have been made undergo being moved, just as those things which are not are motion through themselves, or power through themselves. If therefore those things which are brought into being subsist as rational things, they are also moved in every way, as if from a principle according to nature through "being," toward an end; moved according to knowledge through "well-being." For the end of those things which are moved is motion; that very thing in that which is "always being" is "well-being." Just as the principle is "being" itself, which indeed is God; who both gives "being" and grants "well-being," rightly the principle and the end. For it is from Him that we are moved absolutely, as from a principle; and it is in whatever way we are moved toward Him, as toward an end. But if it is moved rationally and intellectually toward itself; the intellectual thing also understands in every way. But if it understands, it loves in every way what it understands. If it loves, it undergoes in every way a passing over <note>original: "excessum" - an ecstasy or going out of oneself</note> toward that very thing, as something lovable. But if it undergoes this, it certainly also hastens. If it hastens, it certainly intends a powerful motion. But if it intends a powerful motion, it does not stop until it becomes a whole in the whole beloved, and is comprehended by the whole, willingly according to the will, receiving a saving circumscription, so that the whole may be affected by the whole circumscribing it; so that nothing at all remains of wanting to know itself as a whole from itself, being able to be known as circumscribed, but from the circumscriber. Just as air is illuminated through the whole by light; and as iron is all melted by fire.*

Do you see how the aforesaid venerable Master teaches that all motion exists only in those things which start from a principle, and tend through natural motion toward their end? And he defines natural motion itself in a threefold way thus:

> *Motion is a natural power hastening toward its own end. Or thus: Motion is*

<meta>catchword: passion</meta>

<summary>This page explores the philosophical and theological definition of motion, arguing that all created things move from a beginning (being) toward an end (well-being in God). Drawing heavily on Maximus the Confessor, it describes the soul's journey as a "powerful motion" that ends in a mystical union where the creature is "illuminated" by the Creator like air by light.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between a Teacher (M) and a Disciple (D) regarding the nature of God and the definition of motion. It follows the discussion of God as the source of all things.</meta>

# BOOK ONE

...motion is a passion <note>original: "passio"; here meaning a process of being acted upon or undergoing change</note> coming from one thing to another, the end of which is changelessness. Or thus: Motion is an active operation, the end of which is perfection through itself. However, regarding the statement that *motion is a passion coming from one thing to another*, when understood in terms of natural motion, it should not be understood as if the beginning from which the changeable motion starts—that is, the beginning of its own undergoing—is one thing, and the end it seeks is another; for the beginning and the end of all things that move naturally is one. For God is He from whom, through whom, and toward whom all things are moved. But because the concept of the beginning is one thing, and the concept of the end is another, these two concepts are spoken of as if they were "one thing and another," even though they concern the single beginning and end of all things; for example, as if one were to speak of moving from the concept of the beginning to the concept of the end in God.

73. Next, consider that everything which lacks a beginning and an end must also necessarily lack all motion. But God is <term>without beginning</term> <note>original Greek: "anarchos"</note>, that is, without a starting point; because nothing precedes Him, nor does anything cause Him to be. Nor does He have an end, since He is infinite; for nothing is understood to exist after Him, as He is the boundary of all things beyond which nothing proceeds. Therefore, He receives no motion. For He has nowhere to move Himself; since He is the fullness, and the place, and the perfection, and the stability, and the whole of all things—indeed, He is more than fullness and perfection, more than place and stability, more than the whole of all things. For He is more than what is said and understood of Him, in whatever way anything is said or understood concerning Him. 
**D.** These things shine clearly to me, I believe. 
**M.** If, therefore, you assign all motion to the creature, but make God free from all motion, are you so slow of mind as to attribute "acting" or "being acted upon" to Him from whom you take away all motion? For you have already granted, not incautiously I think, through previous rational conclusions, that these two things undoubtedly cannot happen except in those things in which motion exists. 
**D.** Regarding "being acted upon" <note>original: "pati"</note>, I would have no doubt: for I both believe and understand that God is entirely impassible <note>incapable of suffering or being changed by an outside force</note>. By "passion" I mean that which is the opposite of "acting," that is, "being made." For who would say or believe, much less understand, that God undergoes "being made," since He is the Creator and not a creature? For when, as was settled between us long ago, God is said to "be made," it is manifest that this is said in a figurative way. Indeed, He is thought to be made in His creatures universally, since in them He is not only understood to *be* (without which they cannot exist), but He becomes their very essence. *For He is the Being of all things; but the Super-being of the Divinity*, as Saint Dionysius <note>Dionysius the Areopagite, a highly influential early Christian theologian</note> says. He is also said to be made in the souls of the faithful, when He is either conceived in them through faith and virtue, or begins to be understood in a certain way through faith. For faith is nothing else, I think, but a certain principle from which the knowledge of the Creator begins to be formed in a rational nature. But regarding "acting" <note>original: "facere"; literally "to make"</note>, I do not yet see clearly, hearing as I do that all Holy Scripture and the Catholic faith confess God to be the Maker of all things. 
**M.** You have already granted that "acting" cannot exist without the motion of the actor. 
**D.** I have. 
**M.** Therefore, you will either grant motion to God, without which "acting" is not understood, or you will take away both motion and "acting" from Him at the same time.

<margin>☞</margin>
74. For these two are counted among those things that exist together; they arise and fall away at the same time. 
**D.** I cannot give motion to God, who alone is immutable, nor has He anywhere or toward anything to move Himself, since all things are in Him—indeed, since He Himself is all things. But I cannot take "acting" away from Him, since He is the Maker of all things. 
**M.** You will therefore separate motion and acting. 
**D.** Not even that; for I see that they are inseparable from one another. 
**M.** What then will you do? 
**D.** I do not know; and therefore I earnestly pray that you open a path for me and free me from this greatest difficulty. 
**M.** Accept then this progression of reasoning. How does it seem to you? Was God existing before He made all things? 
**D.** It seems to me He was. 
**M.** Therefore, "acting" was an accident <note>a non-essential quality that can change without changing the essence of a thing</note> to Him. For that which is not co-eternal and co-essential with Him is either something else outside of Him, or an accident to Him. 
**D.** I would not believe that there is anything else besides Him and outside of Him: for all things are in Him, and nothing is outside of Him; nor would I rashly grant Him any accident. Otherwise, He is not simple <note>in theology, "divine simplicity" means God is not made of parts</note>, but a certain composition of essence and accidents. For if something else is understood with Him which He Himself is not, or if anything happens to Him, surely He is neither infinite nor simple; which the Catholic faith and true reason most firmly deny. For it confesses that God is infinite, and more than infinite—for He is the infinity of infinities—and simple, and more than simple, for He is the simplicity of all simple things. And it believes or understands that nothing exists with Him; since He Himself is the circuit of all things that are, and that are not, and that can be, and that cannot be; and of those things that seem to be contrary or opposite to Him, not to mention things similar and dissimilar. For He Himself is the similarity of similar things, and the dissimilarity of dissimilar things, the opposition of opposites, and the contrariety of contraries. All these things, beautiful in an ineffable...

<summary>The Teacher and Disciple debate whether God can be said to "act" or "make" things if He is truly motionless and simple. They conclude that God is the infinite boundary of all existence, encompassing even opposites.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between the Master (M) and the Disciple (D) regarding the nature of God and the categories of being. It follows the discussion on whether God can be subject to the category of "doing" or "acting."</meta>

# ON THE DIVISION OF NATURE

and collects and composes them into one concord by a marvelous harmony. For those things which in the parts of the universe seem opposed and contrary to one another, and dissonant from each other, are, in the most general harmony of the universe itself, considered fitting and consonant. **M.** You understand correctly; and see to it that you do not later regret having granted what you have now given. **D.** Proceed; I will follow you in whatever order you wish, and whatever I have already conceded, I shall not take back. **M.** Was God, therefore, not [God] before He made all things? **D.** He was not. For if He were, then making all things would be an "accident" <note>In medieval philosophy, an 'accident' is a property that is not essential to a being's nature, like hair color or a temporary action.</note> to Him. And if making all things were an accident to Him, then motion and time would be understood to exist in Him. For He would move Himself to do those things which He had not yet done, and He would precede His own action in time—an action which was neither co-essential nor co-eternal with Him. **M.** Is it therefore co-eternal and co-essential to God to "make"? **D.** So I believe and understand. **M.** Are they two distinct things—God and His "making" (that is, His action)—or are they one simple and individual thing? **D.** I see that they are one. For God does not receive number into Himself, since He alone is innumerable, and a number without number, and the cause of all numbers above all number. **M.** Therefore, for God, "to be" is not one thing and "to make" another; rather, for Him, to be is the very same thing as to make. **D.** I do not dare resist this conclusion. **M.** When, therefore, we hear that God makes all things, we ought to understand nothing else than that God is in all things—that is, He subsists as the essence of all things. For He alone truly exists through Himself, and everything that is truly said to "be" in those things that exist, is He alone. For nothing of those things that exist truly is through itself. Whatever, however, is truly understood to be in it, it receives by participation in that One who alone is through Himself. **D.** I would not wish to deny this either. **M.** Do you see then how true reason completely separates the category of "doing" <note>original: "categoriam faciendi"</note> from the divine nature, and distributes it to mutable and temporal things which cannot lack a beginning and an end? **D.** I see this clearly too; even now I understand without hesitation that no category applies to God.

75. What then? Should we not, by the same reasoning, look into the power of all the words which Holy Scripture predicates of the divine nature? Should we not estimate that nothing else is signified by them except that simple, unchangeable, and incomprehensible divine essence—and more-than-essence—which surpasses all intellect and signification? For example, when we hear that God "wills," "loves," "esteems," "sees," or "hears," and other words that can be predicated of Him, we ought to think of nothing else but that His ineffable essence and power are being suggested to us through meanings natural to us. This is done so that the true and pious Christian religion may not remain so silent about the Creator of all things that it dares say nothing of Him for the instruction of simple minds, and for refuting the deceits of heretics who are always unfaithful to the truth, laboring to uproot it and seeking to deceive the less learned. Therefore, for God, "to be" is not different from "to will," "to make," "to love," "to esteem," "to see," and other such things which, as we said, can be predicated of Him; but all these are to be taken as one and the same in Him, and they suggest His ineffable essence in the way He allows Himself to be signified. **D.** Nothing else indeed. For where there is true, eternal, and indissoluble simplicity through itself, it is impossible for there to be "one thing and another," or many and diverse things.

76. I would, however, like you to persuade me more openly, so that I may clearly see that when I hear God "loves" or "is loved," I should understand nothing else but His own nature without any motion of a lover or a beloved. For when I have been persuaded of this, I will in no way hesitate wherever I read or hear that He wills, or desires and is desired, esteems and is esteemed, sees and is seen, seeks and is sought, or similarly moves and is moved. All these things are to be taken with one and the same understanding. For just as will, love, esteem, vision, desire, and motion, when predicated of Him, suggest to us one and the same thing; so also the words—whether they be active, passive, or neutral, and in whatever mode of meaning they are uttered—are understood to differ by no distinction of intelligence, as I think. **M.** I do not think you are deceived in such matters; for they can be as you judge. First, then, receive this definition of love. Love is the connection or bond by which the universality of all things is joined in ineffable friendship and indissoluble unity. It can also be defined thus: Love is the natural motion of all things that are in motion, their end and quiet station, beyond which no motion of a creature proceeds. Saint Dionysius <note>Dionysius the Areopagite, a 5th-6th century theologian whose works Eriugena translated from Greek to Latin.</note> openly supports these definitions, saying thus in his *Hymns of Love*:

> *Whether we speak of love as divine, or angelic, or intellectual, or animal, or natural, let us understand it as a certain unifying and continuous power: moving the superior things to the providence of the inferior, and the equals again into a social reciprocity, and finally the lowest subjects toward the better...*

<summary>The Master explains that God's being and action are identical, meaning God is the essence of all things. They conclude that human language (like "love" or "will") describes God's simple essence using familiar terms to help the faithful and counter heresy.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical exploration of the nature of God as the source and object of all love, drawing heavily on the Neoplatonic theology of Pseudo-Dionysius and Maximus the Confessor.</meta>

# BOOK ONE.

...the conversion of subjects. The same author <note>Referring to Dionysius the Areopagite</note> says in the same works: *Since we have arranged many loves out of the One, let us now again roll together and gather all of them into one folded love, the father of them all, from the many; first comprehending them into two loving universal powers, whose dominion and sovereignty arise entirely from the summit of all things, the immeasurable cause of all love, and toward which the universal love of each and all existing things is naturally extended.* Again in the same works: *Come now, and gathering these powers of love again into one, let us say: That there is a certain simple power, moving itself toward a certain unifying temperament from the Best down to the last of existing things, and from that last thing again consequently through all things back to the Best; leading itself back out of itself, and through itself, and toward itself: and always revolving into itself in the same manner.*

Rightly, therefore, is God called Love, because He is the cause of all love, and is diffused through all things, and collects all things into one, and is revolved toward Himself in an ineffable return, and terminates the loving motions of the entire creation in Himself. That very diffusion of the divine nature into all things which are in it and from it is said to "love" all things—not because He is diffused in any way that involves movement (for He lacks all motion and fills all things simultaneously), but because He diffuses and moves the gaze of the rational mind through all things, being the cause of the soul's diffusion and motion to seek Him, find Him, and understand Him as much as is possible. He fills all things so that they may exist, and by a peaceful coupling of universal love, He collects all things into an inseparable unity—which is what He Himself is—and comprehends them inseparably.

Likewise, He is said to be "loved" by all things that are from Him, not because He suffers or is affected by them (for He alone is impassible <note>term: impassible; incapable of suffering or being changed by an external force</note>), but because all things desire Him, and His beauty draws all things to Himself. For He alone is truly lovable, because He alone is the highest and true goodness and beauty; indeed, whatever is understood as truly good, truly beautiful, and lovable in Creatures, is He Himself. Just as there is no essential good, so there is no essential beauty or loveliness, except for Him alone.

77. Therefore, just as that stone which is called a magnet <note>original: "magnes"</note>, although by its natural power it attracts the iron approaching it, in no way moves itself to do this, nor does it suffer anything from the iron it attracts; so the Cause of all things leads back to itself all things that are from it without any motion of its own, but solely by the power of its own beauty. Hence the same Saint Dionysius says among other things: *Why the Theologians sometimes call God Love <note>original: "Amorem"</note>, sometimes Charity <note>original: "Dilectionem"</note>, sometimes the Lovable and the Delectable,* and he concluded the discourse saying: *because by the one He is moved, and by the other He moves.* The venerable Maximus <note>Maximus the Confessor, a 7th-century theologian</note>, explaining this conclusion more aptly, says: *As He is called subsisting Love and Charity, He is moved; but as the Lovable and the Beloved, He moves toward Himself all things capable of receiving love and charity.* And to speak more plainly: *He is moved, as if bringing about an inseparable union, as the receiver of the love and charity of those things; but He moves, as if attracting by nature the desire of those things which are moved toward Himself. And again, He moves and is moved, as if thirsting to be thirsted for, loving to be loved, and cherishing to be cherished.*

For even this sensible light which fills the whole visible world remains always immobile, even though its vehicle, which we call the Solar body, is revolved by eternal motion through the spaces of the Ether around the earth. The light itself, flowing from that vehicle as if from an inexhaustible fountain, so drenches the whole world with the immeasurable diffusion of its rays that it leaves no place to which it might move, and remains always immobile. For it is always full and complete everywhere in the world, leaving or seeking no place except for a certain small portion of this lower air around the earth, which it leaves to receive the shadow of the earth which is called Night. Yet it moves the sight of all animals capable of feeling light and attracts them to itself, so that through it they may behold as much as they can, what they can behold. Therefore it is thought to be moved because it promotes the rays of the eyes to move toward itself—that is, it is the cause of the eyes' motion for the purpose of seeing.

And do not wonder at hearing that the nature of light (which is fire) fills the whole sensible world and is immutably everywhere: for Saint Dionysius teaches this in the book *On the Celestial Hierarchy*, and Saint Basil affirms the same in the *Hexameron* <note>original: "Examero"; Basil's famous series of homilies on the six days of creation</note>: *the substance of light is indeed everywhere, but it breaks through in the luminaries of the world, whether great or small, by a certain natural operation, not only to illuminate, but to distinguish time by the motions of all celestial bodies.* What shall I say of the arts which the wise call the "liberal disciplines"? While they remain entirely complete and immutable in themselves, they are nevertheless said to be moved because they promote the gaze of the rational soul to seek and find them, and attract it to consider them; so that while they are immutable through themselves, as we have said, they nevertheless seem to be moved in the minds of the wise when they move them. And many other things in which the divine power...

<meta>This page continues the philosophical dialogue regarding the nature of God, specifically focusing on the tension between divine motion and divine rest, and the methodology of negative theology.</meta>

-># ON THE DIVISION OF NATURE<-

...an obscure likeness is perceived: for she <note>The Divine Nature, often referred to in the feminine *natura* in Latin</note> is above all likeness and excels every example. While she stands immutably and eternally through herself and in herself, she is nevertheless said to move all things, because through her and in her all things subsist, and have been brought from non-being into being; for by being, she is (whereas all things proceed from nothing into being) and she attracts all things to herself. She is also said to be moved, because she moves herself toward herself, and through this she moves herself and is, as it were, moved by herself. God, therefore, is through Himself Love, through Himself Vision, through Himself Motion; and yet He is neither Motion, nor Vision, nor Love; but more than Love, more than Vision, more than Motion. And He is, through Himself, To Love, To See, To Move; yet He is not, through Himself, To Love, To See, To Move, because He is more than To Love, To See, To Move. Likewise, through Himself He is To Be Loved, To Be Seen, and To Be Moved; yet He is not, through Himself, To Be Moved, nor To Be Seen, nor To Be Loved, since He is more than can be Loved, and Seen, and Moved. He loves Himself, therefore, and is loved by Himself, in us and in Himself; yet He does not love Himself, nor is He loved by Himself, in us and in Himself: but He is more than "loves" and "is loved" in us and in Himself. He sees Himself and is seen by Himself, in Himself and in us; yet He does not see Himself, nor is He seen by Himself, in Himself and in us; because He is more than "sees" and "is seen" in Himself and in us. He moves Himself and is moved by Himself, in Himself and in us; yet He does not move Himself, nor is He moved by Himself in Himself and in us, because He is more than "moves" and "is moved" in Himself and in us.

78. And this is the cautious, healthful, and Universal <note>original: *Catholica*</note> profession to be proclaimed concerning God: that first we should predicate all things of Him according to the <term>kataphatic</term> <note>The "affirmative" way of describing God by attributing perfections to Him</note>, that is, the affirmative way, whether by nouns or by verbs—yet not properly, but metaphorically. Next, we must deny that He is all those things which are predicated of Him through the kataphatic, by means of the <term>apophatic</term> <note>The "negative" way of describing God by stating what He is not</note>, that is, negation—yet not properly, but metaphorically; for it is more truly denied that He is any of those things which are predicated of Him, than it is affirmed that He is. Finally, above everything that is predicated of it, the <term>super-essential</term> <note>original: *superessentialis*; a nature that exists beyond the category of "being" or "substance"</note> nature, which creates all things and is not created, must be praised above all essence. Therefore, what God the Word made flesh said to His disciples—*It is not you who speak, but the Spirit of your Father who speaks in you*—true reason compels us to believe, say, and understand similarly in other cases: it is not you who love, who see, who move, but *the Spirit of your Father, who speaks in you* the truth concerning me and my Father and Himself; He Himself loves and sees me, and my Father, and Himself in you, and moves Himself in you, so that you may love me and my Father. If, therefore, the Holy Trinity loves itself in us and in itself, and sees and moves itself; it is certainly loved, seen, and moved by itself, according to that most excellent mode known to no creature, by which it both loves, sees, and moves itself; and is loved, seen, and moved by itself in itself and in its creatures, since it is above all things that are said of it. For who can say anything of the ineffable, and what can be said? For Him, no proper name, nor verb, nor any proper voice is found, nor is it possible for one to exist, *who alone has immortality and dwells in inaccessible light. For who has known the mind of the Lord?* But before we finish the present discussion, it seemed to me that a chapter by Saint Dionysius <note>Dionysius the Areopagite, a major influence on medieval mysticism</note> concerning the Divine state and motion should be introduced here, if it seems right to you.

79. **Student.** It certainly seems right, and by this latest reasoning I see myself purged of all ambiguity. **Teacher.** In the book *On the Divine Names*, he says: "It remains for us to speak of the divine state, or of God's rest in Himself:
> what else is this but God remaining in Himself? Fixed immovably in His natural immutability, and placed above all according to the same things, and acting around that same thing and in a similar way, and subsisting in every way from Himself according to His most stable self, and according to that same self unchangeable and universally immutable, and this super-essentially. For He Himself is the cause of the standing, the foundation, and the building of all things, He who is above all building and standing; and in Himself He constitutes all things, keeping them immovable in the standing of His own goods. But what of when the Theologians also call Him 'moving' and 'reaching into all things,' though He is immutable? Must this not also be understood in a divine manner? For it is to be piously judged that He is moved, not according to displacement, or alienation, or alteration, or conversion, or local motion—not in a straight line, nor in a circle, nor from both, nor intellectually, nor through the soul, nor naturally—but to say that God enters into essence and contains all things, and provides for all things universally, and is present to all things by the immeasurable surrounding of all, and in all existing things by His providential processions and operations. But one must also grant praise to the motion of God in a divine, immutable sense; and 'straight' must be understood as the inflexible and irrevocable procession of operations, and the generation of all things from Him. But 'spiral' <note>original: *Eliceides*, from the Greek for spiral or helical</note>, that is, oblique or lateral or—

It concludes with a citation from Dionysius the Areopagite regarding the divine "processions" into the created world.</summary>

<header>BOOK TWO.</header>
<sig>F 3</sig>

...the federal <note>original: "deralis," likely "foederalis" meaning a covenantal or binding progression</note> procession, and a fertile state; but that which is according to the cycle, contains that very thing, both the middle and the extremes, and those things which are to be contained; and it is the return into itself of those things proceeding from it. D. Order requires, as I see it, that you gather a brief summary concerning the fact that no one is able to properly predicate of God "acting" and "suffering" <note>In a philosophical sense: "acting" (doing) and "suffering" (being acted upon/undergoing change)</note>, or "making" and "being made," and thus place an end to this little book. M. You have already granted, unless I am mistaken, that for God "to be" is not different from "to act" or "to make," but it is one and the same thing for Him to be, to act, and to make; for a simple nature does not admit the understanding of Substance and Accidents <note>A "simple nature" refers to God's essence, which cannot be divided into a core substance and secondary qualities (accidents) like color or size.</note>.

80. D. I have indeed granted this unshakably. M. Therefore, just as "being" is predicated of Him, while He does not properly "become" being—since He is more than being, and the cause of all being and essence—so also He is said "to act," while He is more than acting and making, and the cause of all making and acting without any motion. This must be understood as being above all motion that can be understood according to accident; for He is the cause and principle of all motions, and of all accidents, just as He is of all essences. D. And this I would grant without doubt. M. What then remains, except that you understand it to be entirely necessary that, just as "being," "acting," and "making" are properly taken away from Him; so also "suffering" and "being made" are taken away. For I do not see how He who does not admit "acting" and "making" could admit "suffering" and "being made." D. Fix the limit of the book, for enough has been embraced within it.

# ON THE DIVISIONS OF NATURE <note>original: "Π Ε Ρ Ι Φ Υ Σ Ε Ω Ν" (Peri Physeon)</note>.

### *Here Begins* BOOK II.

1. M. In the previous book we spoke briefly concerning the universal division of Universal Nature. This was not as a genus into forms, or a whole into parts; for God is not the Genus of the Creature, nor is the Creature a species of God, just as the Creature is not the genus of God, nor is God a species of the Creature. The same reasoning applies to the whole and the parts: for God is not the "whole" of the Creature, nor is the Creature a "part" of God, just as the Creature is not the "whole" of God, nor a "part" of the Creature—although by a higher theory, according to Gregory the Theologian <note>Gregory of Nazianzus, a 4th-century Church Father</note>, we who participate in human nature are a "part of God," since in Him we live, move, and have our being; and metaphorically God may be called both Genus, and Whole, and Species, and Part. For everything that is in Him and from Him can be piously and rationally predicated of Him; but this is by a certain intelligible contemplation of the universe (by "Universe" I mean God and Creature). Now, let us repeat that same division of nature more broadly, if it seems right. D. It seems right and very necessary; for if it is not opened up by a broader inquiry of reason, the matter will seem to have remained merely touched upon and not discussed. M. Such then was, as I think, the fourfold division of the aforementioned universal nature: namely, into that form or species (if the first cause of all, which surpasses all form and species while being the unformed principle of all forms and species, ought rightly to be called a form or species) which **creates and is not created**; moreover, we call God the "unformed principle" lest anyone should think He is counted in the number of forms, while He is the cause of all forms; for every formed thing seeks Him, since He is through Himself infinite and more than infinite, for He is the infinity of all infinities. Because, therefore, He is confined or defined by no form, since He is known by no intellect, He is more rationally called "unformed" than "formed"; because, as has often been said, we can more truly predicate something of God through negation than through affirmation. The **second** is into that which **is created and creates**; the **third** follows, which **is created and does not create**; then the **fourth**, which **neither creates nor is created**. D. It was indeed divided so. M. Since we have already spoken briefly in the prior disputation about the oppositions of the aforementioned forms of nature—for we considered how the third results from the first, since both look toward each other from opposite regions as if across a diameter; for the "created and not creating," as we said, is opposed to the "creating and not-created"; similarly the second form looks from the opposite side toward the fourth; for the "created and creating" is opposed to the "neither created nor creating"—we say that universal nature has "forms" because our intelligence is in a certain way "informed" <note>Given shape or structure</note> by it while it strives to treat of it; for by itself universal nature does not receive forms everywhere, since we say not incongruously that it is contained by God and Creature, and through this, insofar as it is the creatrix <note>The female form of "creator," referring to the creative power of Nature</note>, it receives no form in itself,

<summary>This page concludes Book One by arguing that God is beyond human categories of action and being, and begins Book Two by re-introducing the famous fourfold division of Nature (God as creator, primordial causes, created effects, and God as the final end).</summary>
<keywords>Eriugena, fourfold division of nature, apophatic theology, Gregory the Nazianzen, creator and creature, unformed principle, Neoplatonism</keywords>
