<meta>This page continues the dialogue between the Master (M) and the Disciple (D) regarding the four divisions of nature. It follows the initial definitions of the four forms: that which creates and is not created (God as source), that which is created and creates (primordial causes), that which is created and does not create (the physical world), and that which neither creates nor is created (God as end).</meta>

# ON THE DIVISION OF NATURE

->The diversity of nature formed by itself is presented; now I think we must consider their similarities and differences.<- **D.** The order of things demands nothing less.

**M.** The second form is similar to the first in that it creates, but it differs from it in that it is created; for the first creates and is not created, while the second both creates and is created. The third draws a similarity to the second in that it is created, but differs from it in that it creates nothing; for the second is both created and creates, while the third is created and does not create. The third is similar to the fourth in that it does not create, but similar <note>The text likely means "different" here in logic, though the Latin says "similis" (similar); the Master is contrasting their creation status.</note> in that it is created; for the third is created and does not create, while the fourth neither creates nor is created. Likewise, the fourth is similar to the first because it is not created, but it appears to differ from it because it does not create; for the first creates and is not created, while the fourth neither creates nor is created. And since we have spoken of the oppositions, similarities, and differences, I see that we must speak briefly of their return and collection through that discipline which Philosophers call **Analytics**.

**D.** Order demands this as well; for there is no rational division—whether of essence into Genera, or of Genus into forms and numbers, or of a whole into parts (which is properly called partition), or of the universe into those things which true reason contemplates within it—which cannot be led back again through the same steps by which the division was first multiplied, until one arrives at that One remaining inseparably within itself, from which the division itself took its beginnings. But first, explain a few things about the Etymology of the name itself, which is **Analytics**, for it is not entirely clear to me why it is necessary.

**M.** **Analytics** is derived from the verb *analyo* <note>original: "analyo" (Greek: ἀναλύω)</note>, that is, "I resolve" or "I return"; for *ana* means "back" <note>original: "re"</note>, and *lyo* is interpreted as "I loosen" <note>original: "solvo"</note>, and from this the name *analyo* is born, which is similarly translated as "resolution" or "return." But **Analysis** is properly used for the dissolving of proposed questions, while **Analytics** refers to the return of the division of forms to the principle of that same division. For every division, which is called *merismos* <note>original: "merismos" (Greek: μερισμός)</note> by the Greeks, is seen as if descending downwards from a certain defined one to infinite numbers; that is, from the most general down to the most specific. But every recollection, like a certain return starting again from the most specific and ascending up to the most general, is called **Analytics**. It is, therefore, the return and resolution of individuals into forms, forms into genera, genera into *ousias* <term>ousia: being or substance</term>, and *ousias* into wisdom and prudence, from which all division arises and in which it is finished.

**D.** Enough has been said of the Etymology of **Analytics**; proceed to the rest.

2. **M.** Therefore, by joining the four aforementioned forms into pairs, let there be an **Analytical**—that is, a returning—collection. For the first and the fourth are one, since they are understood only in relation to God; for He is the beginning of all things which have been established by Him, and the end of all things which seek Him, so that they may rest in Him eternally and immutably. Indeed, the Cause of all is said to "create" because from it the universe of those things, which were created by it after it, proceeds in a certain wonderful and divine multiplication into genera and species and numbers, as well as differences and the other things considered in created nature. But because all things that proceed from that cause will return to it when they reach the end, it is therefore called the "end of all," and is said "neither to create nor be created." For after all things have returned into it, nothing further shall proceed from it through generation in place and time, in genera and forms, because in it all things will be at rest, and will remain one individual and immutable whole. For those things which in the processions of natures appear to be divided and partitioned in many ways, are united and are one in the primordial causes; returning to this unity, they shall remain in it eternally and immutably. But concerning this fourth consideration of the universe, which is understood in God alone (just as the first is), we shall dispute more broadly in its proper place, as much as the light of our minds shall grant. Moreover, that which is said of the first and the fourth—that is, that neither this one nor that one is created—is because this one and that one are one, for both are predicated of God. I think this is obscure to no one who understands rightly, for He is created by no one, because He lacks a cause superior to Himself or equal to Himself; for God is the first cause of all, whom nothing precedes, and nothing is understood alongside Him that is not co-essential to Him. Do you see, then, that the first and fourth forms of nature have been recalled into one?

**D.** I see it sufficiently and understand it clearly; for in God the first form is not distinguished from the fourth. In Him, indeed, there are not two but one; yet in our theory, while we conceive of one reason concerning God according to the consideration of the beginning, and another according to the contemplation of the end, they appear to be like two forms, shaped from one and the same simplicity of the divine nature on account of the twofold intention of our contemplation.

**M.** You see rightly; what then? Should we not similarly [join] both the second and the third into one—

<summary>The Master explains "Analytics" as the process of returning divided forms of nature back to their unified source in God. He demonstrates that the first and fourth divisions of nature are actually one, representing God as both the Beginning and the End of all things.</summary>
<keywords>Analytics, Division of Nature, Neoplatonism, God as Beginning and End, Etymology, Merismos, Ousia, Resolution, Return of all things</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between the Master (M) and the Disciple (D) regarding the fourfold division of Nature. It transitions from the general overview of the divisions to a specific focus on the second division: that which is created and creates (the primordial causes).</meta>

# BOOK TWO. 47.

into what [forms] ought we to reduce them? For it does not escape you, I think, that just as the first and fourth forms are rationally understood in the Creator, so the second and third are understood in the Creature. For the second, as was said before, is both created and creates, and is understood in the primordial causes <note>original: "primordialibus causis" — the fundamental blueprints or first principles of all things</note> of created things; but the third form is created and does not create, and is found in the effects of the primordial causes. Thus, the second and third are contained within one and the same genus—namely, created nature—and are one within it; for forms are one within their genus. Do you not see, then, that of the four aforementioned forms, two (the first and fourth) are gathered into the Creator, while two (the second and third) are gathered into the Creature?

**D.** I see it clearly, and I greatly admire the subtlety of these things. For those two forms [the first and fourth] are not distinguished in God himself, but in our contemplation; they are forms of our reason rather than of God, due to our twofold consideration of Him as Beginning and End. They are not reduced to a single form in God, but in our theory <note>original: "theoria" — from the Greek for contemplation or spiritual vision</note>, which, while it considers the Beginning and the End, creates two forms of contemplation within itself. It then seems to reduce these again into one form of theory when it begins to treat the simple unity of the divine nature. For "Beginning" and "End" are not proper names of the divine nature, but of its relationship to those things which have been created. For they begin from Him, and therefore He is called the Beginning; and because they are terminated in Him so that they may find rest in Him, He deserves to be called by the name "End." But the other two forms—the second and third—are not only generated in our contemplation but are also found in the very nature of created things, in which causes are separated from effects and effects are joined to causes, since they are one in a single genus: the Creature.

**M.** Therefore, out of four, there become two.

**D.** I do not resist that.

**M.** What if you were to join the Creature to the Creator, so that you understand nothing else in the Creature except Him who alone truly is? For nothing outside of Him is truly called "essential," because all things that come from Him are nothing else, insofar as they exist, but a participation in Him who alone subsists from Himself and through Himself. Would you then deny that Creator and Creature are one?

**D.** I would not easily deny it; for it seems to me ridiculous to resist such a gathering together.

**M.** Therefore the Universe, in which God and Creature are contained—previously divided into four forms, as it were—is recalled again to one individual principle, which is both the cause and the end.

**D.** I see that enough has been said for now regarding the universal division and unification of universal nature; for we shall consider the subdivisions of the individual forms in their proper places—I mean the second and the third. I would not easily say that the first and fourth admit subdivisions, and therefore I think we should move on to other things that must now be said.

**M.** How does it seem to you? Should we take any other path for the investigation of the proposed questions than to seek now the second form of nature? For we considered the first in the previous book as far as our ability allowed—though in that book we disputed more about the ten categories <note>original: "decem rerum omnium generibus" — referring to Aristotle's Categories</note> of all things (showing they cannot properly be said of the creative cause of all) than about the Cause of all itself. For we judged that what must be said of that Cause should be reserved for the consideration of the fourth form. Not that we promise to say anything there concerning the Divine Nature properly or worthily, but that we shall speak of the return of all things into it through the primordial causes, as much as the Nature itself grants. But in this second Question, by God's bounty, the purpose of our intention is chiefly set: to say something about the procession <note>original: "processione" — the flowing out of creation from God</note> of Creatures from the one and first Cause of all through the primordial essences of the causes—created before all things by it, in it, and through it—into the diverse Genera of things and the diverse forms and numbers unto infinity. And do not wonder if you see certain things said in this place about the return of creatures to their beginning and end; for the procession of Creatures and their return occur simultaneously to the reason inquiring into them, so that they seem inseparable from one another. No one can explain anything worthy or established concerning one absolutely without the insertion of the other—that is, concerning procession without return and gathering, and vice versa.

**D.** The sequence of the disputation requires nothing else than that, after the consideration of the first question, we pass to the second.

**M.** Therefore, as has been said, that second form of universal nature shines forth, which is created and creates; and it is to be understood, I think, only in the primordial causes of things. These primordial causes of things are called by the Greeks *Prototypes* <note>original: "Prototypa"</note>, that is, primordial examples; or *predestinations* <note>original: "proorismata"</note>, that is, predestinations or definitions. Likewise, they are called by the same people *Divine Wills* <note>original: "Theia-thelemata"</note>; they are also usually called *Ideas* <note>original: "Ideæ"</note>, that is, species or forms, in which the immutable reasons of all things to be made were created before they existed. Concerning these, more extensively in the progress [of the work]...

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the division of nature, specifically moving from the general definition of the four species of nature toward the "primordial causes" (the divine blueprints of all things). The author is preparing to reconcile his own system with that of the Greek Church Father, Maximus the Confessor.</meta>

# ON THE DIVISION OF NATURE

<note>original: "DE DIVISIONE NATURÆ"</note>

...we shall speak in the progress of this work, and we shall strengthen our arguments with the testimonies of the holy Fathers. And it is not without merit that they are so named, since the Father—that is, the Beginning of all things—preformed the <term>reasons</term><note>The "rationes" or "Logoi": the eternal divine ideas or blueprints for all created things.</note> of all things he willed to be made within his Word (namely, the only-begotten Son) before they passed into Genera and Species, numbers and differences, and all other things which can be considered in the established creation—whether they are perceived because of their nature, or remain unperceived due to their great depth, yet truly exist.

3. But before we arrive at the theory of the <term>primordial causes</term><note>The primary causes or "prototypes" created by God, which then produce the physical world.</note>, it seemed right to me to insert into this discussion the opinion of the Venerable Maximus <note>Saint Maximus the Confessor (c. 580–662), a prominent Greek theologian whose work the author translated from Greek to Latin.</note> concerning the Division of all things that have been made. For if the manifold division of all things, and likewise their manifold unification, is demonstrated, the path toward the knowledge of the primary causes—which were principally established by God—will appear more clearly. This is especially true since his division seems to differ in no way from our own, except that he subdivides the "sensible creation" (which we placed as the third form of nature because the effects of the causes appear most clearly in it) into three species. Furthermore, he does not distinguish our fourth form from the first, as the following sections will show; for we shall reserve the subdivision of the sensible Creation for the subject of our third inquiry, as God grants. In the thirty-seventh chapter of his *Difficulties* <note>The "Ambigua ad Iohannem," a work by Maximus explaining difficult passages in the sermons of Gregory of Nazianzus.</note>, he says that the Saints—who received the knowledge of things through a succession of those who went before them, having learned directly from those who were the followers and ministers of the Word—declare that the substance of all things that are made is separated by five divisions. Do you see with what great authority he commends the origin of his division? For he records that it began from the Word itself—that is, the Son of God, who is the source of all perfect teaching (rightly so, because he is the Wisdom in which all wise men participate through his followers, the disciples who saw him living in the flesh and heard him teaching). He notes that this was handed down to the successors of the Apostles in doctrine and wisdom immediately—that is, with no other authority intervening—down to his own time.

*The first of these divisions, he says, is that by which nature is divided universally into the uncreated nature and the created nature, which receives its being through generation. For they say that God, through his goodness, made a clear arrangement of all existing things at once. And a little later: The second division is that by which all nature, receiving its being from God through creation, is divided into the intelligible <note>The realm of pure thought and angels.</note> and the sensible <note>The physical realm perceived by the senses.</note>. Then the third, by which sensible nature itself is divided into heaven and earth. Likewise the fourth, by which the Earth is divided into Paradise and the inhabited world. And the fifth, by which man himself—who is, as it were, a most continuous workshop of all things, naturally creating a middle point through himself between all extremes through every difference—is added to those things which are well and beautifully made according to generation. Man is divided into Male and Female, possessing naturally the power of unifying all extremes through the middle points, having a property in his own parts that connects him to all extremes. For one part of himself, as the same Master says elsewhere, he possesses with sensible things insofar as he is a body; and a part with Intelligible things insofar as he is a soul; and therefore he contains the Universal Creation within himself. And a little later: For this reason, man is introduced last among the things that exist, as a kind of natural conjunction making a middle point between extremes through his own parts, and leading into one within himself things that are distant from one another by a great space according to nature.* By "extremes" here he means the invisible and sensible Creation, which naturally differ from each other as if by a very long distance. Just as there are two extreme boundaries of created natures opposed to one another, human nature provides a middle ground for them; for in man they are joined to one another, and from many they become one.

4. For there is no Creature, from the highest to the lowest, which is not found in man. Thus he is rightly called the "workshop of all things"; for in him all things created by God flow together, composing a single harmony from diverse natures, like different sounds. This was done so that, starting from his own division and proceeding through the connection and order of the middle parts, he might receive his end in God—who is the cause that gathers all things into unity—and that the high ascent of all things made through unity might be completed in Him, in whom there is no division. Thus far we have introduced the words of the aforementioned Venerable Master, not in a continuous flow, but with certain parts omitted; he begins the division of the substance of things made from the highest cause of all, and ends the differences of universal substance in man, who was made in the image and likeness of the God. For man, as we have said and shall very often repeat, was created with such dignity of nature that there is no Creature, whether visible or invisible, that cannot be found in him.

5. For he is made of two universal parts of created nature by a certain wonderful uni-

<meta>catchword: uni- (aduna-)</meta>

# SECOND BOOK

->G<-

composed of a unification; for he is joined from the sensible <note>perceivable by the senses</note> and the intelligible <note>perceivable by the intellect</note>, that is, from the very extremities of the whole of creation. There is indeed nothing lower in the nature of things than the body, and nothing higher than the intellect, as Saint Augustine testifies in his book *On True Religion*, saying: *Between our mind, by which we understand the Father himself, and the truth through which we understand him, there is no intervening creature.* <note>original Latin: "Inter mentem nostram... nulla interposita creatura est."</note> By these words of the most holy Father, it is given to be understood that human nature, even after its transgression <note>the Fall of Man</note>, has not utterly lost its dignity, but still retains it. For the Master did not say there *was* no intervening creature, but there *is* none: therefore, even in our weaknesses, we have not entirely deserted God, nor have we been deserted by him, since between our mind and him there is no intervening nature; for the leprosy of the soul or the body does not take away the sharpness of the mind by which we understand him, and in which the image of the creator was chiefly established. Do you see, then, how the division of all substances terminates in human nature?

**Student:** I see it clearly, and I greatly admire the dignity of our nature among all things that were made, while I perceive in it, as it were, a certain wonderful composition of all substantial creatures, being persuaded by the aforementioned reasons.

**Master:** Therefore, since you clearly see the natural division of all things, beginning from the Creator and the Creature, and ending in man—who was established at the summit of divine operation as if in a certain perfection of the number six <note>referring to the six days of creation</note>—let us now look at the unified collection of all substances beginning from man, and ascending through man even unto God himself, who is the beginning of all division and the end of all unification, by considering the words of the aforementioned Father Maximus <note>Maximus the Confessor, a 7th-century theologian</note>.

6. For, as we said before, right reason does not permit one to treat Division absolutely and omit Analysis <note>the process of returning or unifying</note>, but commands us to consult the truth by connecting both. He begins, therefore, from Division itself, as if he were saying openly: since the division of substances which took its beginning from God, and descending step by step, established its end in the Division of Man into male and female; so again the unification of those same substances ought to begin from Man, and ascend through those same steps even unto God himself: in whom, as he says, there is no division, because in him all things are one. The unification of natures will therefore begin from man, through the grace of the Savior, in whom, as it is said, *there is no longer male nor female*, when human nature is restored to its pristine state. For if the first man had not sinned, he would not have suffered the partitioning of nature into a double sex, but would have remained immutably in his primordial reasons <note>the original divine ideas or blueprints</note> in which he was established in the image of God; which the aforementioned Master reveals in these words: he says, for the sake of this thing, the last man is introduced (*among those things that are*), *in no way depending against the primordial reason of that same reason concerning the generation of man of the divine purpose according to the property of male and female. In that most impassible union concerning divine power, everywhere extending through Nature.* So that he might show that the future man, according to the divine purpose, was not to be divided solely according to the naming of male and female; by which reason he was also first made; not divided in those sections now existing around him because of that perfect unification to the proper reason through which he subsists. In these words he most aptly suggests the purpose of the divine counsel regarding the condition of man, if he had not foreknown that he would sin: for man would have been created solely in the simplicity of his nature, and multiplied in that way in which the Holy Angels are multiplied by intellectual numbers; but overwhelmed by the guilt of his transgression, he suffered the division of his nature into male and female; and since he would not observe the divine mode of his multiplication, he was reduced by a just judgment to the cattle-like and corruptible numerousness arising from male and female. This division took its beginning of unification in Christ, who truly showed in himself the example of the restoration of human nature, and provided the likeness of the future resurrection.

7. In those things, he says, *not divided by the sections which are now around him*; he calls "sections around man" those things after sin, not only the division into male and female, but also into the manifold varieties of qualities and quantities, and the differences of a single form; for the diversity of men from one another, by which the species of each one is discerned from others, and the measure of stature is varied, does not proceed from nature, but from vice, and the diversity of places and times, lands, waters, airs, foods, and other similar things in which they are born and nourished. Of the diversity of manners and thoughts, it is superfluous to speak, since it is manifest to all that it took its beginning from the Division of Nature after sin. *Because of that, he says, perfect unification to the proper reason—*

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the restoration of human nature and the cosmic unification of all things, following the philosophical framework of John Scottus Eriugena.</meta>

# ON THE DIVISION OF NATURE

...the essence through which he exists: as if he were to say; man would not suffer the division of his nature if he did not sin, because he would inseparably adhere to his primordial causes <note>original: "primordialibus causis" — the primary patterns or ideas in the mind of God from which all things flow</note>—namely, his reasons <note>original: "rationibus" — the divine blueprints or logos of a being</note> in which he was created according to the image of God—if he did not voluntarily tear himself away from them. For man does not consist in these things in which he now appears to be, but is contained, insofar as he exists, in the hidden causes of nature according to which he was first created, and to which he is destined to return.

8. And since after the unification of man—that is, of the two sexes into the original unity of nature, in which there was neither male nor female, but simply man—there immediately follows the unification of the world with Paradise; then, he says, *joining Paradise and the inhabited world through his own holy way of life, he would make one earth not divided in him and the difference of its parts, but rather gathered together so that none of its parts suffer any removal.* This sentence seems very obscure, for it is not easily apparent what he wishes to argue: whether the world, while all substances are being unified, will be joined to paradise so that no diversity of parts occurs in them, but the whole will be paradise; for in the logic of unification it is always necessary to observe that what appears to be lower is moved into that which is higher, that is, better; but true reason concludes that the better does not pass into the worse—that is, to return in the renewal of natures (otherwise it will not be a unification but a division). For in this way the unification of human nature recalls the division of the two sexes into the simplicity of "man": because "man" is better than "sex." Indeed, male and female are not names of nature, but of its partitioning through transgression <note>original: "praevaricationem" — the Fall of Adam and Eve</note>: but "man" is the specific name of the nature itself. Or will the world and paradise be applied into that one earth, which is the other species of sensible creature divided into heaven and earth, so that the earth itself not only lacks a variety of parts, but is also recalled into a certain simplicity of nature, so that it is believed to be more spiritual than corporeal, as it would have been if man had not sinned? And let us understand what he said; *not segregated in him*, that is, in man. For in man all things are unified according to the difference of their parts, namely, of the earth. So that just as there will be no diversity of parts in man, since no composition <note>original: "compositio" — the state of being made of different material parts</note> will remain in him, so also the earthly nature will be joined to itself, so that it appears to be one in him and with him: for he says *but rather gathered together; so that it suffers no removal of its parts*, that is, separation. For where there is true simplicity, there no variety of removal—namely, segregation—is found; the following words seem most aptly to favor this sense: for it follows *then joining heaven and earth through that very likeness to the life and virtues of the Angels, as much as is possible for men, to make the sensible creature one and inseparable even from itself, not separated in him, namely man; entirely in local spaces, the spirit having been made light and held back by no weight, nor prohibited from ascending to the Heavens, through this to overcome to a perfect invisibility <note>original: "ἀορασίαν" (aorosian) — Eriugena uses the Greek term for "unseeing" or "invisibility" to describe a state beyond physical sight</note> of the mind, that is, an out-stepping or blindness, of one reaching toward God by a wise intention toward him.* Where it is given to be understood that man, when he shall be recalled into the original grace of his nature, which he deserted by transgressing, will collect every sensible creature and those lower than himself into unity for himself, by a certain miraculous power of divine virtue, in the restoration of man: for while the human mind, as he says, reaches toward God to overcome, then it will suffer a divine *invisibility* <note>original: "ἀορασίας" (aorosias)</note>, that is, an ignorance of all creatures, which it will entirely transcend, reaching toward God himself, and contemplating him in all things. For the whole man, as he says, is to be a light spirit, and will be held back on earth by no corporeal weight, nor will he be prohibited from ascending to the Heavens. Then, he says, *coupling the intelligible and the sensible with these, that is, with the aforesaid unifications of natures, through this he will unify the creature to an equality of knowledge with the angels, at once every creature not separately in him according to knowledge and ignorance, being indifferently equal to the angels, by a gnostic knowledge <note>original: "gnosticâ scientiâ" — spiritual or intuitive knowledge</note> of the reasons in those things which exist; through which the very infinite outpouring of the gifts of true wisdom coming upon it, as much as is lawful, purely provides for it thereafter an unknown and uninterpretable knowledge concerning God and immediately for those who are worthy.* The depth of this sentence seems incomprehensible to me, and especially where he says, *and according to knowledge and ignorance by a gnostic knowledge equal to the angels.* And rightly so, for in the earlier parts he seemed to have argued nothing else but a unification of all sensible and intelligible things into a certain unity, so that nothing separable, nothing corporeal, nothing varied remains in them; but that by a certain miraculous regression the lower things always pass into the higher. But now he seems to say that the unification of substantial natures is in the intellect alone, and not in the things themselves, that is, not those things which through generation...

Eriugena discusses how man will eventually transcend physical weight and diversity to achieve an angelic state of knowledge and "invisibility" before God.</summary>

# SECOND BOOK

into diverse genera, diverse forms, and infinite numbers according to the Creator’s providence, they received an intelligible or sensible variety. For we do not understand the unification of created substances in the angels in any other way except through gnostic knowledge <note>original: "gnosticâ scientiâ"; here meaning spiritual or contemplative insight</note> alone, and not by a physical gathering of the things themselves into one: for they know the reasons <note>original: "rationes"; the divine blueprints or 'logoi'</note> of all created natures within themselves under a single continuous stretch of intelligence. Just as in a very wise human soul, there is one and the same inseparable knowledge of diverse arts; so that ignorance—whether in Angels (for we read that some of them are not yet purged of all ignorance) or in men—causes no segregation of things; rather, gnostic knowledge becomes the cause of unification. *For the understanding of things is truly the things themselves*, as Saint Dionysius <note>Dionysius the Areopagite, a highly influential mystical theologian</note> says: *The knowledge of those things which are, is the things themselves: but their primordial causes and reasons are gathered into a certain unification by the action of the intelligence, and not in the physical thing itself.* But before we arrive at the theory of this question, if anything is to be said of it, I judge that the remaining points concerning the aforementioned unification of things according to Maximus <note>Maximus the Confessor, a 7th-century theologian</note> must be stated. *And the end,* he says, *in all these is unifying the created nature to the uncreated through love. O miracle of divine mercy toward us, that the whole man shall be shown to be one and the same thing according to the habit of grace as God, who universally encompasses the whole, and is so made. For everything is said to be because of a likeness according to essence, and receiving the whole itself; namely, from God himself a God, as the end of those things which are moved, and the firm and immutable state of those things which are carried toward Him, being the end of every end, and of statute, and of law, and of reason and intellect, and the infinite and unending end and limit of nature.*

9. To this fifth point, therefore, it is given to be understood from the discourses of the aforementioned Master <note>Maximus</note>, that man was made in the image of God among the primordial causes of things: so that in him every creature, both intelligible and sensible—of which he is composed as if from divided extremities—might become one inseparable thing, and that he might be the mediation and unification of all creatures. For there is no creature that cannot be understood in man: whence also in the Holy Scriptures "every creature" is usually named; for it is written in the Gospel, preaching the Gospel *to every creature*; likewise in the Apostle <note>St. Paul</note>, *Every creature groans and suffers until now.* And if he had not sinned, there would be no division of sexes in him, but he would be only "man"; the world would not be separated from Paradise in him, but all earthly nature in him would be paradise; that is, a spiritual way of life. Heaven and Earth would not be segregated in him, for he would be entirely celestial, and nothing earthly, nothing heavy, nothing corporeal would appear in him; for he would exist and be multiplied into the number predefined by his Creator, just as the angels both are and have been multiplied. Sensible nature would not differ from intelligible nature in him, for he would be entirely intellect, adhering always and immutably to his Creator, and in no way would he recede from his primordial causes in which he was created: Every creature which was created in him would suffer no division in him. But because the first man neglected to remain in such happiness, and fell from it through pride; and the unity of human nature was dispersed into infinite partitions and varieties, divine mercy assumed a "new man" <note>Christ</note>, in whom that very nature which was dispersed in the "old man" might be recalled to its pristine unity: He ordained that He be born in the world, from the world—that is, a man from men for the sake of men. Hence the same Maximus says: *Since therefore,* he says, *man did not move naturally as he was created around his own immutable principle, I mean God: but moved foolishly against nature toward those things which are beneath him, over which he was divinely ordained to rule: he abused the natural power given to him for the unification of separated things, rather into the separation of those things to be unified through generation; natures are renewed, and God becomes man, so that he may save lost man; and joining the fissures in the whole of nature which are against nature, and universally showing the pre-demonstrated reasons of those things which were partitioned; by which unification itself used to become a separator, showing that he fulfills the great counsel of God and the Father, recapitulating all things in himself, that is, recollecting those things which are in heaven and those which are on earth; in whom also they were created. For by the universal unification of all things to himself, beginning from our diversion, he becomes the perfect man, from us, for us, according to us, having all our things perfect; not from a nuptial consequence; showing by this, as I judge, how there was another mode of propagation of men into a multitude foreknown by God, if the first man had kept the commandment: and he would not have cast himself down into that beast-like state by the abuse of his own powers, and been thrust back into the difference of male and female and the division of nature. To become which, as I said, man had no absolute need; and those things without which it is perhaps possible to exist, it is not necessary that these remain forever: for in Christ Jesus, says the divine Apostle, there is neither male nor female.*

# ON THE DIVISION OF NATURE

10. Do you see how much he emphasizes that the cause of the division of human nature must be traced back to the sin of the first man? For without these things <note>referring to the divisions of sex and mortality</note>, he says, it is perhaps possible to exist, and it is not necessary for these to remain forever. It is as if he were saying openly: since if man had not sinned, he could have remained without the division of his nature; for this did not come to him from nature, but happened because of transgression. What necessity, therefore, compels that division to remain after the reunification of nature into its former dignity? For just as nature was able to lack division before sin, so it will be able to remain without it eternally after its restoration. But lest we follow his words one by one—in which he most clearly teaches the reunification of the whole creation, which would have happened in the first man if he had not sinned, and was accomplished in the rising Christ before all and through all—I will introduce a few points out of many for the sake of the present discussion. First, therefore, he teaches that the Lord Jesus unified the division of nature—that is, male and female—in himself: for he rose from the dead not in a bodily sex, but only as man; for in him there is neither male nor female. Although he appeared to his disciples after the resurrection in that very male sex in which he was born of the Virgin and in which he suffered, this was to confirm the faith in his resurrection; for they would not have recognized him otherwise if they did not see the form known to them; for they had not yet fully received the Holy Spirit, who taught them all things. Then, after the resurrection, he joined our world to Paradise in himself: for returning from the dead into Paradise, he conversed in this world with his disciples: showing them clearly that Paradise is nothing other than the glory of the resurrection, which first appeared in him and which he is going to give to all his faithful; and teaching that our world does not have a difference from Paradise according to the principle of nature: for they are not separated by nature, but by qualities, quantities, and other variations which were added to this habitable earth because of the general sin of general human nature, for its punishment, or rather for its correction and exercise. And since the earth is one when it is established in itself, it is inseparable, saving that very principle of nature in which it exists, free from division according to difference; for Paradise is not distinguished from this habitable world by mass or by spaces, but by the diversity of life and the difference of blessedness; for even the first man, if he had not sinned, could have lived happily in the world: since there is one and the same principle in the primary causes of the world and of Paradise, which our God most aptly manifested in himself after the resurrection: for he was simultaneously in Paradise and stayed on this earth with his disciples.

11. For it is not to be believed that he came from elsewhere locally to appear to his disciples, and withdrew elsewhere when he did not appear, since after the resurrection he had surpassed the nature of places and times not only in his divinity but also in his humanity; (indeed, I would not easily believe that spiritual bodies are confined by place and time: just as they are not varied by qualities or quantities, or descriptions of any form; for they are of a most simple nature, which is best gathered from the argument of pure fire, which, while it is diffused through all bodies of the sensible world, is of such subtlety that it is held in no place, and yet is known to manifest its operation in all things,) but at one and the same time, in one and the same place, he appeared for a time in that form in which he had suffered, so that he might nourish their faith, until they were illuminated by the power of true knowledge with which they were afterwards filled: and the space of the momentary appearance having passed, he soon returned to the intellectual and invisible <term>invisibility</term> <note>original: "ἀορασίαν" (aorasia)</note> of the spiritual body, which, as we said before, surpasses all time and all place: or, what seems more credible, in no way departing from the glory of the resurrection in place or time, or quantity or quality (since no one doubts that spiritual bodies lack all these things), he manifested himself to his disciples after the resurrection in that form by which he lived in the world for the salvation of the world: for human reasoning does not prevail where divine power alone is mighty: for what he himself says, *Behold, I am with you all days even to the end of the age*, indicates sufficiently and clearly that he is always and everywhere, not only according to the Word, by which he fills all things and is above all things, but also according to the flesh, which he took into the unity of his substance or person, and raised from the dead, and transformed into God—yet not locally or temporally, nor in any way circumscribed; for in a wonderful and ineffable way he is both above all celestial essences and with the Father according to the humanity which was united to the Word; and he is God and rules the world everywhere, and appears to those who love him, whether invisibly or visibly; he does not desert heaven and he rules the world;

# BOOK TWO.

->He rules; he sits at the right hand of the Father, governing all things in the highest realms, while ministering the salvation of human nature in the lowest: do not, therefore, imagine the humanity of Christ to be confined to a specific place, for after the resurrection it was transformed into divinity. The divinity of Christ is not in a place, and therefore neither is his humanity: the same is certainly understood regarding time, quantity, quality, and circumscribed form. For the whole humanity of Christ—that is, body, soul, and intellect—is most piously believed and most purely understood to be free from all these things after the palm of the resurrection; and he shall lead his elect to the same glory after the Universal <note>original: "Catholicam"</note> resurrection, who are to be one in him and with him. Christ, therefore, was simultaneously in paradise and in the world, showing that the natural principle of both the world and paradise is one, uniting the world and paradise in himself. For true reason does not consider or honor the corporeal masses in the world, distended by spaces, nor the manifold varieties of its diverse parts: but rather its natural and primordial causes <note>The "primordial causes" are the divine ideas or patterns through which God creates the world, a central concept in Eriugena's thought.</note> which are united in themselves and most beautiful; into these, when their end has come, the world shall return and remain eternally. We do not believe there will be any other end for this sensible world except a return into God and into its primordial causes, in which it naturally subsists.<-

12. For as reason teaches, this world would not have broken out into various and sensible species, and the diverse multiplicities of its parts, if God had not foreseen the fall and ruin of the first man, who deserted the unity of his nature: so that at least after his ruin—from spiritual things to corporeal, from eternal to temporal, from incorruptible to decaying, from the highest to the lowest, from a spiritual man into an animal one, from a simple nature to the division of sexes, from Angelic dignity and multiplication to a cattle-like, shameful, and corruptible generation according to the body—he might recognize his miserable destruction, being warned by such punishment: and might seek to return to the former state of his dignity by repenting, laying aside his pride, and fulfilling the divine laws which he had transgressed. For it is not to be believed that the mercy of the most divine Creator thrust sinning man into this world as if moved by some indignation, or eager for some movement of vengeance (for true reason indicates that the divine goodness is free from such accidents), but rather by a certain method of ineffable teaching and incomprehensible mercy: so that since man, by the choice of his will, had refused to keep himself in the dignity of his nature, he might seek the grace of his Creator, having been instructed by his own punishments; and through that grace, becoming obedient to the divine precepts which he had previously neglected through pride, he might return—cautious and provident, humble and mindful of the fall caused by his former negligence and pride—to his original state: from which, once more through grace and the guarded choice of his free will, he would not fall, nor wish to fall, nor be able to fall. But let us return to the unification of natures in Christ. Then, says the aforementioned Master <note>Likely referring to Maximus the Confessor, whom Eriugena frequently quotes.</note>, *through his assumption into heaven, he surely gave life to heaven and earth, and with this earthly body connatural to us, changed indeed into a spiritual substance, returning into heaven, he manifested all sensible nature as being one: then he established sensible and intelligible things; consequently passing through all the celestial, divine, and intellectual orders, he united them with soul and body, that is, our perfect nature; showing the meeting of the whole creature according to its most principal principle, perfectly inseparable and immovable in himself; and the end in all these things according to the understanding of humanity—that is, according to humanity itself—appearing before God for us: as it is written, before the face of God and the Father, insofar as he is man, who can never in any way recede from the Father insofar as he is the Word; and first he united us to ourselves in himself by the removal of the difference according to male and female, and instead of men and women, in whom the mode of division is most clearly seen: he showed men only, properly and truly bearing the image which the marks of corruption can in no way touch.* Do you see how clearly he announces that man, created in the image and likeness of God, was entirely without the difference of sex, and still lacks it insofar as the image and likeness of the Creator remains in him; and that this division happened according to the body alone because of sin: for although spiritual sexes are understood in the soul—since <term>nous</term> <note>Greek: νοῦς</note>, that is, the intellect, is like a male element in the soul, while <term>aisthesis</term> <note>Greek: αἴσθησις</note>, that is, sense, is like a female—nevertheless we do not recognize there a divorce of nature, but the mystery of Christ and the church, and the striving of a single intellect toward the creator and the creature. *And with us*, he says, *and for our sake, at the same time encompassing the whole creature through the middle points as if they were the extremes of its own parts, he joined paradise and the world together insolubly around himself, heaven and earth, sensible and intelligible things: for having a body and sense and soul, according to our nature, and an intellect, by which as if by parts through each thing universally*

associating the related extreme, according to the previously rendered motion, he <note>Christ</note> has divinely recapitulated all things in himself.

13. Examine the power of these words more intently, for they are not devoid of virtue; for he says that Christ, having a body, sense, and soul according to our nature, and also an intellect: for by these four parts, as it were, human nature is constituted. These parts Christ, as a true man, both assumed and united in himself; for he is a perfect man: for he left nothing of man—except for sin—that he did not receive into the unity of his substance and did not unite in himself, that is, make one. For in him after the resurrection there are not four, but one, and not a composite, but simply one body, and sense, soul, and intellect; to which, he says, as if to parts, he associates the universally related extreme to each one individually. It is as if he were saying openly: since Christ took the four parts of human nature and united them in himself, it is manifest that he assumed the entire creation—that is, the intellectual and the sensible—and united it in himself.

For in man, whom he took in his entirety, the whole of creation was founded; for by associating the related extreme to the individual parts, as he himself says, calling the "related extremes" the bodies and all visible things: for these hold the extremity of the whole creation looking downwards, and are related to one another. Looking upwards, however, all intellectual essences hold the other extremity of creation, which are similarly related among themselves. Therefore, to the individual parts of human nature, the related extremes—that is, the connatural natures—are associated in Christ. For by receiving the body as the fourth part of man, he joined to himself every corporeal creature. By assuming sense, he coupled to himself the entire sensible world and everything that uses sense. What shall be said of the soul? Did he not, in the soul which he received, associate in himself not only the rational soul, but also all nutritive and active life? Concerning the intellect, none of the wise doubt: for to the human intellect which Christ assumed, all intellectual essences inseparably adhere. Do you not clearly see every creature—I mean the intelligible, the sensible, and the intermediate natures—united in Christ? *Which the Master* <note>Maximus the Confessor</note> *concluded briefly, saying: showing the whole creation as one subsisting thing at once, like some man, embraced by the coming together of its parts with one another, and looking toward itself through the substance of the universe; according to a single, simple, and indivisible substance resulting from its deduction from that which is not, and an indifferent knowledge: through which one and the same thing every creature can receive a cause that is entirely indistinct.*

Thus far we have spoken concerning the divisions and the unifications of substances, following the venerable Master—I mean Maximus—as far as pertains to the business of the present question; and now, if it seems right to you, we should return to the proposed subject, that is, to discuss that form of universal nature which both is created and creates, and is constituted in the primordial causes, for we proposed to speak of this in the first book.

**Disciple.** It would certainly seem right, if you would explain more clearly certain things said in the aforementioned arguments (since they move me greatly).
**Master.** Tell me what those things are among what I have said that move you so much, and for which you seem to demand an explanation.

14. **Disciple.** In the divisions of created substances according to Maximus, nothing moves me, nor does it seem very obscure or ambiguous, except for the division of man into male and female; and that this proceeded not from the first condition of man in the image of God, but from the penalty of sin. And again, that after the Catholic <note>universal</note> resurrection of all bodies, which is to be at the end of the world, this division will no longer remain, but will return into the unity of the primordial nature. For who, hearing such things, would not be terrified, and immediately break out into these words: "Will there then be no male and female sex after the resurrection?" if both are to be entirely removed from human nature. But what kind of form will appear in man, if no one has the body of a male or a female? Or what kind of recognition can there be, if there is to be an extermination of both sexes, and a coagulation of all men into a simple and indistinct unification of different forms—whether spiritual and incorporeal, or visible and corporeal, circumscribed by place and time? And this was passed over by you in ambiguity and left undiscussed. Although you seem to suggest that in the bodies of the resurrection there will be spiritual quantities and qualities rather than corporeal ones; I will not say how much this doctrine resists the authority of all, or nearly all, the holy masters of the Latin tongue <note>The Latin Church Fathers</note>; who unanimously assert that after the resurrection there will be the integrity of both sexes: so that the man returns in the form of a man, and the woman in the form of a woman, with the marks of all the corporeal parts by which each sex is especially discerned, and so whether...

# BOOK TWO

<note>The speaker here is the Disciple, questioning the Teacher.</note>

...whether one remains eternally in glory or in punishment. However, if there is to be eternal punishment in a bodily sense, just as there is eternal glory—but we shall treat this question in the fifth book. Likewise, it seems inconsistent to many to believe that misery will be co-eternal with blessedness; the words of the Gospel seem to resist this, saying: <note>Matthew 25:41</note> *Depart, you cursed, into the eternal fire.* These matters, as I have said, regarding the aforementioned divisions of the substances created by God, cause me the greatest hesitation. 

As for the return and the unification, I confess that everything you have said so far is universally difficult and obscure to me. For I do not clearly see how the unification of both sexes of human nature was first accomplished in Christ when he rose from the dead, and how it will be in all people in the future world. For we believe that Christ rose in the same sex in which he lived in the flesh, and that he remains and will remain so forever. Who would deny that he was clothed in the male sex before he suffered and rose? Similarly, we believe that all people will rise again in the same sex in which they fall <note>i.e., die</note>. 

How, I ask, will the world be united to paradise, the earth to heaven, and every sensible creature to the intelligible, and all these things be joined to God so that they are one, and no diversity appears in them? And especially that which you promised to explain: how the return and unification of all the aforementioned substances into one will occur? Will it be in actual fact—that is, by the transition of all sensible things into intellectual things, so that when the course of this sensible world is finished, all things are spiritual and nothing corporeal or sensible remains? Or will it be only by the insight of the mind contemplating the natural unity of all things in their <term>primordial causes</term> <note>The original patterns or "ideas" of things in the mind of God</note>? 

For those things which appear to the bodily sense from the outside as various and manifold—differing in places, times, qualities, quantities, and other sensible distinctions of nature—seem to be, and are, a single individual whole to the pure intellect when it seeks, finds, and considers the truth of all things. This is because they subsist eternally in their "reasons" <note>Latin: rationibus; the underlying logic or blueprints</note> according to which they were established by the Creator of all, in the immutable state of their nature and the certain rules of divine providence. 

For as much as exterior natures (I mean the sensible and corporeal) seek to vary by places, times, and other <term>accidents</term> <note>Non-essential characteristics like color or size</note>—for in these their beauty is most attractive, and therefore they are loved immoderately and luxuriously by fleshly souls who are far from truth and unity and scattered into the multiplicity of temporal things—so much do the interior, that is, the intelligible essences, show a simple and individual unity within themselves and among themselves inseparably. Through this, they delight the minds of the wise who contemplate the beauty of their harmony and fellowship. To those returning from the unstable and diverse variety of places and times, the contemplation of unity alone and immutable truth is first promised. 

For I do not think the celestial powers, which Holy Scripture names with various titles <note>e.g., Angels, Archangels, Powers</note>, are to be believed to look upon this visible world from the outside through bodily senses. Yet we must not think they are entirely ignorant of it, since Holy Scripture teaches that all these visible things are ruled and administered by them. Indeed, the divine laws were administered through angels, by whom the morals of good men are corrected, ordered, and led back from wicked ways, and, being exercised by the manifold teachings of divine wisdom, are recalled to the original mode of their nature. Rather, they perceive this world which they govern within the causes which they themselves eternally contemplate, insofar as they are permitted. They see it not divided by spaces of places, nor distinguished by the various motions of times, nor dispersed by the number of its parts, nor dissimilar by the manifold differences of qualities or quantities, nor swollen by the magnitude of mass, nor stretched out by different spaces of length, width, and height; but they see it subsisting as a whole, simultaneously and simply, in its immutable reasons. 

Therefore, concerning these obscure questions which I cannot see clearly by myself, I would judge that they should be more lucidly distinguished by you. I leave it to your judgment in what order they should be opened, provided that they are opened, whether now or later.

**Teacher:** The order of things requires another place for discussing all these matters which, as you say, are not clearly open to you. But we must taste them briefly here, since all these things that seem difficult to you can be concluded by one argument. For if Christ, who understands all things—or rather, is the Intellect of all things—actually unified and ordered all that he assumed, who could doubt that what preceded in the "Head" <note>Christ</note> as the principal example of the whole of human nature will follow in the whole, as we shall discuss in the fifth book? Indeed, the text of this present book demands nothing else than that we dispute, according to our strength and whatever light is granted to our minds, concerning the **Primordial Causes**.

# ON THE DIVISION OF NATURE

The things proposed concerning the fourfold division of universal nature ought, I believe, to be explained in a fitting manner. **M.** I judge that the beginning of our reasoning must be taken from the divine oracles <note>The Holy Scriptures</note>. **D.** Nothing is more appropriate, for it is necessary that every inquiry into truth take its beginning from there. **M.** That most divine prophet—I mean Moses—says at the beginning of the book of Genesis: *In the Beginning, God made heaven and earth*. In this passage, all the expositors of Holy Scripture have exercised the sharpness of their minds and have explained, through diverse modes of understanding, what the prophet—or rather the Holy Spirit through the prophet—wished to signify by the names "heaven" and "earth." Some, indeed, judge that a certain brief comprehension of the whole of perfected nature is suggested by these words. By the name of "heaven," they believe the entire spiritual and intellectual nature, created and formed, is intimated; while by "earth," they believe all corporeal and sensible nature, established in its most perfect definitions, is meant. Others strive to argue for the formlessness of both types of creature: by the name of "heaven," the beginning of the spiritual; and by the designation of "earth," the beginning of this corporeal world. Others affirm that the creation of the entire sensible creature (which is perfected, as it were, by two principal parts, namely heaven and earth) is indicated by these words; so that nothing concerning the condition of the spiritual creature is signified in this place, but the prophet wished only to describe the fullness of the corporeal creature. For between these two parts, heaven and earth—as if between two extreme boundaries, distinct from one another yet connected by their intermediaries—they teach by certain reasons that the universe of this sensible world is composed and united by a certain natural harmony. But it would be long, and unsuitable to the purpose of the present work, to extend the discourse through the many and various opinions of different men; and it will seem superfluous to introduce the understandings of others regarding such a matter, except where the greatest necessity demands it. To me, however, considering the senses of many, nothing occurs as more probable or more likely than that we should understand in the aforementioned words of Holy Scripture—namely, in the signification of heaven and earth—the <term>primordial causes</term> <note>original: "primordiales causas" - the fundamental patterns or ideas of all things existing in the Word of God</note> of the whole creature, which the Father created before all things that are established, in His only-begotten Son, who is called by the name of "the Beginning." And let us understand that by the name of "heaven," the principal causes of intelligible things and celestial essences are signified; and by the designation of "earth," the principal causes of the sensible things by which the totality of this corporeal world is completed. **D.** I would likewise choose this understanding above the others, if I were not moved a little by that ambiguity which seems to arise in the aforementioned distinctions of intelligence. For as it seems to me, there is little or no difference between the understanding of those who speak of the formlessness of both natures (the intelligible and the sensible) and those who judge that the primordial causes of the intelligible and sensible are described by the aforementioned words of Scripture. For we say that <term>unformed matter</term> <note>original: "informem materiam"</note> and its formlessness are a certain cause of things; for in it they begin, albeit formlessly—that is, still imperfectly. They are understood to be almost nothing, yet not entirely nothing, but to be in a way a beginning, and to desire form and perfection.

**M.** Be more attentive, then, and perceive the differences of things acutely, and drive the clouds of ambiguity entirely from the edge of your focus. For I do not think it can escape you that the formlessness of things is one thing, and the causes and perfect Principles of things are another. For if "being" and "not-being" are opposed to each other in a contrary way, nothing is closer to "truly being" than unformed matter; for it is, as Augustine says, "formless, almost nothing." Does it seem to you a small distance between that which is close to true essence, and that which is closest to the privation of true essence (which is nonetheless said to "be")? We have not said that the formlessness of things is the cause of the essence, form, and perfection of things, but rather the privation of essence, form, and perfection. For a cause, if it be truly a cause, encompasses most perfectly within itself all things of which it is the cause, and perfects its effects within itself before they appear in anything else; and while they break forth into visible genera and forms through generation, they do not desert their perfection in that cause, but remain fully and immutably, and stand in need of no other perfection except that one in which they subsist once, together, and always. Indeed, such a difference seems to me to be no small thing; for the primordial causes—which were most perfectly established in their immutable reasons by the one and highest Principle of all, which is from no other—stand far apart from the formlessness of all things, which not only have not yet reached the perfection of form, but scarcely now begin to be, nor have they receded far from non-being. For although the formlessness of things seems to hold a middle place

# BOOK TWO.

between being and non-being, beginning to be out of non-being; for indeed, from non-being, they commence a certain procession toward being: yet they are not yet understood to have reached the perfection of form and essence; they are judged to be closer to non-being than to being. For the formlessness of things is nothing other than a certain movement, entirely deserting non-being and seeking its own state in that which truly exists. But the primordial causes <note>The "primordial causes" are the eternal ideas or archetypes of all things as they exist in the Word of God.</note> were so established in the Beginning—that is, in the Word of God, which is truly said to be and is—that they do not seek their perfection through any movement in something else, but rather in that in which they are immutably and perfectly formed. For they are always formed by being turned toward the one Form of all things which all things desire—I mean the Word of the Father—and they never abandon their formation. Indeed, the causes of places and times are within them: but those things which are under them in the lower orders of things are created by them in such a way that the causes attract those things to themselves, and they all seek the one Principle of all things; yet the causes themselves in no way look back toward those things which are beneath them, but always gaze upon their own Form which is superior to them, so that they never cease to be formed by it. For by themselves they are formless, and they recognize themselves as perfectly established in that universal Form of theirs—I mean in the Word. Who, then, of right understanding, would dare to say of the formlessness of things these things which can be said of the primordial causes? Especially since even the formless matter of things is believed to flow from no other source than from the primordial causes. For if the primordial causes are called the "primordial ideas" which are first created by the one creative Cause of all, and they in turn create those things which are under them—for we have said that the primordial causes are both created and creative—what wonder is it if, just as we believe and confirm by certain reasons that formless matter is among the number of those things established after the primordial causes, we should also confess that it is created by the primordial causes?

16. **D.** <note>D. stands for Discipulus (The Student).</note> Therefore, the understanding of primordial causes is one thing, and that of formless matters is another. **M.** <note>M. stands for Magister (The Teacher).</note> By the aforementioned reasons, we are compelled to concede this. Consider other matters similarly, for various authors hand down what follows: *And the earth was invisible and empty;* or according to the Septuagint <note>The Greek translation of the Old Testament.</note> *invisible and uncomposed, and darkness was over the face of the abyss.* For some wish the name of the "empty and void" or "invisible and uncomposed" earth to be understood figuratively as the unity of visible creatures; and by the term "dark abyss," the unity of invisible essence. For every corporeal and sensible creature is constituted of matter and form, and therefore matter lacking form is called "formless"—that is, lacking form, and invisible and uncomposed, or empty and void. But when form is added, it is called visible and composed, solid and perfect, circumscribed by the certain boundaries of its nature. However, the invisible creature—that is, the intellectual and rational creature—is called "formless" before it is turned toward its own form, namely its Creator. For it does not suffice for perfection to subsist out of essence and essential difference (for every intellectual creature is composed of these two), unless it is perfected by being turned toward the only-begotten Word—I mean the Son of God, who is the form of intellectual life; otherwise, it remains imperfect and formless. Others think the earth was called empty and void, or invisible and uncomposed, because of this mass of the earthly body, first established as void and empty (as it seems to them) because it was not yet adorned with the various kinds and forms of seeds, shrubs, and animals; and "invisible" either because an abundance of watery nature was poured over it everywhere, or because man, who could look upon it, had not yet broken forth from the primordial causes through generation. They say there was "darkness over the abyss" because the air which had been poured around the abyss on all sides was not yet luminous, and therefore could not yet illuminate the abyss placed within it. Although Saint Augustine seems to oppose this sense, it is not entirely rejected by us, because it belongs to Saint Basil <note>Basil the Great, a 4th-century Church Father.</note>. For it is not our place to judge the understandings of the holy Fathers, but to receive them piously and with veneration; yet we are not prohibited from choosing what seems to agree more with the divine utterances and the consideration of reason. But if anyone should say the aforementioned words of the Prophet <note>Referring to Moses, the traditional author of Genesis.</note> hint at nothing other than the primordial causes of things, he will not seem to deviate from the truth. For why? Is it incredible that the <gloss>prototype</gloss> <note>original: "πρωτότυπον"</note>, that is, the principal exemplar of corporeal nature, which is signified by the name "earth," and which God had established in the Beginning—that is, the Father in the Son—perpetually and most perfectly before this sensible world, could be called "empty and void"? "Empty" indeed, because it was empty of every sensible thing in its effects before it came forth into the genera and forms of sensible nature through generation in places and times? And "void" <note>original: "inane"</note> because the prophetic intellect—which in the divine mysteries... <note>The sentence continues on the next page.</note>

# ON THE DIVISION OF NATURE

mysteries had been introduced, he had beheld. For these terms—I mean "void" and "empty"—signify the most full and immutable perfection of the primordial nature created before all things in the Word, rather than the changeable, imperfect, and still formless procession of this sensible world, which is dispersed through places and times, beginning its existence through generation, and seeking to be formed into the various numbers <note>In this context, "numbers" refers to the distinct, countable varieties of created things.</note> of sensible creatures. Indeed, "void" and "empty" are often used even in the praise of bodily things: for the entire space established in the middle between the globe of the earth and the choirs of the stars and the furthest circuit of the world is divided into two parts by the wise of the world and of Holy Scripture. The lower part, from the earth to the Moon, is called air, which is spirit <note>original: "spiritus," here meaning a moving gas or breath</note>; the upper part, from the Moon to the stars of the furthest sphere, is ether, that is, pure spirit. Both are called <term>kenon</term> <note>original Greek: κενὸν, meaning "the void"</note> by the Greeks, and "empty" or "void" by the Latins. But it is established that that purest and clearest upper space is of a superior nature, most serene, and quiet with eternal silence—excepting the harmonic songs and consonances of the Planets, surpassing every earthly and mortal sense by the sharpness of their tones and semitones—gliding with the motions of the most subtle fire, full of daily light, except for the small portion occupied by the shadow of the earth, which is called night. Therefore it is called "void," and not without merit, since it is void of all earth and thick mass, and is filled by no corporeal weight. For the globes of the Planets are most light and spiritual, and because they are burdened by no earthly quality, they produce no darkness. Indeed, the moisture and "filth" appearing in the Moon happens not from the purity of its own nature, but from the vapors of the earthly mass—which the Greeks call <term>atmidas</term> <note>original Greek: ἀτμίδας, meaning "steams" or "vapors"</note>—and its proximity to the moist abyss. Likewise, the cloudiness and darkness of this thicker and lower air comes not from itself, but from its closeness to the waters and lands. For from where else are the Moon's moisture, the air's cloudiness, thunder, lightning, showers, rain, snow, frost, and the various blasts of the winds born? They receive their occasion from nowhere else but earthly and aquatic vapors.

17. What wonder is it then if the primordial causes of visible things are figuratively suggested by the term "empty and void earth," because of the exceeding subtlety and ineffable simplicity of their intellectual nature, before they flowed down through generation into genera, forms, and sensible numbers (in which they appear to the bodily senses as if through certain clouds)? For the aforementioned spaces of the visible world, on account of their subtlety and near-incorporeality, are not incongruously called "void" or "empty." Thus says one of the Poets: *You shall hurl your body in a leap through the empty air.* Likewise Virgil in the twelfth book: *Then the stone itself of the hero, rolling through the empty void.* The same reasoning applies to the other interpretation, which says the earth was "invisible and uncomposed." For what else could that mystical invisibility of the earth more reasonably signify than the obscurity of the primordial causes of corporeal creation, not yet declared by any light of color or any distinction of forms, and moreover surpassing even the purest intellects? Justly also are the primordial causes preached as "uncomposed," for they are simple and entirely lacking all composition; for in them is an ineffable unity and an inseparable and uncomposed harmony, surpassing the joining of parts that are universally different or similar. What shall I say of the principal causes of intellectual essence? Have they not most fittingly been called by the Holy Spirit by the name "dark abyss"? For they are called an "Abyss" because of their incomprehensible depth and their infinite diffusion through all things, which is perceived by no sense and comprehended by no intellect; and because of the excellence of their ineffable purity, they deserved to be called by the name of "darkness." Indeed, even this sensible sun often brings darkness to those gazing at it, when they are unable to look upon its extraordinary brilliance. Thus "darkness was over the abyss" of the primordial causes; for before they proceeded into the multiplicity of celestial essences, no intellect could know what they were as created things. And even now there is darkness over this abyss, because it is perceived by no intellect, except for Him who formed it in the beginning. From its effects, however—that is, its processions into intelligible forms—it is known only *that* it is, but it is not understood *what* it is.
