# BOOK TWO.

This matter requires no small investigation. **D.** Indeed, it requires the greatest, and I earnestly beg that you do not leave this untouched. **M.** Tell me, please, what do you think: do the primordial causes <note>The "primordial causes" are the fundamental ideas or patterns of all things, created by God before the physical world.</note> of things—which we believe are signified by the names "earth" and "abyss," and are called invisible and dark because of their incomprehensible and excellent height—cease to be invisible and incomprehensible when they proceed through generation into genera, forms, numbers, and the differences of things (whether perceived by the senses or the intellect)? Or do they always remain in the excellence of their own nature, in which they were established before all things in the Beginning, falling under no sense or intellect? Do they appear in some way in the things of which they are the causes, yet never abandon the height of their own incomprehensibility, sitting forever as if in certain shadows—I mean in the most secret boundaries of Divine Wisdom? **D.** I would believe them to be perpetually invisible and dark. For if the very Wisdom of God the Father, in whom all things were made and truly subsist, and outside of which nothing is understood to exist, remains in itself incomprehensible and invisible;

> *For He is light inaccessible,* <note>original: "Lux enim est inaccessibilis" — a reference to 1 Timothy 6:16.</note>

surpassing all understanding: what wonder is it if the causes of all things—which were made in her, through her, and toward her—are believed to be eternally invisible and incomprehensible, and to remain unchangeably so? **M.** We can conceive of this by the example of our own nature; for what our intellect first conceives rationally within itself, and leads to the state of pure and perfect intelligence, it always retains within itself, even as it brings it forth externally through certain signs. For example, if a wise mind perceives true knowledge of something sensible or intelligible, that knowledge remains fixed within the mind, yet it sends out "phantasies" <note>In this context, "phantasies" refers to mental images or representations used to communicate thoughts.</note> first into the cognition, then into the senses, and finally into the signs of words and other gestures by which the mind is accustomed to open its secret workings as they descend step by step; it does not delay in bringing these into the notice of other minds through natural or artificial progressions.

**M.** Therefore, the primary causes both "come forth" into those things of which they are the causes, and yet they do not leave the Beginning—that is, the Wisdom of the Father in which they were made. To put it this way: remaining in themselves, they are invisible and always hidden in the darkness of their own excellence, yet they do not cease to appear in their effects, brought forth as if into a certain light of knowledge. **D.** These points can be argued persuasively; but I would like to know whether the principles of things, which are called by the names of primordial causes, understand themselves before they flow forth into the very things of which they become the causes, or not? **M.** This must be answered briefly;

> *If God made all things that He made in His wisdom, as Scripture testifies, saying: "In wisdom You have made them all";* <note>original: "omnia in sapientia fecisti" — Psalm 104:24.</note>

who is permitted to doubt that all things made in Wisdom—just as Wisdom herself knows herself—not only know themselves, but also do not lack knowledge of the things of which they are the principles? For it is not to be believed that anything foolish or ignorant of itself was established in Divine Wisdom; but these matters must be considered more diligently elsewhere. **D.** Let it be so; for it seeks no brief or transitory investigation to ask: if the primordial causes are wise because they were created in Wisdom and subsist eternally in her (who receives nothing foolish into herself), how do many foolish things proceed from wise causes? For not all things that flow from them are wisdom; indeed, we believe two natures participate in wisdom: the intellectual in angels, and the rational in humans. But other things which can be grasped by the senses or the intellect we believe to be devoid of wisdom—though one might answer briefly that it is not strange for the causes of foolish things to subsist in wise examples, just as we see that the occasions for darkness exist naturally in the rays of the sun; while the rays allow no darkness in themselves, they nevertheless generate darkness from causes implanted in them, as if produced by their opposite.

19. **D.** What, then, is the meaning of what follows?

> *And the Spirit of God moved over the waters.* <note>original: "Et Spiritus Dei superferebatur super aquas" — Genesis 1:2.</note>

Was this perhaps added lest the primordial causes be thought to surpass not only the power of intellectual and rational creatures, but even the intelligible knowledge of their Creator, due to the excessive excellence of their nature? **M.**

> *And the Spirit, he says, of God was moved over the waters;*

as if he were saying: the infinite and incomprehensible abyss of the primordial causes—rightly called invisible and dark for this reason—does not so escape all intellect and reason by the height of its obscurity that it cannot even be grasped and overcome by the Spirit of God. For God made them in the Beginning as certain foundations and principles of all natures that are from Him, and by His super-eminent "gnostic" power <note>term: gnostica virtus (the power of knowing). From the Greek 'gnostikos,' referring here to God's supreme cognitive ability to perceive all things.</note> He grasps them; and His Spirit is moved over them, not by a local movement, but by the eminence of His knowledge. It can also be said thus: *The Spirit of God was moved over*

# ON THE DIVISION OF NATURE

waters; for we must not believe that the primordial causes <note>original: "causas primordiales" — the first patterns or blueprints of all things created by God</note> of all things possess such excellence that no higher cause precedes and surpasses them. For there is one and the same Cause of all things, from which, through which, in which, and toward which the preceding occasions of all things were first made. Therefore, Scripture testifies—not without reason—that He is "borne over" all things, because He precedes the order of the entire creature beginning from Himself, and in Him it finds its end. This is not because one thing was created before or after another in terms of time, since all things exist in Him eternally and simultaneously, and were made by Him at once; rather, it is because by an ineffable order, incomprehensible to every intellect, Divine Providence produced the universe of created nature from non-existence into existence. Among these, some are preferred over others by a certain essential dignity; thus, those things which are believed and understood to exist most closely and immediately to the universal Cause of all—I mean God Himself—without any higher creature coming between them, are rightly proclaimed as the most principal beginnings of the created universe. Thereafter, others descend from one to another from the highest to the lowest—that is, from the beginning of the intellectual creature down to the lowest of all, which is the body. Therefore, the Spirit of God is "borne over" the dark abyss of all the causes made in the beginning, because the Creator Spirit alone surpasses the created causes for the sake of knowledge; He is the one and only preceding and super-excellent Cause of all the causes He had created, the most principal fountain of all things that flow from Him into infinity and return into Him. And He emanates from no other (if we understand this properly of the Holy Spirit) except from the Father; for from Him is the Son through ineffable generation, in whom as in the "Beginning" He made all things; and from Him is the Holy Spirit through procession, who is "borne over" all things. Indeed, the Father wills, the Son acts, and the Holy Spirit perfects. Nor does it seem an obstacle that Scripture says the Father made all things in the Son; for it is not one thing for the Father to will all things to be made and another for the Father to make all things in the Son, but rather the Father's willing and the Father's making are one and the same; for His action is His will. The same must be understood regarding the Son and the Holy Spirit, for their operation is nothing other than their will; nor is the will of the Father one thing and that of the Son or Holy Spirit another, but there is one and the same will, one love, of the three substances of the one essential Goodness, by which the Father moves Himself to make all things in the Son and to perfect them in the Holy Spirit. Whatever the Son makes and the Holy Spirit perfects is all referred back to the Father as the maker and perfecter, because from Him are all things: from Him is the Son through generation, who makes all things; from Him also is the Holy Spirit through procession, who perfects all things: the supreme Trinity, which is understood to *be* from those things that exist, is known to be *wise* from those things that have wisdom, and is most clearly proven to *live* from those things that move. Therefore, It exists, It is wise, and It lives.

However, if we read it as a certain *Syrian* <note>Likely a reference to Ephrem the Syrian or a specific Syriac translation known to the author</note> is said to have translated it (who, as Saint Basil says, withdrew from worldly wisdom as much as he approached the doctrine of true things), it says: *and the Spirit of God cherished the waters*, and a clearer understanding occurs. For the Holy Spirit "cherished"—that is, nourished with the warmth of divine love—the primordial causes which the Father had made in the Beginning (namely, in His Son), so that they might proceed into those things of which they are the causes. For this is why eggs are cherished <note>or "brooded upon"</note> by birds—the image from which this metaphor is taken—so that the internal and invisible power latent within them may break forth into visible forms and bodily beauties through the measures of places and times, as fire and air work upon the moisture of the seeds and the earthly matter.

20. **Student.** This does not conflict with a sound understanding, but I would like a brief and lucid <term>recapitulation</term> <note>original Greek: ἀνακεφαλαίωσις (anakephalaiosis) — a summary or bringing together of main points</note> to be made by you, so that I may more clearly and distinctly understand which specific words of the aforementioned Holy Scripture are intended to signify the primitive causes of things and the Cause of all causes.

**Teacher.** Be present then, to look into these matters more diligently.

**Student.** I am ready.

**Teacher.** Understand that the primitive causes, which Saint Dionysius <note>Dionysius the Areopagite, a highly influential mystical theologian</note> calls the "principles of all things," are signified simply and generally by these words: *In the beginning God created heaven and earth*. That is, God, in His Word, created the causes of both intelligible essences and sensible things universally. But the Prophet <note>Moses, traditionally regarded as the author of Genesis</note> wished to divide specifically what he had pronounced generally. Therefore, recognize that the causes of sensible things from which this world is composed—which are beyond the contemplation of the mind's eye due to the excessive subtlety of their nature, and lack all bodily thickness due to their extraordinary purity—are suggested by these words: *But the earth was invisible and unformed* (for so the seventy translators <note>The Septuagint (LXX), the Greek translation of the Hebrew Bible</note> rendered it); or according to the Hebrew truth: *But the earth was empty and void*. Then, the primitive principles of the celestial and intelligible essences, which by the great height and subtlety and obscurity of their condition surpass every intellect—

# BOOK TWO

H 3

superiority, you should know that the harmony of both interpretations has been introduced by these words: *And darkness was over the waters.* <note>Genesis 1:2</note> But how the Cause of all causes, the Beginning of all things, and the unique Fountain from which all things in heaven and on earth flow, transcends and knows from above all that He established in the primordial causes—by the inaccessible and incomprehensible excellence of His light, and by His own super-essentiality and excellence—behold that this is intimated by these words: *And the Spirit of God moved over the waters.* For what Theology <note>The author uses "Theology" here to refer to Sacred Scripture or the inspired word of God.</note> has specifically brought forth concerning the Holy Spirit, a sound faith both believes and understands commonly regarding the supreme and Holy Trinity. For the Holy and inseparable Unity is carried above the infinite abyss established by itself, by the excellence of its own essence and wisdom. 

If, however, you wish to follow that Syrian <note>Likely referring to St. Ephrem the Syrian or a source cited by Basil.</note> whom Saint Basil praises with great honors in his *Hexameron* <note>A famous series of homilies by Basil the Great on the six days of creation.</note>, who—in place of what other translators put as *the Spirit of God moved over the waters*—translated from the Syrian language (which is close to Hebrew) as *the Spirit of God cherished the waters*, you will find that the Divine piety is hinted at by these words. It overcomes the dark abyss of primordial causes by the height of its mercy, cherishing and fertilizing it, so that from the hidden and unknown boundaries of its nature, it might come forth into the ease of knowledge through generation, and through genera into the proper forms and species of sensible and intelligible substances—a manifold procession into its various and innumerable effects. You will find this suggested by the words: *And the Spirit of God cherished the waters.* 

And through this, understand that the supreme and singular Cause of all things, I mean the Holy Trinity, is clearly declared by these words: *In the Beginning God created heaven and earth.* Namely, the Father in the name of "God," His Word <note>The Logos or Son.</note> by the designation of "Beginning," and a little later the Holy Spirit where it says, *the Spirit of God moved over the waters.* For Sacred Scripture did not bring forth this passage concerning any other spirit. Behold, you have the primordial causes and the Cause of causes aptly and distinctly declared, if I am not mistaken, by the aforementioned words of Theology.

**D.** I clearly have them and gladly accept them: that the Father is signified by the name of God; the Son of God by the "Beginning"; and the Holy Spirit by the "Spirit of God." By the term "Earth," the causes of visible things are indicated; by "Abyss," the causes of intelligible things; and the super-eminence of the Divine nature—that is, the height of the Cause of causes—is suggested by the symbol of "brooding" or "cherishing" or "fertility."

**M.** Therefore, before we come to the consideration of the six-fold quantity of primordial causes, distinguished by Theology into the first six intelligible days, I think a few things should be said about the first and highest Cause of all, I mean the Holy Trinity, if it seems right to you.

**D.** I could seek nothing more greedily, believe nothing more healthfully, nor understand anything more deeply than what is said about the universal, ineffable Fountain of all things through true and probable investigations. For there is no other salvation for faithful souls than to believe what is truly preached about the one Principle of all, and to understand what is truly believed.

**M.** Tell me then, I pray, what do you understand when you hear Theology saying: *In the beginning God created*?

**D.** Nothing other than what was agreed between us: that the Father made all things in His Word. For when I hear "God," I think of God the Father. When I hear "Beginning," I understand God the Word.

**M.** Regarding this which the Theologian says, *In the beginning God created*, do you understand it to mean that the Father first begat His Word and then made Heaven and Earth in Him? Or perhaps He begat His Word eternally, and in Him eternally made all things; so that in no way does the procession of the Word from the Father through generation precede the procession of all things from nothing in the Word through Creation? And to speak more clearly: were the primordial causes in the Word of God (in whom they were made) not always there, and was there a "time" when the Word was and the Causes were not? Or are they co-eternal with Him, so that the Word was never without the causes established in Himself, and the Word is understood to precede the causes established in Himself in no other way except that the Word creates the causes, while the causes are created by the Word?

**D.** I would not at all concede that first point. For I do not see how the Generation of the Word from the Father could temporally precede the creation of all things by the Father in and through the Word; rather, I judge these to be co-eternal to one another. I mean the Generation of the Word and the creation of all things in the Word, for no one rightly thinks there is any accident or temporal motion or temporal procession in God. However, I might not incongruously concede what was posed second: that is, that the Generation of the Word from the Father in no way temporally precedes the creation of all things in the Word by the Father, but that they are co-eternal. For the Prophet also says to the Father in his addresses: *You have made all things in wisdom* <note>Psalm 104:24</note>; for the Father simultaneously begat His wisdom and in that wisdom made all things. And elsewhere the same Prophet says in the person of the Father: *My heart has brought forth a good word, I dedicate my works to the king* <note>Psalm 45:1</note>; as if he were openly saying: "From the secret of my substance, in the Word being born within it, I make my works in Him, which I give to Him as He reigns."

<summary>The Master and Disciple discuss the Trinitarian nature of creation, interpreting the opening of Genesis as a description of the Father creating through the Word (the Beginning) and the Holy Spirit (the brooding spirit). They conclude that the generation of the Son and the creation of the primordial causes are co-eternal acts within God.</summary>

# ON THE DIVISION OF NATURE

The same is said again to the Father: *With you is the beginning in the day of your power* <note>original: "Tecum principium in die virtutis tuæ" from Psalm 110:3</note>; as if he had said, the beginning of all things—which is your Word—is always with you and in you. If, therefore, the beginning is always in the Father and with the Father, there was never a time when the beginning did not exist; rather, it is the eternal beginning. And because it does not "happen" to be the beginning by chance, it was never without those things of which it is the beginning. *In the day of your power*; "in the day" means in the knowledge of the intellectual and rational creature regarding their generation from you. *Of your power* means of your wisdom; for the "day of the Father's power" is understood in this place, not unsuitably, as the knowledge of the ineffable birth of God the Word from God the Father. Insofar as it is granted to nature to understand what surpasses all intellect, access is granted to the "unapproachable light" through <term>Theophanies</term> <note>Divine appearances or manifestations through which the unknowable God becomes visible to the mind.</note>, which Theology instills into pure intellects. It fixes this knowledge in an ineffable and unknown way, so that they may possess within themselves a certain habit of true knowledge concerning that which is entirely unknown through itself. For the "power of the Father" is the "wisdom of the Father" in which He made all things. But that knowledge of the divine generation is still in us through faith, while in an Angel it is through direct sight—or rather, it is through direct sight neither in me nor in an Angel; for *who shall declare his generation?* <note>Isaiah 53:8</note> Indeed, no human and no celestial power can know the generation of the Word from the Father, how or of what kind it is. For no man or angel knows what it is, or who begot it, or who it is, or what it is that was begotten. Only He who begot knows what He begot, and that whatever was begotten is begotten; and He who begot knows Himself, and what He is, and how and in what manner He begot whatever He begot from Himself. Similarly, whatever was begotten knows itself—what it is, and how, and in what manner it was begotten—and knows Him who begot it, and who He is, and what He is. For if no one knows the Father except the Son, to whom shall the Word of the Son reveal Him? It follows then to say: no one knows the Son except the Father, and to whom shall the Word of the Father reveal Him? Thus we understand that they are revealed through Theophanies; for it is impossible for the essence of the Father and the Holy Spirit, and their substances, to be revealed through themselves directly to a creature. The same must be understood regarding the manner and quality of the divine generation—if indeed "who" and "what" can be properly said of the divine nature. For it is more than person, more than substance. Likewise, if "manner" and "quality" are to be properly predicated of that nature, it must be a nature that surpasses every manner and every quality, and all other things which are spoken of according to <term>accidents</term> <note>Non-essential properties; in Aristotelian logic, things that can change without changing the essence of the subject.</note>. We agreed upon these matters in the previous book.

It follows: *in the brightness of the Saints from the womb before the morning star I begot you.* <note>original: "in splendoribus Sanctorum ex V. A. L. G. Te." - an abbreviation for "ex utero ante luciferum genui te" from the Vulgate Psalm 109:3.</note> The order of the words is: from the only-begotten I begot you, *in the brightness of the Saints before the morning star*. And here again, understand the secret depths of the paternal substance, from which the only-begotten, who is the Word of the Father, was born, and from which He is always being born, and in which He always remains while being born. Of these depths it is said in the Gospel: *No one has ever seen God; the only-begotten Son, who is in the bosom of the Father, he has declared him.* For He is not separated from the Father, who is eternally and unchangeably in the Father, as He Himself says: *I am in the Father and the Father in me.* Therefore, the Father begot His Son from His own womb *in the brightness of the saints*; for as the Son is born from the Father, the "brightness of the saints" comes to be—that is, the knowledge of the elect and their substantial predestinations in the Word of God. As the Apostle witnesses, He predestined us into the kingdom before secular times. He did not say "before eternal times," for the eternal times are the co-essential eternity of the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit. In this eternity, the substantial brightness of the saints and the <term>primordial causes</term> <note>The fundamental patterns or "ideas" of all things as they exist eternally in the mind of God.</note> of all things were eternally established, and the effects of those causes were foreknown and predestined. But "before secular times" refers to those times in which the causes—once and eternally made in the Beginning—proceed into their effects, whether visible or invisible, through the administration of divine providence, according to a pre-defined and foreknown order of ages. Indeed, we believe that the predestinations and foreknowledge of the Saints (which the Psalmist calls "splendors") and the reasons of all things visible and invisible are co-eternal with the Father and the Son. *In the dispensation of the Word of God*, says Augustine, *they are not made, but are eternal.* He says "not made" in terms of time and place through generation, but "made" eternally in the Word according to the condition of the primordial causes. For in the beginning He made <term>Heaven and Earth</term> <note>Abbreviated in text as D.C. & T. (Deum Caelum et Terram).</note>. The same author says elsewhere: *The Word of God through which all things were made*, where all things live unchangeably—not only those which have been, but those which are to be. Yet in Him they have not "been" nor "will be"; they simply *are*, and all are one, or rather, are more than one. Likewise, John the Theologian says in the Gospel: *What was made in him was life.* Therefore, *in the brightness of the saints the Father begot his Son from his womb*; that is, in the only-begotten He begot the knowledge of the future saints—indeed, He created them. *For the knowledge of the saints in the wisdom of the Father is their creation.* For the intellect of all things in God is the essence of all things. Since indeed

It emphasizes that God's essence remains unknowable except through "Theophanies" or divine manifestations granted to the intellect.</summary>

# SECOND BOOK.

Indeed, for God, to know things before they are made is the very same thing as to make the things He knows. Therefore, God’s knowing and His doing are one. For by knowing He creates, and by creating He knows. Knowledge does not precede the act, nor does the act precede knowledge; wherefore all things are at once and together co-eternal by reason of the relationship between the Creator and the created. For God did not come to know all things after they were made, but before they were made, He knew all things that were to be made; and what is more wonderful, all things exist precisely because they were foreknown. For the essence of all things is nothing other than the knowledge of all things within the Divine Wisdom: *for in Him we live, move, and have our being.* <note>Acts 17:28. The author argues that our very existence is sustained by being "known" by God.</note> For knowledge, as Saint Dionysius <note>Dionysius the Areopagite, a highly influential mystical theologian in the Middle Ages.</note> says, of those things which exist, is the things themselves. Therefore, the "splendors of the Saints" are their clear and known substitutions <note>original: "substitutiones" — here meaning their fundamental substance or standing-under</note> within the divine wisdom and within the primordial causes; and all of this is "before Lucifer." This means that both the Birth of the Word from the Father and the splendor of the knowledge of the Saints—indeed, of all Natures in their principal causes—precedes Lucifer. 

But who this "Lucifer" is, before whose rising all the aforementioned things are understood to exist, is treated in many and various ways by the wise, whose opinions it would be tedious to introduce now. For that which is said through the Prophet Isaiah, *How art thou fallen from heaven, O Lucifer, son of the morning?* <note>Isaiah 14:12.</note> is clearly understood to refer to the devil and to his "body," which is understood to be composed chiefly of the impious, heretics, and apostates. To some, however, the "Lucifer" of the Psalmist seems to signify the devil; to others, it signifies that brightest star itself which is accustomed to precede the rising of the sun, before whose appearance above the horizon Christ is said to have been born of the Virgin. So Saint Augustine writes in his *Decades*; but since the interpretation of Holy Scripture is infinite, I think I should state what seems more suitable to the present discussion.

**Disciple.** Tell me, I pray, and explain it fully.

21. **Master.** For my part, in this matter, let my own simplicity suffice, for the cleverness of others is not appropriate here. This mystical and prophetic "Lucifer," before whose rising the splendors of the Saints are understood to be in the only-begotten Word of God, seems likely to signify this visible world, by that figure of speech which in Greek is called <term>synecdoche</term> <note>A figure of speech where a part represents the whole, or vice versa.</note> and is interpreted as "conception," by which form a part is signified by the whole, and the whole by a part. Therefore, before this visible world proceeded into genera and species and all sensible numbers through generation, before the ages of the world began, God the Father begat His Word, in which and through which He created the most perfect primordial causes of all natures. These, administered by divine providence in a certain wonderful harmony, complete this visible world through their processes of generation—by the measures of places and times, and the manifold differences of genera and species—from the beginning in which it started to the end in which it shall cease to be; not by the abolition of essence, but by the change of qualities and quantities through a natural course. 

And do not wonder that this whole world is signified by its part, which is Lucifer, when you see that by the sun itself (to which Lucifer always clings, for he does not recede far from it, but either rises a little before it in the morning or sets a little after it in the evening) this same world is mystically signified, as Solomon says: *What is that which hath been? The same that shall be. What is that which shall be? The same that is to be done: there is nothing new under the sun.* <note>Ecclesiastes 1:9.</note> Indeed, we do not incongruously understand "the sun" here to mean every visible creature. For Theologians signify by these words not only those parts of this world which are contained within the solar circle—I mean the nature of lands and waters, and the air with that part of the lower ether in which the lunar globe revolves—but also the spaces above the world from the solar line even to the extreme limit of the celestial sphere. For in all these things "under the sun"—that is, under the world—nothing is new, whether one understands that nothing is new in the course of the present life which did not previously appear in the natural order of things (for nothing else is felt or understood in time except the revolution of the same things); or, as Maximus <note>Maximus the Confessor, a Greek theologian.</note> understands this passage of Scripture: *That which was before this world, the very same shall be after it*; that is, those things alone which are eternal existed before this world and shall exist after it. And "nothing under the sun is new" means that whatever is "new" under this world is nothing; for this whole world is called "new" because it is not eternal, and therefore it is nothing. For it shall perish with all things that are in it, as the Psalmist witnesses, saying: *The heavens are the works of thy hands; they shall perish.* <note>Psalm 102:25-26.</note> If, then, the greatest part of the world, which is held to be the heaven and the most beautiful, and which is known to encompass the other parts on every side, is to perish; do you think that it will remain permanent in its other lower and viler parts? For in every composite body, while the things that contain it perish, those things which are contained [cannot] retain their permanence...

<meta>The text breaks off as the author prepares to argue that if the "container" (the heavens) perishes, the "contained" (the earth/lower elements) must also pass away.</meta>

# SECOND BOOK

is signified specifically. Likewise the Apostle <note>referring to St. Paul</note>, in whom he says *we live, move, and have our being* <note>Acts 17:28</note>, is himself the image of the invisible God, the firstborn of every creature, in whom all things were created in the heavens and on earth: whether visible or invisible, whether thrones, or dominions, or principalities, or powers, and all things were created through him and in him. Indeed, we find that the same Theology applies to the Holy Spirit the distribution of all causes, which the Father created universally and essentially in his Word. For if the partitioning and distribution of divine gifts is given to him (as the Apostle witnesses), why should the division of the primordial causes, substantially established in the Word, not also be given to him? *For to some,* he says, *is given the word of wisdom through the Spirit, to others the word of knowledge according to the same Spirit, to others faith in the same Spirit, to others the gifts of healing in the same Spirit, to others the working of miracles, to others prophecy, to others the discerning of spirits, to others kinds of tongues, to others the interpretation of speeches: but one and the same Spirit works all these things, dividing to each one as he wills.* <note>1 Corinthians 12:8-11</note> We can also argue this from the Scripture of Genesis, where it is written, *and the Spirit of God brooded over the waters:* <note>Genesis 1:2</note> for to what other end should the Spirit of God be thought to have brooded over, fertilized, and nourished the waters of the primordial causes, if not so that he might distribute and order those things—which were made without form, united, and simple in the Word—through the differences of kinds, forms, wholes, parts, and all numbers? And if no one among the faithful and right-thinking hesitates to affirm that the Spiritual gifts, which the Prophet Isaiah prophesied would rest upon the Head of the Church which is Christ, were distributed to the incarnate God-Word by no other than the Holy Spirit; what wonder is it if the same Spirit divides and gives to the Church, which is his body, not only the gifts of grace through Christ, but also the gifts of nature through the same Christ? Furthermore, he grants the gift of essence to every creature, visible and invisible; so that there are things which merely exist; the gift of life to living things, by which they live; the gift of sense to sentient things, by which they feel; the gift of reason to rational beings, by which they search out and find the natures of things by the act of reasoning and true diligence; and the gift of understanding to intellectual beings, by which they move toward God—the cause, namely, of all gifts—unknowingly and above every creature by an ineffable motion of the will and a wondrous striving. For the mystical lampstand of the Prophet Zechariah, to say nothing of the Mosaic one since it pertains to the same Mystery, signifies the Church. Its lamp placed above is the paternal and true light which *enlightens every man coming into this world.* <note>John 1:9</note> Our Lord, that is Christ, who for us, from us, and in the nature of our flesh was both conceived and born, and appeared to the world, is called the lamp upon the lampstand of the Church, while he becomes, according to nature, the supernatural wisdom and Word of God the Father. Upon him—first because according to the understanding of his humanity he is the head of the Universal body of the Church—the donations of the Holy Spirit, which are usually signified by the number seven, remain; for the divine gifts which are distributed to the Church through the Holy Spirit, because they are distributed by one spirit, beginning from his head, I mean Christ, are usually signified by the name of the Spirit himself. For the Spirit himself cannot be called a "gift" according to his nature, but rather the distributor of gifts; and therefore he is called "sevenfold," who universally and properly divides to the Church the fullness of divine things which is suggested by the number seven. For the Spirit himself is co-essential with the Father and the Son according to his divinity; upon whom—I mean the Son—he causes the divine gifts which he distributes to rest according to his humanity: *And there shall rest,* says the Prophet, *upon him the Spirit of the Lord,* as if he were aptly saying, *and there shall rest upon him the gifts which the Spirit of God divides: the spirit of wisdom and understanding, the spirit of counsel and fortitude, the spirit of knowledge and piety, and the Spirit of the fear of the Lord shall fill him.* <note>Isaiah 11:2</note> All of these, that is, all these gifts of the Spirit, the head of the Church which is Christ, receiving first according to his humanity, shares with his body, his Church, through the Holy Spirit. But the Holy Spirit himself, just as he subsists in nature according to the essence of God the Father, so he is also in the nature of the Son according to essence; because he proceeds ineffably from the Father substantially through the born Son: and through this, the gifts which he distributes are not his alone, but also the Father's from whom he proceeds, and the Son's from whom and through whom he proceeds. And do not think that only the multiform gifts of the Spirit, by which the multiform seeds of sin are expelled from the Church and utterly extinguished, are distributed by the Spirit. For wisdom acts against the temptation of foolishness, understanding for the removal of stupidity, counsel for the destruction of indiscretion, fortitude for the loosening of weakness, knowledge for the abolition of ignorance, piety for the persecution of impiety and of the very depravity of works. Fear drives away the blindness of thought. But also besides these gifts of grace, by which the Church is purified, built up, enlightened, and perfected,

# ON THE DIVISION OF NATURE
I

No faithful person should doubt that the gifts of all good things—which exist both according to essence and according to natural accidents—come from no other source than the Cause of all goods. I mean from the Father, from whom all things are; through the Son, in whom all things are; nor is there any distribution of them through any other than the Holy Spirit, who divides all things to all as He wills. Every best gift, says the Theologian <note>original: "Theologus"; likely referring to St. James (James 1:17) or Dionysius the Areopagite</note>, and every perfect gift is from above, coming down from the Father of Lights <note>original: "d. p. d. e. d. a. p. l." abbreviating "datum optimum et omne donum perfectum desursum est descendens a Patre Luminum"</note>; that is, from the Father of all good things which exist according to nature and according to grace. Indeed, all gifts are beautifully called "lights," which descend from the Unbegotten Light through the Begotten Light, and through the Proceeding Light. According to the capacity of nature and the bounty of grace, they are divided into the individual substance of each essence, whether general or special. For what the Apostle seems to say as if to man alone—*O man, what do you have that you did not receive?*—can be said universally to every creature, visible and invisible. For there is no created nature that has anything except what it received from its Creator. If it exists, as we said before, it received its being from there; if it lives, life from there; if it feels, feeling from there; if it enjoys reason, reason from there; if it understands, intellect from there; and a thousand other things of that kind. If, therefore, nothing is understood to exist in the nature of created things except what is given by the Creator, it follows that the creature is nothing else, whether essentially or according to accident, but the data and gifts of creation. Theology dedicates a certain distribution of gifts as a property, as it were, to the Holy Spirit. Therefore, all things that the Father does in the Son, the Holy Spirit distributes, and divides to each their own as He wills. Do you see, then, how Theology is understood to give to each of the substances or persons of the divine goodness their own properties? For it gives to the Father the "doing" of all things; it gives to the Word <note>The Son</note> that all the <term>primordial causes</term> <note>The original patterns or "blueprints" of all things existing eternally in the mind of God</note> of things are made in Him universally, essentially, and simply; it gives to the Holy Spirit to distribute those things made in the Word, having been made fruitful into their effects. That is, into genera and species, numbers and differences—whether of celestial and spiritual essences entirely lacking bodies; or those adhering to the purest spiritual bodies made from the simplicity of the <term>Catholic-elements</term> <note>The universal or fundamental elements of the universe</note>; or of the sensible things of this visible world, universal or particular, separated by places and mobile in times, differing in quality and quantity. Indeed, one might not irrationally approve that the primordial causes themselves entirely lack a visible or invisible body, as well as quality and quantity. By "invisible body," we mean the simple subsistence of the individual elements of this world, understood through itself. As for the celestial essences—that is, the angelic powers—although they are not incongruously thought to exist beyond this sensible world in the essence of their intellectual nature, they are not to be judged as deprived of spiritual bodies, if the opinions of the Holy Fathers are consulted. For they are to be believed to descend to the human senses (to which they often appear) not in foreign bodies, but in their own spiritual bodies always adhering to them. This is not because the operation of the inseparable unity of the divine substances is segregated (for what the Father does, the Son also does, and the Holy Spirit also does), but because divine Theology seems to distribute certain properties to each of them, and they are truly understood to have them. In the Holy Trinity, the unity of common operation is known, and the property of distinct operations is not excluded. For if there is in it a unity of essence and a difference of substances, I do not see why a common operation and a different one should not also be believed and understood in it; so that a common operation is attributed to the common essence, and a threefold action is not taken away from the substantial Trinity.

23. **D.** I see clearly, and it seems likely to me and in accord with the divine utterances; but I wish you would persuade me of what has been said concerning the properties of the divine administration by some similarity taken from our own nature, since it is said to be like God.
**M.** I would not easily believe that the Trinity of our nature is hidden from you.
**D.** Tell me, I pray, what it is.
**M.** Do you remember what was agreed between us in the dispute of the previous book? Did it not seem to us that there is no nature which is not understood to subsist in these three terms, which are called by the Greeks (as we have often said) <term>ousia, dynamis, energeia</term> <note>original: "ουσία, δύναμις, ενέργεια"</note>: that is, essence, power, and operation?
**D.** I certainly remember, and I hold to it most firmly.
**M.** Our nature, then, which is called human because all men participate in it, subsists in essence, power, and operation.
**D.** I should think no one skilled in divine Philosophy doubts this.
**M.** What do you think? Was our nature (as Holy Scripture witnesses) created in the image and likeness of God?
**D.** You act generously; he who doubts this is not a man.
**M.** Do you think God Himself, who created our nature in His own image, is a body or a spirit?
**D.** To hesitate here is also ridiculous. For God is...

<summary>This page discusses the distinct roles of the Trinity in creation—the Father as source, the Son as the locus of primordial causes, and the Holy Spirit as the distributor of forms—while maintaining their essential unity. It begins a comparison between the Divine Trinity and the human trinity of essence, power, and operation.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between a Master (M) and a Disciple (D) regarding the nature of the soul as an image of the Divine Trinity, following the discussion of man's creation.</meta>

# BOOK TWO.

God is Spirit, and *those who worship him, must worship in Spirit and in truth*. <note>John 4:24</note> **M.** Therefore, it is not according to the body, but according to the soul that the image of God is impressed upon our nature. **D.** This is also most true to confess. **M.** God is Trinity and Unity, that is, three substances in one essence, and one essence in three substances or persons: for just as the Greeks say <term>one essence, three hypostases</term> <note>original: "μίαν οὐσίαν, τρεῖς ὑποστάσεις"</note> or <term>three persons</term> <note>original: "τρία πρόσωπα"</note>, that is, one essence, three substances or persons; so the Romans say one essence, three substances or persons. In this, however, they seem to differ: we do not find the Greeks saying <term>one hypostasis</term> <note>original: "μίαν ὑπόστασιν"</note>, that is, one substance; but the Latins are very frequently accustomed to say one substance, three persons. The Greeks, however, say <term>of the same essence, of the same glory</term> <note>original: "ὁμούσιον, ὁμόδοξον"</note>, that is, of one essence, of one goodness, of one Deity: or one essence, one goodness, one Deity. These names, which among the Greeks suggest the inseparability of the divine nature, are not easily turned into the Roman speech, and in no way perfectly, as I judge; therefore, their meaning alone is transferred in separate words through <term>periphrasis</term> <note>A roundabout way of speaking; using many words to describe one concept.</note>, so that only their power may be understood, since their translation word-for-word is not expressed. **D.** The Catholic faith of the Universal Church confesses all of this and, as much as is granted, understands it. But where are these points leading? **M.** Nowhere else than that we may seek with all our strength how the trinity of our nature expresses the image and likeness of the creating Trinity within itself: that is, what in it is more suitably joined to the Father, what to the Son, and what to the Holy Spirit. I say "more suitably": for although the whole trinity of our nature is suitably the image of the whole Divine Trinity (for the whole bears the image of the Father, the whole of the Son, and the whole of the Holy Spirit), yet there is that in it which, as I judge, seems able to be accommodated more specifically to the individual persons. For even in itself, our whole trinity is in each of its parts: for its essence is its power and operation; its power is its essence and operation; its operation is its essence and power. Just as the Father is in the Son and the Holy Spirit; and the Son is in the Father and in the Holy Spirit; and the Holy Spirit is in the Father and in the Son.

**D.** Nothing occurs to me as more probable than to accommodate essence to the image of the Father; power to the Son; and operation to the Holy Spirit in our nature. Since the paternal substance, which begat the substance of sonship from itself and sent forth the substance of procession from itself, is not undeservedly called the principal substance—not because the one essence of the Sacred Trinity is separable (for it is one and indivisible), but because, while being one, it does not lack substantial differences. For there is the Deity begetting, the Deity begotten, and the Deity proceeding; and while it is one indivisible Deity, it is not, however, undistinguished by substantial differences. Power also is not incongruously joined to God the Son, since he is often called the "Power of the Father" by Theology. But to take one testimony from many, hear the Apostle: *For his invisible things are clearly seen, being understood by the things that are made; also his eternal power and divinity.* <note>Romans 1:20</note> For in this place, by the "power of the Father" and "wisdom of the Father," I say we understand the Son, and by "eternity" the Holy Spirit, as witnessed by *Maximus*, the venerable master. <note>Likely Maximus the Confessor, a 7th-century theologian.</note> For that the Holy Spirit is accustomed to be called by the name of "power" in the Scriptures, the Gospel testifies, where the Lord says, *healing* the woman <term>with an issue of blood</term> <note>original: "αἱμοῤῥοῦσαν"</note>, that is, suffering a flow of blood: *I perceived that power has gone out from me*, <note>Luke 8:46</note> that is, the Holy Spirit, who distributes the gifts of healing. What shall I say of the essential operation of our nature? Is it not most aptly coupled with the Holy Spirit, to whom, as if properly, the operation of divine powers and gifts is attributed, both universally and specifically to each one? Therefore, in the essence of our nature, the property of the paternal substance is recognized; in the power of the substance, that of the Son; and in the operation of the substance, that of the Holy Spirit. **M.** I judge that your response does not deviate from the truth. But see what you think of that most famous trinity of our nature, which is understood as intellect, reason, and sense: does it differ from the one we spoke of before—the soul—and are these the same thing and not two different things of one and the same nature of ours? I speak of sense, however, not the exterior but the interior; for the interior is co-essential with reason and intellect, while the exterior, although it seems to pertain more to the soul than to the body, does not however constitute the essence of the soul. Rather (as the Greeks say), it is a certain joining of soul and body, for when the body is dissolved and life recedes, it is utterly destroyed. For if it remained in the soul and pertained to its substance, it would surely use it even outside the body; but because it neither uses it nor can use it without the body, it remains that it neither stays in the dissolved body nor adheres to the soul when it leaves the governance of the body. For even the very definition by which Saint Augustine wished to define exterior sense does not seem to be counted among the substantial parts of the soul; *Sense is*, he says, *a passion of the body, not hidden from the soul by itself.* <note>Augustine, De Musica VI.5.10</note> Likewise another, which says,

# ON THE DIVISION OF NATURE

The sense is a mental image <note>original: "phantasia"</note> of sensible things received through the instrument of the body. Similarly, it does not seem to join the nature of the soul, but rather to be established as a messenger between the body and the soul. If anyone looks closely at the property of the Greek language, they will find the property of two senses in man. For in that language, *nous* <note>original: "νῦς"</note> is called **intellect**, *logos* <note>original: "λόος"</note> is **reason**, and *dianoia* <note>original: "διάνοια"</note> is **sense**—not that exterior sense, but the interior one. In these three, the essential trinity of the soul, established in the image of God, subsists. For there is intellect, reason, and the sense which is called interior and essential.

The exterior sense, however, which we called the bond of body and soul, is called *aisthesis* <note>original: "αἴσθησις"</note>. The instruments in which it resides are called *aistheteria* <note>original: "αἰσθητήρια"</note>, as if they were the "guardians of sense" <note>original: "αἰσθήσεως τηρητήρια"</note>, that is, the custody of the sense. For in them the sense is guarded and operates: and they are five in number: hearing, sight, smell, taste, and touch. Nor should it move you that I have called the five-part instrument by the names of the five-part sense; for it is a very frequent use in common speech and Sacred Scripture to call the seats of the senses by the names of the senses themselves. For the eye is called "sight," and the ear "hearing," and the other senses similarly provide the meaning for their instruments. 

However, the sense is not called five-part because it is divided within itself by the number five; for it is simple and uniform, and possesses its seat in the heart as if in a principal place. But through the five-part instrument of the body—as if through five gates of a city—it receives within itself the likenesses of sensible things coming from the qualities and quantities of the exterior world, and other things by which the exterior sense is formed. Like a certain porter and messenger, it announces those things which it introduces from the outside to the presiding interior sense.

**Disciple.** Neither does it move me that the instruments are signified by the names of the senses, nor does the difference between the two senses, which you have clearly distinguished, escape me, I believe. And what I feel concerning the aforementioned trinity of our nature, I shall set forth according to the capacity of my talent. There seem to be two trinities by which our nature is proven to subsist, insofar as it is made in the image of God: but when truth is consulted, they are found to differ from each other not in reality, but in names alone. For *nous* and *ousia* <note>original: "νῦς & οὐσία"</note>, that is, **intellect** and **essence**, signify the most excellent part of our nature, or rather its most excellent motion. Since (as you yourself understand) it is not one thing for our nature 
> to be, and another to be moved; for its essence is a stable motion 
> and a mobile state around God and the creature.

But while it is moved around God, who surpasses all things, it is called its greatest motion; while it is occupied with the **primordial causes** <note>The original blueprints or archetypes of all things in the mind of God.</note>, which are next and immediate after God, it is understood to be moved by a certain middle governance. When, however, it endeavors to perceive the effects of the primordial causes, whether visible or invisible, it is known to undergo its extreme motion—not because the same substantial motion can be increased or diminished in itself, but because it is indicated to be least, middle, or greatest according to the dignity of the things around which it revolves. 

Therefore, the essence of our soul is the **intellect**, which presides over the whole of human nature and is carried unknowingly around God above all nature. *Logos* or *dynamis* <note>original: "λόγος vero, vel δύναμις"</note>, that is, **reason** and **power**, suggests the second part, as it were; not irrationally, because they revolve around the principles of things which are first after God. The third part is designated by the terms *dianoia* and *energeia* <note>original: "διάνοια & ἐνέργεια"</note>, that is, **sense** and **operation**, and occupies the extreme place of the human soul; and not undeservedly, since it revolves around the effects of the primordial causes, whether they be visible or invisible. And through this, we ought to understand not two substantial trinities, but one and the same, founded in the likeness of its Creator. 

But I wonder for what reason I do not see you enumerate **life** itself—which is called *threptike* and *auxetike* <note>original: "θρεπτικὴ & αὐξητικὴ"</note> by the Greeks, and **nutritive** and **augmentative** by our people (for it nourishes bodies and provides growth to them)—either as a fourth substantial part of human nature, or within the substantial trinity; it seems entirely passed over, as if it in no way pertained to the constitution of our nature.

**Master.** Do not wonder, for we have not done this without cause. Indeed, the discourse now is not concerning the universal human nature, which seems to consist of five parts—that is, body, vital motion, sense, reason, and intellect—but only concerning that part in which the image and likeness of the Creator is seen; that is, concerning the **intellect**, **reason**, and **interior sense**, or (as I might say) **essence**, **power**, and **operation**. For in this triad, the expressed image of the supreme and Holy Trinity is recognized. For concerning the vital motion, by which the soul nourishes the body, collects it into one, vivifies it, ministers to it, and gives it growth, and concerning the body itself, which occupies the extreme place of the whole creation, there will be another place for discussing when, with God as our guide, we dispute concerning sensible natures—which part, since it is outside the property of our intelligible essen-

<meta>The previous page discussed the three motions of the soul; this page continues the analysis of the third motion and its relationship to the angelic nature.</meta>

# ON THE DIVISION OF NATURE

...not because it is not simple in itself, just as the first and second motions are simple; but because it does not begin to know the reasons <note>Latin: *rationes*, often translated as "essences" or "ideas" in Eriugena's thought</note> of sensible things through itself. First, it receives the phantasms <note>mental images or impressions</note> of those things through the five-fold exterior sense, according to the number of bodily instruments in which and through which it operates. Gathering, dividing, and ordering these images, it arranges them; then, reaching through them to the reasons of the things of which they are phantasms, it forms and confirms those reasons within itself. 

And do not be disturbed that a little while ago we defined the exterior sense as being the phantasm of sensible things, while now we teach that one reaches the phantasms of those same sensible things through it. For the aforementioned third motion begins to be moved by the phantasms of exterior things reported to it through the exterior sense. There are two species of phantasms: the first is that which is first born from sensible nature in the instruments of the senses, and is properly called an "image expressed in the senses"; the other is that which is formed in subsequent order from the aforementioned image, and is the phantasm itself which is customarily and properly called the "exterior sense." The former always adheres to the body, while the latter adheres to the soul; and although the former is in the sense, it does not perceive itself, whereas the latter both perceives itself and receives the former. 

When that third motion leaves behind the phantasms of visible things and purely understands the reasons—stripped of all bodily imagination and simple in themselves—it, being itself simply simple, reports the universal reasons of visible things (freed from every phantasm and perceived most purely and truly in themselves) through the middle motion to the first motion. Then the first motion, whatever it perceives from the third through the middle, and from the middle itself immediately in the governances of created things, refers it through itself immediately to the unknown—unknown because it is the Cause of all—and to the Principle of all things (that is, the primary causes made by Him and in Him, and distributed by Him). That is, it understands that all things proceed from God through these causes into all that follow them, and return again through them into Him.

Therefore, the motion of a soul purified through action, illuminated through knowledge, and perfected through Theology <note>Here meaning the highest mystical contemplation of God</note>—by which it is eternally revolved around the unknown God—understands God Himself to be entirely absolute, beyond its own nature and the nature of all things, beyond all that can be said or understood, and even beyond all that can neither be said nor understood and yet in some way exist. Denying that He is any of the things that are or are not, and approving that all things predicated of Him are predicated not properly but metaphorically <note>Latin: *translative*</note>, this motion is called *nous* <note>Greek: νοῦς, "mind" or "intellect"</note> by the Greeks, and by our people "intellect," "spirit," or "mind." It exists substantially and is understood to be the principal part of the soul.

For it is not one thing for the soul to exist essentially and another for it to be moved substantially. Indeed, the soul subsists in its motions, and its motions subsist in it; for it is a simple and indivisible nature, distinguished only by the substantial differences of its motions.

For the holy fathers teach that the celestial essences—which the divine oracles call "Celestial Powers" and "Angels"—are substantially nothing other than intelligible, eternal, and ineffable motions around the Principle of all, from whom, through whom, in whom, and toward whom they are moved and subsist. For this is the circular motion of the celestial powers around their Principle: from Him as from a beginning; through Him and through their reasons established by Him; in Him as in their natural laws which are in Him and which they neither wish nor are able to transgress; and into Him as they return to their end. This motion exists in intelligence alone; for they are not ignorant that they are from Him, and that their intellect is exercised through Him and in Him, and that they have no other end besides Him. 

What, then, prevents us from similarly understanding that human intellects revolve unceasingly around God, since they are from Him, through Him, in Him, and toward Him? For they revolve in the same intelligible circle, especially since the divine oracles declare that man was made in the image of God—a thing not openly found to be said of the Angels. We read that the celestial powers stand before and minister to God, but the Catholic faith testifies that human nature, having become God in the Word of God, sits at the right hand of God and reigns. For what the Lord says generally of all men after the universal resurrection—*They shall be as the angels of God in heaven*—I believe is to be understood not regarding the worthiness of nature, but regarding the equality of immortality, and that they shall lack all sex, body, and every corruptible generation. For it is not incongruous to believe that the first condition of man before sin in Paradise—that is, in celestial beatitude—was equal to the Angels and, as it were, of the same nature. For concerning both these natures, I mean the Angelic and the Human, Theology pronounces, saying:

<summary>Eriugena describes the soul's third motion, which processes sensory data into pure intellectual reasons, and argues that the human intellect, like the angels, finds its essence in a circular motion around God.</summary>

# SECOND BOOK

->**The Second Book**<-

<margin>+</margin>saying, *Who made the heavens in understanding* <note>original: "Qui fecisti cælos in intellectu" (Psalm 136:5)</note>, that is, so that they might be intellects essentially and substantially. But because *man, when he was in honor, did not understand, and was compared to the foolish beasts, and became like them* <note>Psalm 49:12</note>, he fell far away from Angelic dignity and tumbled into the calamity of this mortal life. However, after *the Word was made flesh* <note>John 1:14</note>, that is, God was made man; that which is written in the Psalm is fulfilled: *What is man that thou art mindful of him, or the son of man, that thou visitest him?* <note>Psalm 8:4</note> Admiring, clearly, the height of the first condition of human nature, he says, *Thou hast made him a little lower than the Angels* <note>Psalm 8:5</note>, that is, you permitted him to be lowered by the merit of his own pride, and you left him to fall into the foulness of an irrational life by his own will. For God is said to "do" what he "permits to be done," according to a certain manner of speaking. *Thou hast crowned him with glory and honor, and hast set him over the works of thy hands, thou hast put all things under his feet.* Do you see how much human nature was humbled in the first man after sin, and how much it is exalted through grace in the second man—I mean in Christ? For he is not only restored to the original state of his nature from which man fell, but is even exalted above all celestial powers. For where sin abounded, grace abounded more. If, therefore, human nature has not only reached a renewed Angelic dignity in Christ, but has even been assumed into God beyond every creature—and it is impious to deny that what was done in the Head shall be done in the members—what wonder is it if human intellects are nothing else but ineffable and unceasing motions? I speak of those who are worthy to be around God, *In whom they live, and move, and have their being* <note>Acts 17:28</note>. They "are" indeed through the reasons by which they exist; they "move" through the reasons of the virtues by which they are able to exist well; they "live" through the reasons by which they exist forever.

**D.** I not only grant this, but I also understand that the most excellent motion of the soul around the Unknown God, beyond every creature, is most truly called and subsists as "intellect." But how, or by what reason, the intellect—while it is enclosed within the boundaries of human nature—can ascend above itself and every creature, so that it may complete its substantial motions around the unknown God (who is far removed from all created nature), I do not understand.

**M.** In this part of <term>theory</term> <note>theoria: contemplative or speculative knowledge</note> which disputes concerning intellectual and rational substances, when one arrives at considering how a created nature can ascend outside of itself so that it may cling to the creative nature, all inquiry of reasoning concerning the power of nature fails. For there, it is not the "reason of nature" that is seen, but the ineffable and incomprehensible depth of divine grace. Indeed, there is no power naturally within any created substance through which it can both overcome the boundaries of its own nature and reach God himself immediately through itself; for this belongs to grace alone, and to no power of nature. Hence the Apostle confesses that he did not know *how he was caught up into Paradise*, saying, *I know a man caught up, but I know not whether in the body or out of the body* <note>2 Corinthians 12:2-4</note>; as if he were to say: "I do not see in the natural motions of the soul, whether in the body or out of the body, any power through which I could be caught up into the third heaven; God alone knows, by whose grace alone I am not ignorant that I was caught up." Indeed, no nature can ascend by itself into that place where the Lord says, *Where I am, there also shall my servant be* <note>John 12:26</note>.

**D.** Although the answer was brief, it was clear enough; therefore, direct the sharpness of your mind to consider what follows: the second motion of the soul.

**M.** The second motion of the soul is (as we have said) that which is contained within the boundaries of nature, and defines God himself according to Cause. That is, it knows only this much concerning the unknown God: that He is the Cause of all things that are; and that the <term>primordial causes</term> <note>the fundamental blueprints or "ideas" of all things in the mind of God</note> of all things were created eternally by Him and in Him. It imprints the knowledge of these causes (insofar as they are granted to be understood) upon the soul itself, whose motion it is. For just as the soul receives images of sensible things from below—which the Greeks call <term>phantasias</term> <note>mental images or appearances</note>—so from above, that is, from the primordial causes, it fixes within itself knowledges which are usually called <term>Theophanies</term> <note>divine appearances or manifestations</note> by the Greeks and "divine appearances" by the Latins. Through these, it perceives a certain notice of God; I mean through the first causes—not so that it understands *what* they are substantially (for this surpasses every motion of the soul), but so that it knows universally *that* they are, and that they flow forth into their effects in an ineffable procession. And this is the motion which is called <term>logos</term> <note>word/reason</note> or <term>dynamis</term> <note>power/virtue</note> by the Greeks, and "reason" or "virtue" by our people; and it is born from the first motion, which is intellect. For just as a certain wise craftsman begets his art from himself within himself, and foreknows in that art the things he must make, and [knows] their causes before they appear in act and work uni-

universally and causally creates; so the intellect, from itself and in itself, has begotten its own **reason**, in which it foreknows and causally pre-creates all things that it wishes to make. For we say that "counsel" is nothing other than the concept of an artistic mind. Therefore, the second movement of the soul is **reason**, which is understood as a kind of substantial gaze within the mind, and like an art begotten by the mind from itself and in itself, in which it foreknows and pre-establishes those things it wishes to make. And so, it is not without merit that this is called its "form"; for the intellect itself is unknown through itself, but in its form, which is reason, it begins to appear both to itself and to others. For just as the Cause of all things <note>God</note> cannot be found through itself—neither by itself nor by anyone else—as to *what* it is, but is known in a certain way in its **Theophanies** <note>Divine appearances or manifestations in the created world</note>; so the intellect, which always revolves around God and was created entirely similar to His image, cannot be understood by itself or by anyone else as to *what* it is; but in the reason which is born from it, it begins to appear. As for what I said—that the Cause of all things and the intellect are not understood by themselves as to what they are—the logic of this will be considered a little later. Regarding the second movement, what it is and where it draws its origin, enough has been said, I believe.

**Disciple.** Quite clearly.

**Master.** There remains, then, the third movement, which is concerned with the individual accounts of individual things that are established simply—that is, universally—in the **primordial causes** <note>The fundamental blueprints of reality existing in the Word of God</note>. And since it takes the starting point of its substantial movement from the **phantasies** <note>Mental images or impressions</note> of sensible things reported to it through the exterior sense, it arrives at the purest discernment of all things through their own specific reasons: into the most general essences, and into more general genera, then into the most specific forms and species—that is, into numbers innumerable and infinite, yet limited by the immutable analogies of their own nature. And that movement is what is called in Greek *dianoia* <note>original: "διάνοια", discursive thought</note> or *energeia* <note>original: "ἐνέργεια", activity/operation</note>, but in Latin is called **sense** or **operation**; I mean the sense itself which is substantial and interior, which likewise proceeds from the intellect through reason. For everything that the intellect impresses upon its "art"—that is, upon reason—from its cognitive contemplation of the primordial causes, it divides into the specific reasons of individual things (which were created primordially and universally in the causes) by means of its "sense" proceeding from itself, which is called by the name of "operation." Indeed, all essences are one in reason, but in sense they are discerned into different essences. Thus, reason perceives the simplest knowledge of all essences from the most simple unity of their principles through the intellect; but sense separates that very simplicity through differences. Similarly, reason knows the genera of things uniformly and simply in their universal causes and in themselves through the intellect; but sense partitions that most general simplicity—which is in itself individual, subject to no differences, underlying no accidents, stretched by no spaces, composed of no parts, and varied by no local or bodily motion—into the diverse genera and differences mentioned before, and a thousand others. The same occurs in the different forms, which by deliberate reason are one in their genera, yet by the operation of sense are distinguished from one another through natural distinctions; that is, in what way they are divided into the discrete multiplicities of things by natural movements under the guidance, command, and administration of Divine Providence, the intellect itself investigates and pursues without error through the sense that is of the same substance as itself, and comprehends them by certain rules. What shall I say of the infinite numbers, which become as manifold as the action of the interior sense makes them, whether in itself or in the nature of things? Yet, when considered by reason, they are universally and simply one in their forms. To conclude briefly: whatever the soul knows uniformly and universally through its first movement (which is the intellect) concerning God and the primordial causes, it fixes in the same uniform and universal way by its second movement (which is reason). But whatever it receives from higher things through the intellect and formed in reason, it distributes all of this into discrete essences, discrete genera, diverse forms, and manifold numbers through the sense in lower effects. And to speak more plainly: whatever the human soul knows uniformly through its intellect in its reason concerning God and the principles of things, it understands all of this manifoldly through the sense in the effects of the causes; and again, everything that it understands as manifoldly scattered in the effects through the sense, it perceives through reason to subsist uniformly as one in the causes. Indeed, it knows most purely through the intellect that all things both begin the momentum of their multiplication from the one Cause of all, and in no way desert the simplicity of unity in which they eternally and immutably subsist in that Cause; and that they terminate the end of their entire movement in it. Concerning the three movements of the soul—that is, the intellect,
