# BOOK ONE
K
<meta>Catchword: in</meta>

...which is essence; and concerning reason, which is power; and concerning sense, which is called operation, enough has been argued, I believe. **D.** Most clearly and in many ways.

**XXIV. M.** Look then, and with the sharp edge of the mind—all the fog of doubt having been driven away—recognize how clearly, how expressly the substantial Trinity of the divine goodness smiles upon those who look rightly at the motions of the human soul. It manifests itself most limpidly to those who seek it piously, as if in its own mirror made according to its image. And although it is remote from every creature and unknown to every intellect, it brings itself forth through its image and likeness as if known and comprehensible to intellectual eyes, and as if present. Of its own accord, it purifies the small mirror in which it is reflected, so that the one essential goodness may shine forth most brightly in three substances <note>original: "substantiis" - here used in the sense of 'persons' or 'hypostases'</note>. This unity and trinity would not appear in itself through itself, because it escapes every intellect by the exceeding infinity of its brightness, unless it impressed the traces of its knowledge upon its image. Indeed, the most open likeness of the Father shines in the soul <note>original: "animo" - the higher mind or intellect</note>, of the Son in the reason, and of the Holy Spirit in the sense. 

For just as we call the Son the "Art" of the omnipotent Craftsman—and not without merit, since in Him, as His own wisdom, the omnipotent Father-Craftsman made all things whatsoever He willed, and keeps them eternally and unchangeably—so also the human intellect creates through knowledge whatever it perceives most purely and unhesitatingly concerning God and the principles of all things, as if in its own "art" (I mean in the reason) by a certain wonderful operation of science, and hides it away in its most secret boundaries through memory. 

Moreover, just as the Father, the omnipotent maker of all things, divides all things—which He created simultaneously and once for all, primordially, causally, uniformly, and universally in His Art (which is His wisdom and power, in His Word, in His only-begotten Son)—through the Holy Spirit proceeding from Himself and the Son into the innumerable effects of the primordial causes <note>term: primordial causes - the first patterns or "ideas" of things created by God</note>, whether they have flowed into intelligible essences and differences surpassing all bodily sense, or have flowed forth into the various and manifold ornament of this sensible world, variable by the distinctions of places and times: so the intellect—that is, the principal motion of the soul formed from the gnostic <note>note: "gnostica" - here meaning 'pertaining to knowledge' or 'cognitive'</note> contemplation of intelligible things—divides everything whatsoever it creates and stores in the art of reason through the interior sense of the soul into a distinct and unconfused knowledge of individual things, whether intelligible or sensible. For all things which the intellect considers universally in the reason, it partitions particularly through the sense into the distinct knowledges and definitions of all things. 

You see, therefore, that the Father has created in His Son all things whatsoever He willed, and has distributed them particularly through His Holy Spirit, and is distributing them, and will distribute them. Recognize that, according to the likeness of the three persons of the Godhead, our intellect creates—that is, forms—a knowledge by an act of science universally in the reason of all things whatsoever it can understand concerning God and the causes of things; and through the sense, which is co-substantial with itself, it divides this knowledge particularly into the individual definitions of things which it collects in the reason by the power of unconfused contemplation; that is, it accommodates its knowledge to each thing by the gaze of most careful discretion. 

**D.** These things shine forth to me in a certain way, but I do not yet clearly see what the difference is between the operation of the creating Trinity (which is not created) and the act of the created trinity which creates. I say "creates"; for we do not doubt that the trinity of our nature—which is not the "Image of God" itself, but was "made according to the image of God" (for the only true Image of the invisible God, dissimilar in nothing, is the only-begotten Word of God, co-essential with the Father and the Spirit)—was not only created out of nothing, but also creates the senses adhering to it beneath itself, and the workshops of the senses, and I mean this whole mortal body of its own. Since it was indeed made from God according to the image of God out of nothing, it creates its own body; not, however, out of nothing, but out of something. For the soul, by gluing together incorporeal qualities into one, and taking and placing a certain subject for those qualities from quantity, creates a body for itself in which it may openly manifest its hidden actions (which are invisible through themselves) and bring them into sensible notice. This was already disputed in the previous book, and will be investigated more diligently when we come to the consideration of the primordial causes; but now I beg you to open up what is pressing.

**M.** The solution to this question seems to me very easy and almost without any labor. For the supreme Trinity, the creatress of all things and created by none, made all things which it made out of nothing. For it is proper to the divine goodness to call those things which it wills to be made from non-existence into existence. For even this name, which is "goodness" <note>original: "bonitas"</note>, derives its origin from no other source than from the Greek verb which is *boō* <note>original: βοῶ</note>, that is, "I cry out"; now *boō* and *kalō* <note>original: καλῶ</note>, that is, "I cry out" and "I call," possess one sense; for he who calls, very often...

<summary>The Master explains how the human soul mirrors the Trinity through the triad of Intellect, Reason, and Sense, while the Disciple asks for clarification on how the soul "creates" its own physical body.</summary>
<keywords>Trinity, Image of God, Intellect, Reason, Sense, Creation from nothing, Body and Soul, Etymology of Goodness, Primordial Causes</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the division of nature, specifically focusing on the etymology of God's goodness and the five-fold motion of creation, transitioning into the relationship between the human soul and body.</meta>

# ON THE DIVISION OF NATURE

breaks out into a shout. Therefore, God is not improperly called "good" and "goodness," because He cries out with an intelligible shout for all things to come from nothing into being; for this reason, in Greek, God is called *kalos* <note>original: "καλὸς"</note>, that is, "beautiful" or "good," because of the fact that He calls <note>original: "καλεῖν" (kalein), a wordplay linking beauty/goodness with the act of calling</note> all things into essence. Indeed, all things that naturally subsist were called from nothing into essence by the Creator through a five-fold motion of universal creation. For some were called so that they might only exist essentially; some so that they might exist and live; in some, the sense of a substantial life is added; in some, reason is heaped upon the vital sense; and in some, intellect is placed above to complete the perfection of the aforementioned natural motions. 

And the first motion is seen in natural bodies; the second in that life by which trees and plants both live and grow; the third in irrational living creatures; the fourth specifically in human nature; and the fifth in Angelic nature. From these five steps, the goodness of the highest and sacred Trinity is perceived in the founding of things from nothing, and its ineffable operation is made manifest. 

But that Trinity which was founded in our nature according to the image of the Creator creates nothing from nothing. For that belongs to God alone, and to no creature. Its action is seen to be twofold: for either it investigates those things which its Creator founded from nothing through its rational and intellectual motions, and disposes those things which it knows in the nature of things by the pure gaze of intelligence within the innermost seats of its reason, and collects the knowledge of all things it can know into one—that is, to use the same example, discerning essence into genera, genus into forms, and form into individuals by a <term>gnostic operation</term> <note>an act of spiritual or intellectual knowing</note>; or it divides them in multiple ways, distributing individual knowledges to the individual things of which they are knowledges; for example, it makes one by the act of science the genera in essence, the forms in genus, and the numbers of individuals in form. And this is the principal and highest operation of the rational nature. 

The second operation is that which (as we have said) is recognized in the creation of its own body and is revealed in its careful governance. First, it receives the matter of the body from the qualities of sensible things, and without any temporal interval, it applies a vital mode to it, by which it both gives life to and nourishes that matter, and brings it to the increase of perfect stature through the measures of places and times. It also provides an exterior sense to it, through which it receives the <term>phantasies</term> <note>mental images or impressions</note> of all things it touches from the outside, and other things which can be thought and understood concerning the soul's constant care and continuous action regarding its body and bodily things—whether being present to the senses while waking, or removed from them while sleeping and treating with itself the images of things it had drawn in through the senses, or fashioning images of images, or distributing the bodily nourishment which it receives from the outside into the structure of the body through the hidden passages of the veins and nerves, which the Greeks call <term>pores</term> or <term>arteries</term>. For just as the highest Trinity moves, rules, and orders the universe of the whole creation which it founded from nothing by the rules of its providence, and permits nothing of what it founded to perish—that is, to return entirely to nothing—so the trinity of our nature provides for the universe of its body and the safety of all its senses, and gives it life, moves it, and contains it, as far as its mortal fragility permits.

XXV. But see that you do not begin to surmise from these reasons that the condition of the body precedes the creation of the soul by some temporal space. For the soul precedes the body only in the dignity and excellence of nature, but not in place or time; for at once and simultaneously in that one man who was made in the image of God, the <term>reasons</term> <note>the underlying rational blueprints or principles</note> of all men were created according to body and soul. For in no way does the essence of the soul precede the essence of the body according to the delays of time, just as the essence of the body does not precede the essence of the soul. 

And do not think that I said that first essential body founded in Paradise was created by the soul—though it was made as yet only in reason, just as the soul itself was (for in that general and universal man made in the image of God, all men were founded according to body and soul at once and simultaneously in possibility alone, and in him all sinned before they came forth into their own substances; that is, before each one appeared according to the <term>Angelic multiplication</term> <note>a spiritual form of reproduction or expansion without physical corruption</note> in his distinct difference in a rational soul and a spiritual body: which incorruptible body would have adhered eternally and co-eternally to the soul if he had not sinned, and in which all men are to rise again). For that body was founded substantially by the one Creator of all things, immediately together with the rational soul in heavenly blessedness—or rather, for the sake of heavenly blessedness. I say "substantially" because the true substance of every creature is its reason, foreknown and founded in the <term>primordial causes</term>, by which God defined that it shall be thus and not otherwise. I also said "for the sake of blessedness," for I do not see how man would lose blessedness if he [possessed] it fully in reality...

<summary>Eriugena explains the etymological link between God's goodness and His "calling" of creation into being, outlining a five-fold hierarchy of existence. He then compares the Divine Trinity's governance of the universe to the human soul's governance of the body, asserting that soul and body were created simultaneously in the primordial causes.</summary>
<keywords>Eriugena, Neoplatonism, Five-fold motion, Trinity, Soul and Body, Primordial Causes, Creation from nothing, Gnostic operation, Angelic multiplication</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical discussion from Book One regarding the nature of the human soul and its fall. The author is exploring the origins of the physical, mortal body as a consequence of sin.</meta>

->BOOK TWO<-

...that he might taste it fully and perfectly. For first, as I believe, he turned toward himself rather than toward God, and for that reason he fell. Indeed, I do not hesitate to assert that this corruptible and material body, which was taken from the clay of the earth, was created—and is created daily—by a certain proper operation of the soul. This occurred after sin, by the merit of sin, in order to discipline the soul that had become negligent toward the keeping of the commandments. You should not be disturbed by the fact that Holy Scripture <note>original: "Scriptura Sacra"</note> maintains that God took mud from the earth and formed the body of man from it. For the action of a creature is not unsuitably attributed to Him from whom every natural action begins. 

Indeed, even among the celestial essences—descending by degrees from the first order, which begins immediately after God, down to the lowest—whatever a superior order administers and perfects in an inferior order through its own specific action, the whole of it is attributed to Him from whom every natural motion starts and from whom natural action descends from the highest to the lowest. For the Cause of all things itself, although not through itself (since it is immutable), creates, moves, and governs the entire universe of nature established by it through the creature subjected to it; nevertheless, the entire dispensation of divine providence is poured back into that Cause, because it is the cause of all things. 

What shall I say of the orders of the Church established in this still-mortal life, over which the order of Bishops is placed? Is not everything which the other subsequent arrangements perform in their distributed offices reduced to that Bishop, since the lower orders receive from him what they must carry out? For by him, individual symbolic ministries <note>The author uses "Symbolica ministeria" to describe the sacramental duties of the clergy.</note> are distributed to individuals, and the spiritual operation of the whole Church is referred through him back to the Cause itself—I mean God—of all good and mystical actions. 

Why then is it a wonder if the first man, created in the image of God and having transgressed the divine precept (and through this being expelled from the blessedness of Paradise), should create for himself a fragile and mortal dwelling-place from the mud of the earth? He was admonished by divine providence so that, because he had proudly neglected to possess and guard the celestial and spiritual body created by God Himself, he might—having been humbled—make for himself a mortal lodging taken from earthly matter, a fit reward for his disobedience. In this lodging, having been punished, he might repent; and having been exercised, he might seek to return to the former dignity of his nature by recognizing and humbling himself. Nor is Scripture silent on this matter.

XXVI. For immediately after the transgression, speaking of the simple human nature (which was divided into two sexes after the fall, though it was one before sin), it says: *And they sewed fig leaves together, and made themselves aprons* <note>Genesis 3:7. The author interprets the "aprons" or "coverings" as a metaphor for the physical body.</note>, openly suggesting by the symbol of the leaves the fragile and fleeting condition of this mortal body, which man wove for himself after his ruin. Indeed, our mortal bodies are very much like broad fig leaves. For just as those leaves create shade and shut out the rays of the sun, so our bodies both bring the darkness of ignorance upon our souls and repel the knowledge of the truth. Yet these leaves of ours—I mean our earthly bodies—are accustomed to hedge in and overshadow the sweetness of earthly and deadly delights on every side, and to deceive us in them. 

And so that you may recognize that the creation of our mortal body is most clearly attributed to Him by whose counsel all things are done which we read concerning our discipline, renovation, and salvation, hear the same Scripture saying: *The Lord God also made for Adam and his wife tunics of skin, and clothed them.* <note>Genesis 3:21.</note> Here we understand, not incongruously, that nothing else is signified by the "tunics of skin" except the mortal bodies which the first humans made for themselves after their transgression, by the permission of the Creator's just judgment. And does it seem more credible to you that a mortal man made mortal flesh for himself, rather than that God created it through Himself? Rather, He only permitted and admonished it. For God is immortal, and whatever He makes through Himself is immortal. 

Indeed, everything mortal—whatever is seen to exist in this sensible world that is both fragile and transitory—either we ourselves make by our wandering, irrational motions, or it is permitted to happen because of our fault. This occurs either through the administration of good powers or through evil powers that hinder the natural course (lest it reach its end) by established intervals. For no one who philosophizes correctly doubts that a vital motion acts within seeds <note>original: "seminibus"; referring to the biological or metaphysical "seeds" of life.</note> so that they may proceed through generation into visible forms; but that vital motion does not always manifest the power of its action equally in every kind, whether because of certain accidents which do not suit the seeds and are born from contrary qualities, or because of adverse powers (as we said) which resist natural motion, concerning which there is another place for discussion. But perhaps someone will say: that first incorruptible body, which would have adhered to the soul if it had not sinned, where is it now? For that which is incorruptible cannot perish. To this I respond briefly—

<summary>The author argues that the physical, mortal body is a consequence of the Fall, created by the soul as a "mortal lodging" for repentance. He interprets the biblical "tunics of skin" and "fig leaves" as metaphors for the material flesh that obscures spiritual truth.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between a Master (M) and a Disciple (D) regarding the division of nature, specifically focusing on the resurrection of the body and the nature of the soul as the image of God. The previous page likely discussed the hidden nature of the soul's essence.</meta>

->ON THE DIVISION OF NATURE<-

...to be answered, still lies hidden within the secret boundaries of its own nature; however, in the world to come, it will appear; when this mortal [body] shall be changed into that [immortal] one, and this corruptible shall be clothed in incorruption. Listen to the Apostle:
> *The animal body is sown, the spiritual body shall rise: it is sown in weakness, it is sown in power: it is sown in dishonor, it shall rise in glory*, <note>original: "Seminatur corpus animale, surget corpus spirituale..." quoting 1 Corinthians 15:42-44.</note>

namely, the glory of immortality and incorruptibility, whether in the good or in the evil. For I judge this to have been said generally concerning every human body, for in all there will be the glory of eternal immortality, though not of equal blessedness, since the whole of nature shall be restored.

### XXVII.
**D.** Enough has been said on this question for now, I think; we should return to the consideration of that Trinity in which we were created according to the image and likeness of God, and carefully consider whether that image imitates the likeness of Him whose image it is in all things, or whether it fails in some point of dissimilarity and does not entirely reach the status of a perfect image. For insofar as it imitates, it is rightly called an image; but if it wavers in anything, it departs from the logic of a perfect image. **M.** We believe that man was made most perfectly according to the image and likeness of God, and failed in nothing in Paradise before sin, except for the "logic of the subject" <note>The "logic of the subject" refers to the fact that the creature is a dependent being, whereas the Creator is self-subsisting.</note>. For God, subsisting through Himself and receiving the power to subsist through Himself from no predecessor, brought man from nothing into essence according to His own image and likeness. Note, therefore, that God possesses His essence through Himself as *without beginning* <note>original: ἄναρχος (anarchos)</note>, that is, without a starting point; man, however, created from nothing, had a beginning to his condition, not only in the primordial causes in which all things were created at once, but also in the processions into diverse essences and forms, whether intellectual or sensible. All other things which are said and understood concerning God through essential excellence are clearly seen in His Image through grace. Indeed, the Creator—invisible, incomprehensible, surpassing all understanding—created His image to be like Him in all things. For our intellect is neither known by itself as to *what* it is according to its essence, nor by any other besides God; but just as it knows only this much about its Creator—that He *is*—but does not perceive *what* He is: so concerning itself, it defines only that it was created; but in what way or in what substance it was established, it cannot understand. For if it understood in any way *what* it is, it would necessarily deviate from the likeness of the Creator. For God is the *Archetype* <note>original: Πρωτότυπον (Prototypon)</note>, that is, the principal example, by nature; the image is God by grace. The Archetype is diffused through all things, distributing essence to them; the image, purified by the light of grace, illuminated, and perfected, runs through all things, confirming the knowledge of them within itself. The Archetype penetrates all things He has made, dividing His gifts to each according to their own *proportion* <note>original: analogiam</note>; the image surveys all things, praising the Giver of goods for all His innumerable distributed gifts. For some gifts are substantial, others are added to the substance. And just as the Archetype created His image in which He might show a certain knowledge of Himself, so the image made an image for itself <note>The body</note> in which it might manifest its movements through hidden means. For the soul is the image of God, but the body is the image of the soul, and so on for the other things to be understood and pronounced concerning the likeness of the image. Whoever wishes to know more fully about these things, let him read the book of Saint Gregory of Nyssa *On the Image* <note>Referring to "De opificio hominis" (On the Making of Man).</note>. **D.** I accept these things gladly; but I see another difference besides the substantial one, which seems to divide the image from the principal form. **M.** Tell me, I ask, what that is. **D.** Does it seem to you a small difference between that nature which knows itself, both that it is and *what* it is, and that nature which only knows that it is, but does not understand *what* it is? For you will not deny, I estimate, that God Himself understands *what* He is; but we do not deny that other created essences and substances cannot understand themselves, lest we seem to impudently resist Gregory of Nazianzus and the aforementioned Gregory of Nyssa, who unanimously prove by certain reasons that no created essence can be defined as to *what* it is by itself, or by any other endowed with reason or intellect. Do you see, therefore, the dissimilarity of the image and its principal form—that is, of the human intellect and God the Creator—not only in the logic of the subject, but also in this: that the Form itself knows *what* it is, but the image understands how to define substantially neither itself, nor its Form which it imitates?

### XXVIII.
**M.** I see that you have been deceived by a likeness of true reasoning; and not without cause, for unless one looks sharply and diligently at those things in which you seem to be mistaken, they will be thought not only probable, but even true. **D.** Explain, I pray, where I am mistaken. **M.** Does the divine essence seem to you to be infinite, or finite? **D.** To doubt this is both impious and most foolish, especially since He is not [merely] essence, but more-than-essence, and essential...

<summary>The Master and Disciple discuss how the human soul is a perfect image of God because, like God, its true essence is incomprehensible and indefinable even to itself. They debate whether God's self-knowledge constitutes a difference between the Creator and the created image.</summary>
<keywords>image of God, resurrection, immortality, Gregory of Nyssa, Gregory of Nazianzus, divine essence, self-knowledge, Archetype, soul and body</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between a Master (M) and a Disciple (D) regarding the nature of God and the limits of human reason, specifically focusing on the concept of infinity and the categories of Aristotle.</meta>

# SECOND BOOK

->K 3<-

...that the infinite cause of all things must be both believed and understood; and not only infinite, but the infinity of all infinite essences, and more than infinite. 

**Master:** Correct and according to the universal faith; therefore, He is altogether infinite. 

**Disciple:** I have granted this, and I do not regret granting it, but I most firmly approve that it cannot be otherwise. 

**Master:** See that you do not withdraw your word. 

**Disciple:** There is no suspicion of that. 

**Master:** Therefore, when we ask "what is this or that," does it seem to you that we seek anything else but either a substance already defined, or one capable of being defined? 

**Disciple:** Nothing else. For this word "what," while it is interrogative, seeks nothing except that the substance it seeks be in some way defined. 

**Master:** If, then, no wise man inquires generally about every essence "what it is," since it cannot be defined; but rather inquires from its circumstances—within which it is circumscribed like boundaries, I mean place and time, quantity and quality, connection, position, motion, habit, and the other accidents <note>In philosophy, "accidents" are qualities that can change without changing the essential nature of a thing, like color or size.</note> by which the distance of the subject itself, unknown and indefinable in itself, is manifested only as *being*, but not *what* it is—what learned student of Theology would presume to ask about the divine substance "what it is," when he most clearly understands that it can neither be defined, nor is it any of those things that exist, but surpasses all things that can be defined? 

**Disciple:** I would not presume to ask this, knowing that He is altogether infinite. Hear Gregory of Nazianzus <note>A 4th-century Church Father known as "The Theologian."</note>: *If there is nothing at all*, he says, *of existing things according to being itself, the very accumulation of those things which are understood and spoken by us concerning that which is sought, is not the thing itself; but it is something else besides that about which these things are; for it contains them, but is in no way contained by them: let every soul rest from every reason of those things which are around God, nor rashly leap into a definition of Him, but in silence let it worship only the ineffable truth of the divine essence, which is above intellect and the summit of all knowledge.*

**Master:** If, therefore, none of the wisest can know the reasons of the substance of existing things according to which they are founded, who would dare to define God in anything? He could be defined by Himself, and through this He would not be universally infinite, but particularly so. If He cannot be defined by the creature alone, but can be by Himself; or to put it this way, if He exists as finite to Himself but infinite to the creature; and if this were granted, it would necessarily follow that either God is not universally infinite, if He does not receive definition from the creature alone but does from Himself; or He is not defined at all, neither by the creature nor by Himself, so that He may be universally infinite and in no way perceive definition. 

**Disciple:** I judge the darkness of this present reasoning to be inaccessible, and unless He who is sought extends His right hand to those seeking Him, I would easily believe there is no entry into it. For if God does not define Himself, or cannot be defined by Himself, who would deny that ignorance and impotence fall upon Him? Ignorance, namely, if He does not understand what He is; and impotence, if He cannot define what exists. For He will seem to be impotent when He cannot define Himself in anything; but if He both understands and defines what He is, He will be proved not to be altogether infinite, while He cannot be defined by the creature alone because He is in no way understood by it; yet by Himself He is both defined and known as to what He is. 

**Master:** Do not be troubled, but rather be of good heart, for this consideration draws us to understand ourselves, and leads us to those things which are to be piously thought, understood, and pronounced concerning our God, with Him leading the way. For as much as it is thought dark and laborious at the entrance of the inquiry, so much will it be found clear and fruitful. *For it cannot be (as Saint Augustine says) by a certain divine providence, that the power of finding should be lacking to religious souls piously and studiously seeking themselves and their God, that is, the truth.*

**Disciple:** I am not troubled, but I am moved, and rightly so, by the obscurity of the present question; and I do not think its solution will be easy. 

**Master:** Let us return, then, to those things which were discussed between us in the first book and (unless I am mistaken) brought to a clear conclusion. 

**Disciple:** Remind me of those, I pray. 

**Master:** Do you not remember <note>original: "Recordariine" (likely a typo for "Recordari ne")</note> that it was clearly settled between us that none of the categories concluded in the number of ten <note>Referring to Aristotle's ten categories: substance, quantity, quality, relation, place, time, position, state, action, and passion.</note> can in any way be properly predicated of the divine nature? 

**Disciple:** That was unshakably both granted and established. 

**Master:** We shall not, therefore, sweat as laboriously as you think in solving the difficulty of this question, if we acutely perceive the settled conclusions of the first book. Indeed, the subtle and perceptive utility of those conclusions will be manifested in this place to bear the most beautiful and useful fruit. 

**Disciple:** It will be very necessary, if it be so. 

**Master:** It plainly will; the series of reasoning seems to demand that we briefly recall those categories to memory. 

**Disciple:** No other way of inquiry is pleasing. However, I would like you to recapitulate them interrogatively. 

**Master:** Look, then, intently at this order of questioning: What, how much, what kind, toward what, in what position, in what habit, in what place, at what time, does it act or suffer? Likewise, if you wish, in Greek: *What, how much, what kind, toward something, to lie, to have, where, when, or to do, or to suffer...* <note>original: "Τί, πόσον, ποιὸν, πρὸς τι, κεῖσθαι, ἔχειν, ποῦ, πότε, ἢ πράττειν, η πα..."</note>

<summary>The Master and Disciple discuss the impossibility of defining God's essence, arguing that God is "more than infinite" and beyond the ten Aristotelian categories. They conclude that if God could be defined even by Himself, He would not be truly infinite, leading to a mystical paradox of divine "ignorance" of His own definition.</summary>
<keywords>divine infinity, apophatic theology, Aristotle's categories, Gregory of Nazianzus, St. Augustine, definition of God, divine essence, substance, accidents</keywords>

--- Page 94 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between a Master (M) and a Disciple (D) regarding the nature of God's "ignorance"—the idea that God does not "know" what He is because He is not a "what" (a defined thing).</meta>

# BOOK TWO

->**Page 81**<-

However, He does not understand Himself to be in any of these things, but recognizes that He is above all orders of nature by the excellence of His wisdom, and below all things by the depth of His power, and within all things by the unsearchable distribution of His providence, and that He encompasses all things; for in Him are all things, and outside of Him there is nothing. For He alone is measure without measure, number without number, weight without weight; and rightly so, because He is measured, numbered, or ordered by no one, not even by Himself, nor does He understand Himself to be in any measure, in any number, or in any order, since He is not substantially contained in any of them, as He alone truly exists as the infinite one in all things and above all things.

And do not object to me why I said that the order of bodies is not able to extend itself into natures higher than itself, when we believe that all bodies are destined to pass into incorporeal qualities and substances. For when this happens, they will cease to be bodies. Yet while they are bodies, they cannot transgress the boundaries of their nature, neither lower nor higher. Concerning this part of Philosophy, we shall treat it more diligently when we consider the return of things into their causes. <note>A reference to the "Reditus," a major theme in Eriugena's work regarding the return of all creation to God.</note> But now let us look at the matter at hand: that God does not understand *what* He is. And do not be ashamed to admit whether what we are attempting to persuade you of regarding this seems probable to you.

**D.** Concerning this wonderful divine ignorance, by which God does not understand what He Himself is, I confess that what you have said—though obscure—seems to me not false, but true and likely. For you are not suggesting that God is ignorant of Himself, but only that He is ignorant of *what* He is, and rightly so; because He is not a "what," since He is infinite both to Himself and to all things that come from Him; and through this, in this species of ignorance, the highest and ineffable wisdom most clearly and beautifully smiles upon us. *For the foolishness of God is wiser than men.* <note>original: "Stultum enim Dei sapientius est hominibus." A quote from 1 Corinthians 1:25.</note>

**M.** What then if someone should ask about God, how great or of what kind He is; would it seem right to you to answer, "so great" and "of such a kind"? I do not mean that quantity and quality of which the Prophet speaks: *Great is the Lord and greatly to be praised, and of His greatness there is no end.* <note>Psalm 148:3.</note> See how loftily the Theologian speaks: "Great," he says, "is the Lord." But lest anyone should think He is limited by a finite quantity, he immediately adds, "and of His greatness there is no end." Likewise, lest anyone should think there is a finite quality in Him, he did not simply say "and praiseworthy," but added "greatly" <note>original: "nimis," meaning exceedingly or beyond measure.</note>; for that is called "greatly" which exceeds every limit. Therefore, I do not mean (as I said) that infinite quantity, nor that quality which is "greatly." For these are not incongruously thought of concerning God; but rather the quantity and quality which are spoken of as accidents in a subject. <term>Accident: A philosophical term for a property that can change or be absent without the subject ceasing to be what it is (e.g., being "tall" or "tan").</term>

**D.** Certainly not right. For where a defined substance or (so to speak) a defined subject is not found, it seems to me very foolish and worthy of laughter to seek and affirm quantity and quality there. And through this, while neither God Himself nor any other intellect can find in Him any defined substance or defined subject according to which it might be said or understood *what* He is, does it not appear most clearly that no finite or infinite quantity, no finite or infinite quality, can be known in Him by Himself or by another? For if He exalts above every finite and infinite substance by the infinite and more-than-infinite excellence of His own power, who would not immediately and without any doubt burst forth to openly confess and exclaim that no finite or infinite quantity or quality falls upon Him, or that He is in any way a "what" at all? For where a "what" is found, there immediately is a "how much" and "of what kind." But where there is no "what," it is impossible to find "how much" and "of what kind." If, therefore, in the genera of things neither "what," nor "how much," nor "of what kind," nor any accident is understood—because they are simple—and these are sought only in individuals, who but a fool would dare to seek "what" or "how much" or "of what kind" in God?

**M.** What then? Do you think there is in Him "relation," which is called <term>πρός τι</term> <note>Greek: "pros ti," meaning "toward something" or "relation."</note> by the Greeks?

**D.** I would not say even this. Where a defined substance cannot be understood, I have no doubt that no relation can occur. For relation is not according to nature, but according to a certain coupling of two or more subjects. Concerning the rest of the categories, it seems superfluous to speak of them one by one, I think. For if God Himself does not understand Himself in any defined substance, or quantity, or quality, or relation, to whom will it not appear clearer than light that no position or habit, no place or time, no acting or suffering <note>These refer to Aristotle's Ten Categories: Substance, Quantity, Quality, Relation, Place, Time, Position, State, Action, and Passion.</note> happens to Him at all, and through this none of them can be understood in Him by Himself or by another? Since a defined substance is not found or understood in Him, it is for the ignorant to doubt that none of the accidents belong to Him. For where a defined subject is lacking, there no accident is understood as joined, or separate, or in any way distinct by the nature of the subject. But to speak briefly of each, which we treated copiously in the previous book; who would give a "position" to the divine nature, while it is in itself...

<summary>This page explores the concept of "Divine Ignorance," arguing that because God is infinite and transcends all categories of being (like quantity, quality, and relation), He does not "know" Himself as a defined thing or "what."</summary>
<keywords>Divine Ignorance, Negative Theology, Aristotle's Categories, Infinity of God, Eriugena, Substance, Relation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue regarding the "Division of Nature," specifically exploring how the ten Aristotelian categories (position, habit, place, time, action, passion, etc.) do not apply to the Divine Essence. It follows the argument that God is "ignorant" of His own definition because He is infinite.</meta>

# ON THE DIVISION OF NATURE
### Page 82

Does [the Divine Nature] recognize any **position**? For position belongs to parts within a place. For example, the position of the human body is the arrangement of its limbs, because every limb is limited within its own order. Position is also called the posture of the whole body—for instance, standing, sitting, or lying down. No one who philosophizes rightly is unaware that the power of the Divine Essence is entirely lacking in all of these. For He is neither a whole nor a part; He does not sit as if weary, nor lie down as if prostrate, nor stand still after any movement. The Son is said to "sit at the right hand of the Father"—the whole Son, both Word and flesh. The "right hand of the Father" is the power of the Father, the strength of the Father; thus the Son himself is the right hand of the Father. Therefore, where He sits, He sits within Himself, remaining **consubstantial** <note>original: "consubstantialis"; of the same substance or essence</note> with the Father, judging all things in tranquility. He "stands" for Stephen <note>A reference to the martyrdom of St. Stephen in Acts 7:55-56</note>, fighting alongside His soldiers, giving them the strength to conquer on earth; the right hand of the Lord has performed a feat of strength, granting them the crown of confession in heaven. See how He sits and how He stands! This "position" is mystical, not physical; not local, but spiritual. Nevertheless, recognize this as the "position" of the Divine Nature.

Who could assign **habit** <note>The category of "having" or "clothing/disposition"</note> to Him, while He perceives no habit within Himself? Indeed, He is sufficient unto Himself for "having," and no virtue happens to Him as an accident, since He subsists within Himself as the simplest virtue—and more than virtue—and the source of all virtues. Whatever substance possesses virtue does not have it from elsewhere except by participation in those general virtues which the Causal Virtue of all things established in its **primordial principles**.

The Divine Nature lacks all **place**, although it places all things that come from it within itself. Therefore, it is called the "place of all things," yet it does not know how to "place" itself, because it is infinite and **uncircumscribed** <note>original: "incircumscripta"; without boundaries or limits</note>, and it permits itself to be placed—that is, defined and limited—by no intellect, not even its own. Indeed, from that which is infinite and more than infinite, all things finite and infinite proceed, and into the infinite and more than infinite they return.

That nature lacks **time**, for it does not know itself to have a beginning or an end, nor any motion by which everything that moves from a beginning toward an end is moved. It knows no increase within itself, which occurs through the numbers of places and times; nor does it know decrease, since it is full and perfect in itself.

What should be said of **acting** and **suffering** <note>The categories of "action" and "passion" (being acted upon)</note>? Is it not inept and incongruous if someone thinks that acting or suffering happens to that Nature, which perceives in itself no motion for acting and no capacity for suffering? For it is not as it is in us, where substance is one thing and the **accident** <note>A non-essential quality that can change without changing the essence of the thing</note> of the substance is another (that is, action and passion). Thus, [the Divine Nature] recognizes no composition of substance and accidents within itself. It lacks these things and knows they are not in it, for it is most simple and foreign to all composition. Indeed, His will—which is Himself substantially, and more than substantially, and more than will—is His action and passion. For He is said to "act" because He wills all things that exist to be made; but He is said to "suffer" because He wills to be loved by all, and He loves Himself in all things. For He is substantial and true Love, and more than substantial Love; and all things love Him, whether they know it or not—that is, whether by an intellectual or rational motion led by grace, or by a simple natural appetite. For nothing has been created by Him that does not have a desire for Him.

Do you see, therefore, that regarding all things embraced within the boundaries of the **ten categories** <note>Aristotle's classification of all things that can be predicated of a subject</note>, God is not undeservedly said to "not know" that He subsists in His own nature, when He is proved to excel entirely by the height of His power and His infinity? For that which is infinite by every reason and mode is infinite by essence, by power, by operation, and by both ends—I mean "upwards" and "downwards," that is, according to beginning and end. It is incapable of being grasped according to essence, unintelligible according to power, uncircumscribed according to operation, without beginning from above, and without end from below; it is infinite, and simply to be called—more truly through all things—infinite.

**D.** <note>The Disciple</note> I see clearly and perceive that this is true, fortified by the conclusions of true reasoning. But it moves me greatly: by what reason does "ignorance" fall upon God, from whom nothing is hidden, either in Himself or in those things which are from Him?

**M.** <note>The Master</note> Be attentive, then, and look diligently at what has been said. For if you consider the power of things and words with a pure gaze of the mind, you will find most clearly, with no darkness standing in the way, that no ignorance falls upon God. For His "ignorance" is an **ineffable intelligence**; and so that we may attempt to prove this from what has been said, look at the power of the words. Does it seem to suggest anything else to you when we say "God does not know what He is," than to say "He understands that He is none of the things that exist"? For how can He recognize in Himself that which cannot be in Himself? For if the "reasons" of things—which He created in Himself (that is, the Father in the Son)—are one individual thing in Him, and they receive no definition of their own substance, nor their own differences or accidents (for they undergo these things in their effects, but not in themselves), what must be thought of His own ineffable and ungraspable nature?

<summary>The Master explains that God is "ignorant" of His own definition because He transcends all Aristotelian categories like place, time, and action. This "divine ignorance" is actually a form of infinite intelligence, as God recognizes He is not any finite, definable thing.</summary>
<keywords>divine nature, ten categories, infinity, divine ignorance, consubstantial, action and passion, primordial principles, apophatic theology</keywords>

--- Page 96 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical and theological exploration of "divine ignorance" and the limits of knowledge, likely following a discussion on the nature of God's essence.</meta>

# SECOND BOOK

Who could imagine anything in [the Divine] that is limited by a boundary, extended in space, divided into parts, or composed of substances and accidents? Therefore, this very ignorance is the highest and true wisdom. This is like what we say: for example, if one of us were to say of himself, "I do not understand myself to be a stone, unfeeling and lacking all vital motion"; this means, "I understand that I am entirely *not* an unfeeling stone lacking vital motion." Likewise, I do not understand any living man in the flesh to lack sense and reason; because I know that every man living in the flesh does *not* lack sense and reason. Similarly, I do not understand any irrational motion to exist naturally in my soul; that is, I know most certainly that no irrational motion is naturally present in my soul. 

And we can prove this from Holy Scripture: for we read in the Gospel that the Lord, in the future judgment, will answer the wicked, *I never knew you* <note>Matthew 7:23</note>; that is, "I did not know you in the patterns of all things which the Father created in me, since I understand that you are not among my own—not in terms of how I naturally established you, but in how you have fallen away from the laws of your nature." For I know what I made in you, but I do not know what I did not make; and therefore I do not punish nor command to depart from me that which I recognize in myself; but that which I do not recognize in myself, that I punish and command to depart from me. God, therefore, is "ignorant" of that which He did not make in the impious and in sinners—namely, their malignant and irrational motions. 

There is also another species of ignorance in God, when He is said to be ignorant of those things which He foreknew and predestined, while they have not yet appeared through experience in the course of created things. Hence He says in the Gospel: *But of that day and hour no one knows, not even the angels in heaven, nor the Son of Man, but the Father only* <note>Mark 13:32</note>. In this, it is no wonder if the knowledge of the future judgment remains hidden from human minds still weighed down by mortal flesh. Nor do we entirely deny that the Angels are still capable of ignorance, since Holy Scripture maintains they are always learning, as Saint Dionysius the Areopagite <note>A 5th-6th century Christian theologian and philosopher</note> witnesses in the seventh chapter of *The Celestial Hierarchy*, saying: 

> The theologians clearly declare that the subordinate ranks of the celestial essences are orderly instructed in deifying sciences by those above them; but the highest of all are illuminated (as far as is permitted) by the Divinity itself, and Jesus himself teaches them immediately, showing them his manifest human kindness. For I (he says) discuss justice and the judgment of salvation; I marvel (he says) because even the first of the celestial essences, and all the pre-eminent divine illuminations together, reverently seek answers to their questions. Do they not ask from there, "Why are your garments red?" <note>Isaiah 63:2</note> They deliberate among themselves before asking, showing indeed that they are learning and that they desire deifying knowledge.

If therefore, by the testimony of the aforementioned Father, the celestial powers learn, they are necessarily not devoid of all ignorance. And if this is so, it is not surprising that they can still be ignorant of the mysteries of the future judgment.

But what the Lord says of himself—*nor the Son of Man, but the Father only*—creates a most obscure question. For how can the Son be ignorant of what the Father knows, especially concerning the day of judgment, when the judgment specifically belongs to the Son? For the Father (as He himself says) *judges no one, but has given all judgment to the Son* <note>John 5:22</note>. However, Saint Epiphanius, Bishop of Constantia in Cyprus, in the book he wrote on the faith, opened the darkness of this question most elegantly and accurately, saying: 

> The Father alone knows the future judgment, not only through foreknowledge, but also through experience. For the Father knows the judgment through experience, since He has already in reality given all judgment to the Son; for the Father has entirely completed the judgment by giving it all to the Son. The Son, however, both knows and does not know the judgment. For He knows it by foreknowledge, but not yet by experience; and therefore He does not know it by experience because the judgment has not yet actually taken place—that is, the separation of the wicked from the elect—since the harvest of the Church is still mixed with wheat and tares.

Hear also of Apostolic ignorance, of which it is written in the Acts of the Apostles. When Paul was speaking harshly to the high priest Ananias, he said: *I did not know, brothers, that he is the high priest. For it is written: You shall not speak evil of a ruler of your people* <note>Acts 23:5</note>. Does it seem to you that the most wise Apostle, so skilled in the law, did not know Ananias was the high priest, as the other Jews did? This is by no means to be believed; rather, he is said to have been "ignorant" of him because he did not see him as having been ordained by God. "I was ignorant, brothers," he says, "that he is the high priest, because I know that he was appointed neither by God nor according to the law, but according to Jewish superstition; and therefore I am ignorant that he is the High Priest, since I truly know that he is not so. For if I knew him to be a true and legitimate ruler of the people, I certainly would not speak evil of him." Since I am truly ignorant that he is a ruler in truth, I do not repent of speaking harshly to him. 

Observe another ignorance of the same man. *I know a man (he says) caught up even to the third heaven; whether in the body or out of the body, I do not know: God knows* <note>2 Corinthians 12:2</note>. I do not know (he says) in the body, when I know it was not in the body; or I do not know if it was out of the body; because out of the body...

<summary>This page explores the theological concept of "divine ignorance," arguing that God's "not knowing" refers to things He did not create (like sin) or things not yet realized in time. It uses examples from the Gospels, St. Paul, and Dionysius the Areopagite to show that ignorance can be a form of higher understanding or a lack of experiential knowledge.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue on the nature of God, specifically focusing on the concept of "divine ignorance" as a form of infinite knowledge. The speaker is likely the Teacher (Magister) addressing the Student (Discipulus).</meta>

# ON THE DIVISION OF NATURE

I recognize that I was not snatched away in the body. For I was not snatched into the third heaven by the operations of the soul through the bodily senses, nor by its operations outside the body; rather, I know most clearly that I was snatched above every creature by the operation of divine grace, without the administration of any created thing. Therefore, I do not know whether it was in the body or out of the body, because I know that I was snatched neither in the body nor out of the body. <note>The author is referencing St. Paul’s vision in 2 Corinthians 12:2, interpreting "not knowing" as a state of being beyond physical or mental categories.</note> And lest we dwell too long on the same matter (for time will fail us before we run out of examples of divine ignorance, whether in Scripture or in the nature of things), I think it suffices to know this much: there are three principal types of divine ignorance.

The first is that by which God does not know evil, because His knowledge is simple and is formed only by the substantial good—that is, by Himself. For He alone is the substantial good through Himself; all other good things are good by participation in Him. Thus, God does not know evil. For if He knew evil, evil would necessarily exist in the nature of things. Since divine knowledge is the cause of all things that exist, God does not know things because they exist; rather, they exist because God knows them. Divine knowledge is the cause of their essence, and through this, if God knew evil, it would be understood to exist substantially in something, and evil would be a participant in good, and vice would proceed from virtue and malice from goodness—which true reason teaches is impossible.

The second type is that by which God is said to be ignorant of things other than those whose patterns <note>original: "rationes" - the eternal ideas or blueprints of things</note> He both made and knows eternally within Himself. For those things of which He naturally possesses the power, He essentially possesses the knowledge. The third is that by which God is said to be ignorant (as we said before) of those things which do not yet appear manifest by the experience of action and operation in their effects; yet He possesses their invisible patterns, created by Him and known to Him within Himself.

To these aforementioned types, a fourth is added, which the order of our discussion now requires us to treat: that by which God is said to be ignorant that He is among the number of things which were made by Him, which the Philosophers attempt to enclose within the ten-fold quantity of the categories. <note>The "ten categories" of Aristotle: substance, quantity, quality, relation, place, time, position, state, action, and passion.</note> And through these it must be said universally: God does not understand Himself to exist in any of those things which are comprised by the Philosophers within the ten genera of things, nor in those things which more diligent inquiry finds outside of them—whether they be according to substance or according to accident; nor in those things which can be found in no substance or accident, whether they are in hidden patterns, or in possibilities, or in impossibilities. For He knows Himself to be none of them, while He understands Himself to excel all things by His ineffable essential power—and more than power—and by His incomprehensible infinity.

XXIX. Moreover, I said that diligent inquiry could find certain things in the nature of things beyond those comprised by the ten categories. For those too were discovered by the Philosophers, lest anyone of lesser capacity should think that the diligent investigation of things cannot proceed beyond the aforementioned quantity of categories. For reason encompasses the more general genera of these; indeed, they exist in motion and in rest. Likewise, rest and motion are gathered into a universal essence, which allows for the division of itself into infinity. For that substance which holds the first place in bodily things is finite and subject to accidents; but that universal essence receives no accident into itself. Indeed, in its subdivisions reaching down to individuals, it is capable of accidents, but in itself it is simple and subject to no accidents.

As for what I added—that there are certain things in the nature of things which are known neither in substance nor in accidents (that is, which are neither substances nor accidents)—understand this to be said of patterns not yet appearing in any thing, whether substantially or according to accident, to the sense or the intellect. Also, no one among those who philosophize correctly will contradict that possibilities and impossibilities are counted in the number of things; which are said to exist for no other reason than that possibilities *can* happen in some thing, even if they are not; while impossibilities are contained solely by the power of impossibility; for their "being" is the impossibility of truly appearing in any intellectual or sensible thing. Concerning these, whoever wishes to perceive them fully should read *On Interpretation* <note>original: "peri hermeneias"</note> by Aristotle; in which the Philosopher disputed either about these alone—that is, possibilities and impossibilities—or at least chiefly about them. Now, therefore, let us pass on to consider what remains. It seems enough has been discussed concerning the types of divine ignorance by which God is said not to know.

<term>D.</term> <note>D. stands for Discipulus (Student)</note> Enough indeed, and more than enough; I see it is not necessary to dwell on them any longer. For it has been made clear, brighter than a sunbeam, that by "divine ignorance" nothing else is to be understood but the incomprehensible and infinite divine knowledge. For what the Holy Fathers—I mean Augustine and Dionysius—most truly pronounce concerning God; Augustine indeed, who says [God is better known]

# BOOK TWO

He is known through not knowing; and I believe that Dionysius <note>Dionysius the Areopagite, a 5th-century mystical theologian who argued that God is best described by what He is not.</note>—whose "ignorance is true wisdom"—should be understood this way not only regarding the intellects that seek him piously and studiously, but even regarding God himself. For just as those who investigate by the right path of reasoning cannot understand Him in any of the things contained in the nature of the world, but know Him as exalted above all things; and through this, their ignorance is true wisdom, and by not knowing Him in the things that exist, they know Him better above all things that are and are not: so also it is not irrationally said of Himself, that insofar as He does not understand Himself to exist within the things He has made, He understands Himself to be above all things. And through this, His own ignorance is true intelligence. Insofar as He does not know Himself to be grasped within the things that are, He knows Himself to be exalted beyond all things; and therefore, by not knowing Himself, He is better known by Himself. For it is better to know oneself as removed from all things than to know oneself as established in the number of all things. 

**M.** You understand correctly; and I perceive that you see clearly and without doubt what reason suggests about such matters. Nor do you now see, I think, any difference between the image <note>the human soul</note> and the original form, except for the nature of the subject.

For the Supreme Trinity exists substantially through itself and was created from no cause; but our trinity of nature was made from nothing by that very Trinity which is eternal in itself, according to its image and likeness. And if any difference is found beyond this—between the image and the original pattern—it does not proceed from nature, but happens through sin; not from the envy of the creating Trinity, but from the fault of the created image. For everything that is understood of God according to the power of His essence can also be said and understood of His image in those who are purified, illuminated, and perfected, according to the grace of creation—excepting, as we said before, that the divine nature is God by excellence of essence, while the human nature is God by the bounty of divine grace. And that the former is the creator and created by none, while the latter is created by the former; and the soul creates those things which adhere to its nature below itself—I mean this mortal body, joined to the soul after sin, which is also called the "image of an image." For just as God created the soul in His image, so the soul made the body as a kind of instrument, in some way similar to itself. But we must return to the <term>Theory</term> <note>original: "Theoria" – here meaning divine contemplation or vision</note> of the divine Trinity, which is the cause of all things, if what has been said of these matters suffices for you. 

**D.** They certainly suffice.

**M.** In the supreme and singular cause of the universe, therefore, from which and in which the principles of the whole creation—that is, the primordial causes—exist and were founded, I think we should consider whether that cause, while being unity and trinity (for the divine goodness is one essence in three substances, and three substances in one essence; or according to the usage of the Roman language, one substance in three persons, and three persons in one substance), has within itself causes that are in some way different from one another. That is, whether just as one essence in three substances is preached of God, so also we must believe and understand one essential cause in three subsisting causes, and three subsisting causes in one essential cause. It is not inappropriate to explain what should be thought and preached about such <term>Theories</term> <note>contemplations</note> in the meditations of the orthodox faith. 

**D.** On the contrary, it is most appropriate and necessary for the salvation of faithful souls. 

**M.** What then? Should we believe, and as far as it is given, understand and preach this concerning the cause of all: one essential cause in three substantial causes, and three substantial causes in one essential cause? 

**D.** It does not occur to me what would prevent this from being understood and preached. For if God is the cause of all, does it not follow that God is known as the cause of all? Or if God is the cause, and the cause is God, is it not a consequence that we should not hesitate to understand everything we must believe about God in the same way, without any discrepancy, regarding the Cause? For if the Catholic faith confesses one God existing through Himself in three substances subsisting through themselves, what stands in the way of us saying, piously and orthodoxly, one Cause existing through itself in three causes subsisting through themselves? Therefore, there is one cause in three causes, and three in one. 

**D.** This is now both granted and given.

### XXX.
**M.** Now, therefore, let us return to <term>Theology</term> <note>original: "Theologia"</note>, which is the first and highest part of wisdom, and not without merit; for it is concerned either solely or mostly with the speculation of the divine nature. It is divided into two parts: affirmation and negation, which in Greek are called <term>Kataphatike</term> <note>affirmative theology</note> and <term>Apaphatike</term> <note>negative theology</note>. We used one of these in the first book, where by certain reasons we denied that the ten categories and all genera, forms, numbers, and accidents of things are properly preached of God. And again in the present book, we have repeated the same <term>Apaphatike</term> as the series of questions required, when we said that God understands none of those things which are, or which are not, in His own essence, because He surpasses all...

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between a Master (M) and a Disciple (D) regarding the nature of God, specifically transitioning from negative theology (what God is not) to affirmative theology (what can be said of God).</meta>

# ON THE DIVISION OF NATURE

essence: and that He Himself, since He is in no way defined, entirely ignores what He is, how great He is, and of what quality He is, because nothing is accidental to Him <note>original: "nihil ei accidit" — meaning God has no "accidents" or non-essential properties in the Aristotelian sense</note>, and He is understood in nothing; and through this, He utterly denies that He is comprehended within those things which are and those which are not—which species of ignorance surpasses all knowledge and understanding. But now we attempt to inspect the other part, which I call <gloss>affirmation</gloss> <note>original: "κατάφασιν" (kataphasin)</note>, under the guidance of Him who is sought, and who seeks that He be sought, and who meets those seeking Him, and desires to be found; and this is the part which contemplates what ought to be brought forward concerning the divine nature as if properly, and to be understood cautiously and rationally. **D.** Regarding the negative part, I have been sufficiently persuaded in the previous sections: but now I wait with burning expectation for what you may explain regarding the affirmative. **M.** It has now been brought to a pure conclusion between us (as I judge), not rashly, that everything which is granted to be understood concerning God by the approval of the Catholic faith, must similarly be confessed by those philosophizing piously concerning the Cause of all things. **D.** This is unshakably established. **M.** Do we not, therefore, believe in the unbegotten and begetting divinity, and the unbegotten deity, and the proceeding deity; and while there is one and inseparable divinity, does it not nevertheless receive substantial differences? For in the Father we receive and religiously believe in the unbegotten deity, in the Son the begotten deity, and in the Holy Spirit the proceeding deity, and we approve this using the most sound authority of Saint Dionysius the Areopagite <note>A 5th-6th century Christian philosopher whose works were central to medieval mysticism</note> and other Fathers. **D.** Whoever hesitates in this stands far from the truth. **M.** There is, therefore, a substantial cause that is unbegotten and begetting; and there is a substantial cause that is begotten; likewise, there is a substantial cause that is proceeding; and the three causes are one, and one essential cause. **D.** From the aforementioned reasons, this is necessarily concluded. **M.** In the Cause of all things, therefore, there is a preceding cause, and there are following causes. For the Father precedes the Son and the Holy Spirit; for from Him the Son is begotten, and the Holy Spirit is proceeding; and through this, the Father is not incongruously believed to be the Cause of causes. For He is the cause of the nascent cause and of the proceeding cause. For fatherhood precedes sonship, but no one of those who are rightly wise has said that sonship precedes fatherhood; hence the Son Himself says of Himself, *The Father is greater than I*, for the Father is greater than the Son, not according to nature, but according to cause. For the Father is the cause of the Son, but the Son is not the cause of the Father, nor do we consider here a reciprocal relation of names, but the power of the substances. For we look at the relationship in the substances or persons in one way, and at the generation or procession in another; in the former, how they are named from one another; in the latter, how they subsist among themselves. Thus the Son is from the Father, but the Father is not from the Son: for in God, the relations of human generations are not considered, but the substantial relationships of ineffable substances. The Father is indeed greater than the Son according to cause. For Sacred Theology first considers the relationship of the Father to the Son, and then of the Son to the Father. And so that you may understand and believe this more certainly, return to the authority of Gregory the Theologian <note>Gregory of Nazianzus, one of the Cappadocian Fathers</note>, who in his first sermon on the Son, disputing against the Arians, wisely teaches that *the Father is greater than the Son according to cause*; defining that the Son exists from the Father, and the Father does not subsist from the Son; yet we do not say this by rejecting the sense of those who assert that our Lord said of Himself according to His humanity, *The Father is greater than I*. Indeed, both understandings are to be received with sound faith. **D.** Yes: for although they are not of the same subtlety and depth, neither departs from the Catholic profession. **M.** Therefore, it is not the same thing for the Father to subsist naturally and to be the cause of His Son. For the nature of the Father is not the cause of the Son, since the nature of the Father and the Son is one and the same, because the essence of both is one and the same. And through this, it is the same thing for the Father to be the Father and the cause of the Son. For these names are not predicated according to nature, which is one and the same in the Father and the Son; but according to the relationship <note>original: "habitum" — the way one person relates to another</note> of the begetter to the begotten, and of the preceding cause to the following cause; just as "Son" or "begotten cause" is not said of the Son according to nature, but according to the relationship of the Son to the Father, and of the begotten cause to the begetting cause. **D.** These things were already persuaded in the previous book, and are now not irrationally repeated again. **M.** The Father, therefore, is the cause of the Son and of the Holy Spirit: but the Son is the cause of the establishment of the causes principally; and of the distribution of those same causes, the Holy Spirit is the cause. **D.** I perceive that to confess that also does not shrink from the truth.

### XXXI.
But to me, thinking and believing such things about the triple cause of all, another darkness occurs. For I do not clearly consider whether the Father alone is the cause of the Holy Spirit, or the Father and the Son; so that just as the Catholic faith confesses that He proceeds from the Father and the Son, we might also believe Him to possess two causes of His procession. For if the Holy Spirit proceeds from two persons or (as the Greeks say) from two substances, what is strange, or contrary to true Religion, to confess that He proceeds from two causes?

<summary>The Master explains the transition from negative to affirmative theology, defining the Father as the "Cause of causes" within the Trinity. The discussion moves toward the theological debate over whether the Holy Spirit proceeds from one or two causes.</summary>
<keywords>Trinity, Affirmative Theology, Kataphasis, Causality, Father, Son, Holy Spirit, Dionysius the Areopagite, Gregory the Theologian, Procession</keywords>

# SECOND BOOK.

-><-

And I demand that you explain this darkness to me. M. It is indeed a most dense darkness, and it envelops not only you, but me as well. And unless the Light itself reveals the beginning to us, the study of our reasoning will profit nothing in revealing it. To the heap of obscurity is also added the fact that the Creed <note>original: "symbolum fidei"</note> handed down by the Council of Nicaea <note>The first ecumenical council in 325 AD which defined the nature of Jesus' divinity.</note> according to the Greeks, professes that the Holy Spirit proceeds from the Father alone, as witnessed by Epiphanius, Bishop of Cyprus, in his book *On the Faith*. According to the Latins, however, He proceeds from the Father and the Son; although in certain Greek expositions we find that the same Spirit proceeds from the Father through the Son. Therefore, struck by the difficulty of the present question, I am tossed by contrary waves of thought. For I am deliberating what I should do about this: whether we should honor it with silence, since it exceeds the strength of our intention; or whether, in some way, as a divine ray shines into the soul, we shall attempt to look upon something between us that is not rashly defined? D. Do not be afraid: for He who is sought does not desert those seeking Him, nor does He deny the possibility of finding Him to those who investigate piously and humbly. For He Himself says, *ask, and you shall receive; seek, and you shall find; knock, and it shall be opened to you.* For everyone who asks, receives; and he who seeks, finds; and to him who knocks, it shall be opened.
<margin>·</margin> M. First, therefore, I think we must speak of those things which in this question seem to contradict themselves. D. Nor is there any other way of seeking, for no one will join together things that agree unless he has first taken care to distinguish things that disagree. M. Does it seem to you to rightly suit the faith that we believe the Son is born from one cause, namely the Father, but the Holy Spirit proceeds from two, namely the Father and the Son? For it does not easily occur to reason that one cause should flow together from two causes, especially in a simple nature—and more than simple—and to speak more truly, in that very simplicity lacking all division and number; but to break forth from one cause can be approved by many examples. For it is clearly evident to all who philosophize rightly that many forms are born from one genus; many numbers from a monad <note>term: monad (the number one or unity as the source of all numbers)</note>; many lines from a point. The diverse forms of things, which are born from one genus, become the cause of individual species, from which and in which multitudes, quantities, qualities, and differences are born. Numbers proceeding from a monad are the causes of diverse proportions; proportions are the causes of proportionalities, and proportionalities of harmonies. Lines proceeding from one point are recognized as the causes of angles and sides, of width and height, which again become the occasions for geometric bodies. What shall I say of the element of fire? Which, though it be one and simple, considered in itself and through itself, is the cause of heat and light at the same time; heat, moreover, is the cause of burning for things that burn; light similarly is established as the cause of shining for things that shine; and splendor begets diverse colors. The element of air is the occasion for diverse voices; diverse voices become the origins of diverse tones. The element of water, though it be simple and one in itself, is the cause of diverse humors, which again emit from themselves diverse qualities of odors, tastes, and greenery. From one earth are born diverse quantities of diverse bodies: I say "quantity" now, not the incorporeal kind, but the mass itself, which no one doubts is a composition of diverse parts; and other things of that kind are very easily found by the same reasoning in the nature of things, whether visible or invisible. D. No faithful person doubts that the Son is born from one cause, that is, from the Father; but whether the Holy Spirit proceeds from one cause, namely the Father, or from two, that is, from the Father and the Son, I would not dare to rashly affirm or deny; and because of this, I do not clearly see how it does not agree with the professions of the right faith, since I have very frequently found the Holy Spirit called the Spirit of the Father and the Son. For He is the Spirit of both, since He proceeds from the Father through the Son, just as He is the gift of both, since He is given from the Father through the Son; and He is the love of both, joining the Father and the Son, as Saint Augustine teaches in the books which he published *On the Supreme and Holy Trinity*, seeking His image in human nature with wonderful investigation, and showing it with most brilliant discovery; proving that the trinity of our interior nature—that is, of the rational soul created in the image of God—is the mind (the principal part of the soul), its self-knowledge (by which it knows itself), and the love (by which it joins itself and its self-knowledge). For the human mind begets its knowledge, by which it knows itself, as a kind of offspring of itself, from itself; and its self-knowledge is equal to itself, because it knows itself entirely, in the likeness of God the Father, who begets from Himself His Son, who is His Wisdom <note>original: "sapientia"</note>, by which He knows Himself; and He is equal to Him, because He understands Himself entirely; and He is co-essential to Him, because He begets from Himself Him whom He begets. From the human mind proceeds a certain appetite, by which it seeks itself so that it may bring forth its knowledge; which appetite or inquiry, when it reaches the perfect discovery of knowledge, becomes love, which [joins] the mind and its know-
